## Preface

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

Dear Friends,

We keep thinking about our brain through the brain. Our Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning Industry innovators replicate the same brain functions in the innovative design of their logic and algorithms. Accordingly, we can explain everything about Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning with the help of simple day-to-day examples.

Most of us are interested in buying a visa for the future AI-driven earth. Few of us feel the algorithms and logic are complex to read if the phrase explains the theory. This book is changing the knowledge pattern. Most of the time, we plan to cover examples first before theory. Sometimes, we must keep the fundamental theory at the top. Next, however, we will cover the extensive examples at the bottom.

Besides, we include all the predominant topics. We will cover all advanced deep-learning concepts in the three volumes. In this first volume, we address three crucial vital subjects.

1.  Artificial Intelligence
2.  Statistical Methods
3.  Machine Learning

We cover adequate knowledge of statistical methods. It covers the various lines of business. If someone is interested in statistics, our chapters are sufficient to start. Nevertheless, the intention of covering the statistical method is different, and these statistical methods should cover machine learning concepts.

We will publish volume II and volume III of this series soon. If anyone of you is interested in personal coaching (including group and institution level) on any of these subjects and advanced AI &ML topics, please approach me through my mobile number, +91 9845689152, and my email ID manip2023@yahoo.com.

All the very best!

Manikandan Paneerselvam

01

## Introduction to Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

Machine Learning is the proper subset of Artificial Intelligence. It is what we all know. Here logic and algorithms are innovative in both fields. Therefore, instead of exploring both subjects in different textbooks, we plan to cover both Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in one book. It will eventually serve readers to correlate both fields together. Moreover, the fundamentals of both these subjects are from Mathematics and Statistical methods. Therefore, we are adequately covering the required statistics depth in this volume.

### 1.1 The Level and Depth of the Subjects in This Book

We are sufficiently covered all the required algorithms and subjects in this book. This book intends to cover all types of readers. The principal objective of this book is to cover all critical topics from three different subjects.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Artificial Intelligence.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Required Statistical Methods for Machine Learning and Artificial Intelligence.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Machine Learning.

## Artificial Intelligence: Introduction

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

When we instruct any command, the executor should execute the command. Whatever the rules we describe, the executor should follow the set of rules— We usually see these patterns in our computer world when dealing with them. Even a calculator also does the same thing. The pre-defined set of procedures will get performed when we ask for help from the calculator and computers.

For example, if we need to know the answer to “4+5”, our brain is already programmed to answer this. Our mathematics teacher inserted this program or procedure into our brains while studying in kinder garden. Similarly, we teach calculators and computers via programs. The calculator performs the addition well based on the program or the pre-defined procedure.

So, whatever mathematics teachers programmed into our brains, we have translated into the calculator. In the larger picture, any modern computation or calculation via computer and calculator executes a set of rules. Computers and calculators are just following the rules. It does not have thinking, and it does not make intellectually any decisions by its thinking.

Other than the programs in the human brain (Maths teacher’s program on addiction and other mathematics calculations), human being also thinks and reacts based on thinking—for example, decision-making.

Henceforth, we are going to travel with one young man Raja. Raja is 25 years old. When Raja drives the two-wheeler on the road, he gets one mobile call while driving. Raja’s friend Ravi called him and mentioned one particular accident on 36th Street. However, Raja should go via 36th Street for one crucial meeting. Raja is currently in the situation to decide whether he can go via 36th Street. He thinks a lot now.

If another friend Mohammad had called him, Raja would have agreed 100% without any doubt and could have decided to avoid that street. However, his friend Ravi called him and informed him—Most of the time, Ravi lies about many things. Ravi is doing these things just for fun, and he is funny. Now Raja gets confused because he got a call from Ravi. Raja is finally planning to react with a few options like

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)He must avoid 36th Street itself.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)He must go on the same route.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Call Mohammed or some other friend to confirm this 36th Street issue.
    

Whatever the thinking process Raja is going through now, we call this Intellectual thinking. If we plan to program this intellectual thinking to a computer, again, we go for a rule engine. The rule can say like this: If the called person’s name is Ravi, the program will provide three blocks of code as random options to get executed. The blocks of code will be like

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)avoid it,
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)use the same route,
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)or call Mohammed.
    

Here, the computer just executed the program like above with random choice. However, the actual human being, Raja, suddenly gets a few intuitions and makes decisions based on knowledge-based calculations. Something like probability is low on the 36th Street news because a few cars suddenly go in the same route. His brain will make these dynamic decisions. We can only program certain cases as rules-based logic. It is not possible to make computers think via a rule-based engine.

However, we need this kind of quick evidence-based decision-making intelligence in computers. We make computer thinks intellectually and reacts based on the requirements. This particular set of logic and programs is called Artificial Intelligence.

We call these programs intelligent program agents because agents perform actions based on their intelligent software design with some specific architectures. It perceives the environment via a few sensors, making decisions based on the agent design and performing the execution via actuators (Executors). It is straightforward. The robot is one part of an intelligent agent. It has a camera in his eyes to watch everything. It acts via legs and hands. Sometimes it displays the monitor as part of the action plan wherever required. These are one particular sample in the intelligent agent world.

### 2.1 Rationality

Within our human beings, the Intelligence quotient differs from person to person. The intelligence level or design is different for all humans. For example, instead of Raja, if Raja’s brother Ryan were in the same situation, Ryan would have taken a different decision. Thinking ability is different between people. When we push intelligence to computers, what level of intelligence do we have to design?

Moreover, what type of intelligence do we have to design? Artificial Intelligent researchers found that these levels must be rational. Rationality is the core.

Rational thinking makes decisions only via logic and reason. This decision should be 100% technical without any emotions. We can consider our example again if we must understand the difference between emotions and rationality. Raja is not confirming in his mind that Ravi is correct. Ravi already did these kinds of franks multiple times. Eight times he has done it already. However, somehow Raja needs clarification, and he keeps some perception about the situation in his brain without any evidence. These logics are purely called emotional perception. Raja makes decisions based on his emotional perception without any evidence. Most humans are not 100% rational because they will not make decisions based only on evidence, facts, and logic. Here emotional quotient plays a lot in human beings.

Therefore, making rational agents in an Artificial Intelligence system is the key. The AI system’s purpose gets completed when the agent reacts rationally. Also, an intelligent agent is not only thinking and providing solutions. It also works as humans (acts as humans in many cases. For example, robotics is part of artificial intelligence. It acts like a human being and has human hands and legs. This hardware intelligent agent must perform its actions rationally. So rational thinking and rational executions are essential for any intelligent design.

The overall top view of the Artificial Intelligence agents will get discussed in Chapter 8. However, till then (Chapters 3 to 7), we will discuss the inner logic of the agents.

One more topic we need to cover here in this section. Artificial intelligence also takes ideas and help from existing traditional fields (Fields of engineering, medicine, Economics, and science). What are those?

Overall Artificial intelligence is playing a vital role in the contemporary era. Logic and algorithms are getting sequentially innovated in history. We need to see all the logic, design, and algorithms with practical examples. Read the next set of chapters in sequential order. We will be amazed and wonder about the logic and innovations of artificial intelligence. We will appreciate the logic.

## Artificial Intelligence: Search and Problem Solving

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 3.1 Problem-Solving Introduction

The predominant work for any intelligent system is to solve problems. Imagine our human brain. In our day-to-day life, we all have some path in our actions, and we face huge issues in our path. We deal with these issues with the help of our brains.

Our young man Raja resides in one of the major cities in India and studying second year Bachelor of Science. His path is to complete the course within the third-year limit (Three-year studies). All of a sudden, he gets one problem in his studies. He did not pass three subjects in the 3rd\-semester exams (Second Year). He must solve this problem on time. He keeps thinking about that. How to solve this issue? His mind is given ten options to him. Those are like “Going to tuition,” “Avoid watching movies on television,” “Avoid going to movie theatres,” and “Avoid talking more with his girlfriend.”. Moreover, he is an intelligent young man who can solve this problem.

Like Raja, in our daily life, we all get problems in the path we travel; we get problems in reaching goals. How are we solving these issues by ourselves? We all solve all our issues with the help of our brains. Initially, we plan the solution and implement the solution. Isn’t it?

Similar to this. Our design of intelligent agents must involve solving problems via plan and execution. The agent must solve any problems rationally. To solve any given problem, what inputs are required to process, and what plans are required?

There are a few steps to handle these problems. We are going to discuss these steps one by one.

### 3.2 Find the Goal and Formulate the Goal

The goal is the key to any problem. In our example, Raja must finish his backlog within this year. So whatever Raja is facing now comes under a long-term or mid-term goal category.

However, we also deal with short-term goals in life. Henceforth, we will discuss one example from the short-term goal category. Season-based city exhibition is happening in the centre of Bangalore, India. Suddenly, the Police department gets a call, and someone says there is a bomb in the exhibition hall. If someone touches that bomb’s top button, it will get triggered, and the entire exhibition hall will get destroyed. One terrorist placed this bomb in the exhibition hall. When the exhibition security team caught him suspiciously, he died of a heart attack. So, no one knows about the location of the bomb. The security team found that there was one more terrorist, who was inside the exhibition hall and he was searching for the bomb to trigger it. From the public side, no one is inside the exhibition hall now. Someone needs to go inside and find both the bomb and the terrorist. The total land size is 5 acres, and Twenty-five activity centers are in this exhibition hall.

Also, finding the shortest path in-between the activity centers is challenging within the target time. Anytime the active terrorist may trigger the bomb.

![](Images/Image169146.png)

Figure 3.1 Example – Bangalore Exhibition Hall’s map

Refer the Figure 3.1 above for the Exhibition Hall information. Seeing this map, we can get a few inferences to solve the shortest path. However, we need to strategize our solution effectively with the help of this map. The exhibition hall has many activation centers. Now the questions are like where to start searching for the bomb and where to end. The strategy is required. One robotic intelligent agent has been hired to solve this issue.

So, initially, finding the goal is significant. The goal can be anything. For example, suppose we want to solve a bomb-threatening case ahead of terrorists. Our intelligent robotic agent should identify a few suspect areas in that case. The bomb could be anywhere in these activation centers. It could be in a movie theatre, pizza shop, cafeteria, or Indoor playing area. First, however, we need to strategy with some specific goal. Identifying the goal in any problem is called goal formulation. It is a goal setting, and this is the first step.

### 3.3 Design the Problem and Problem Formulation

The next set of activities goes with formulating problems. How are we going to solve the problem? First, we need to identify the simplest way to solve the problem. Otherwise, we need to identify the possible ways to solve the problem. This methodology is essential while strategizing a solution to the problem.

Considering our exhibition hall example, the agent must strategize to reach the goal quickly. The strategy, which is helping to reach the goal, comes under problem formulation. There will be many solutions for any given problem. For example, suppose the robotic agent needs to reach bookstall from a mobile shop in the exhibition hall; the agent has three options. One is towards the dance floor, another towards the restroom, and the last option is via the security gate.

Among these three options, the agent can choose any of the routes. First, however, it needs to find the optimal decision as a rational agent. Furthermore, the agent is new to this geography and has yet to learn about this place (Considering that no one has given training about this place). In real life, we are also blind to our problems and face many problems for the first time.

Furthermore, we have two different possible situations for our intelligent agent. If someone gives the above map, it is easy for our intelligent agent to derive a few informed decisions. On the other hand, suppose no one has provided the map to our intelligent agent; it refers to an uninformed situation. Whatever the case is, the agent will plan and formulate the problem logically. This plan is called problem formulation.

### 3.4 Search and Executions

In the artificial intelligence world, looking for the sequence of actions to reach the goal is called an execution plan. Moreover, this planning process is called search. For example, to find the route in-between the exhibition office to bookstall, the solution comes with some sequences of actions. Furthermore, it guarantees the shortest optimal path. These operation takes problems as inputs and returns a solution in any action sequence. Once we find this solution, we will know how to execute these actions in real time.

In our example, the intelligent robotic agent will enter the exhibition hall, only when the agent has a good plan of action and solution. Once it formulates the goal, it formulates the problem, gets the search plan, and finally goes into the hall for execution. Whatever the problems and plans are, the last step will be the execution, which will follow the strategized plan. Therefore, problem formulation is essential.

So, our algorithm for problem-solving is straightforward. It just formulates the goal and the problem. Then search for the solution within the problem formulation. Eventually, the execution will take place. Most of the time, based on the outcome, the agent will update the learning details in the knowledge base.

### 3.5 Steps Involved in Problem-Solving Agent Algorithm

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Goal formulation
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Problem formulation
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Search sequence
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Return the action plan and update the knowledge base
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ask actuators(executors) to perform
    

### 3.6 Some Crucial Components of the Problem

Initial State: We need to have an initial state for any problem. It will describe the initial location of the problem. For example, the intelligent agent in a mobile shop must be mentioned like this.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)In (Mobile Shop)

Actions: The second one is possible actions. In our example, our intelligent agent will finalize all the possible actions. It is the description of the possible actions available to the agent. Given the particular state at any time, the agent needs to find all possible actions which execute the actions from that state. All these actions are available to any state. For example, when the intelligent agent is in a mobile shop (In (Mobile Shop)), the corresponding actions will be

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg){Possible to Go {(Dance Floor)}, Possible to Go {(Rest Rooms)}, Possible to Go {(Security Gate)}}

Transition Model: The result will be there for all transitions. The transition model describes the action, including the specific results. The function RESULT (s, a) describes the results of the action. In our example, the intelligent agent is planning to go security gate. So, what are the results that the agent is going to get? The result is just reaching the security gate. At this moment, these models will be as simple as below.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Result (In (Mobile store), Going to (Security gate)) = In (Security Gate)

State Space: There will be a state space. State space represents all the initial state, actions, and transition model combinations. We can draw this complete state space in a graph. For example, if we see Figure 3.1 above, we can visualize all the intermediate points between the exhibition hall’s locations. It is because it uses the Graph data structure.

Path: A path is the sequence of states connected by the actions within the state space.

Goal Test: Every time, the intelligent agent needs to check whether it has reached the goal. Sometimes, many sets of achievable goals will be there for the problem. The goal test aims to test whether the agent has reached at least one goal among all these possible goals. Sometimes, the single goal will be there. In our example, the agent’s goals have two elements in the set {To find the bomb and to find the terrorists}. If the agent finds one of the goals, the game ends. Sometimes, we may have a sub-plan with a single goal. In our example, the agent’s sub-goal might be to reach the bookstall. So, we can denote this as a Goal test:

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)In {Book Stall}.

Path cost: The agent will assign numerical costs to all the paths. Generally, a problem-solving agent must choose the cost function, which should reflect its performance measure. In our exhibition hall example, we must solve the issue within a specific time. Terrorists should not catch the bomb before the agent reaches it. So, path cost is essential. That too, optimal path cost is important. Finding the shortest path in the plan is essential. For this path, a cost analysis will help. If we see our example, the distance is in meters. So, we calculate the shortest distance from one location to another. In that case, we can calculate it with the help of distance in meters.

Step cost: Under path cost, we have step cost too. In-between two nodes, whatever the distance we have mentioned in Figure 3.1 is called step cost. In our example, the agent must find the distance (The distance in meters) between two activation centers.

Optimal Solution: For any agent problem-solving activity, finding the optimal solution is the key. We can solve any problem with any plan. Nevertheless, finding the optimal plan matters, and this optimal solution will help the agent to reduce time, storage, and processing energy.

Even our intelligent agents, irrespective of whether the agent is a software or hardware agent, problem-solving is the key. Initially, the agents will plan the solution path with the help of problem-solving components like initial state, actions, transition model, goal test, and path test.

In this section, we will see other examples of this. Suppose we go back to Raja’s case instead of our exhibition hall example. Raja needs to plan the solution before he executes it. His problem is to finish the Bachelor of Science course within the allocated years. Now we will talk about all the problem-solving components of this problem.

He checks the goal in every model exam from the set of model exams. Once he is successful in the model exam, he reaches the goal.

Like Raja’s example and Exhibition Hall’s example cases, all other problems in the world need effective optimal planning. Say, for example, if the agent is playing a static game like Maze, the agent will plan the game before start playing the game. Irrespective of whether we are human beings or agents, we need a clear plan to play the Maze. The initial state is the starting point in the Maze. After that, the goal test will execute at every step. See below Figure 3.2 for the Maze game, which has initial and goal states.

### 3.8 Tree and Graph

Usually, in artificial intelligence, we try to keep the problem stats into two different data structures. One is a tree, and the other is a graph. For example, all the activity areas are connected well in the exhibition hall case. It naturally forms the graph data structure. In our example, links between activation centers are visible in Figure 3.1.

Sometimes, the tree data structure will get formed in the solution space. For example, tree data structures usually have a parent with succeeding children as leaves. Then, all the leaves will have sub-leaves as children. It keeps going in that fashion. Refer the below Figure 3.3 for details.

![](Images/Image169163.png)

Figure 3.3 Tree data structure

Here the search strategies are different in tree and graph data structures.

If the tree starts from the parent (Initial state), the next move has multiple options. It can move into any child in the tree. Once it decides on the child, the sub-child decision will perform.

In Artificial Intelligence, intelligent agent problem-solving has many search algorithms. These algorithms decide the path and movements in the tree or graph data structure. These algorithms have a few characteristics like “How soon we reach?”, “what was our previous step?”. Ample memory storage needs to be used to maintain all these data simultaneously.

For trees, we need to use frontier to keep track of all available leaf nodes, which we can expand. We also call this an open list. Expanding nodes on the frontier continues until either a solution is available or there are no more states to expand. Search algorithms keep all these open list details in memory. Then, the agent will try to expand all the nodes inside the frontier.

So, we have seen the importance of the frontier in the previous paragraph. Here the logic for expansion will be complex in graphs. Usually, we move from one node to another in a tree. We will not have the parent node as an option when it expands because it is not bidirectional. Nevertheless, the graph is bidirectional, and it gets the parent node as one option again to expand. Sometimes these movements will be in a loop. We need to end up in the first node again from the second node in the graph. It is called a loopy path. For example, if the agent moves from the mobile shop to restrooms, again, it will have one child option as a mobile shop.

If the intelligent agent goes from the mobile shop to the restroom, and the restroom to the mobile shop, and again the mobile shop to rest room, this situation is called a loopy path.

So, we need to maintain few additional information in the graph data structure. It is called explored set. Whatever we have explored so far, those nodes will be in the explored list. The agent does not need to revisit the location one more time. This logic will get handled in graph data structure algorithms.

When we have problems, we must understand whether our solution set or nodes are visible via a tree or graph. Based on this, we need to make decisions. Also, in tree and graph nodes, we need to understand the details of the parent node, child node, leaf node, frontier, and an empty list. Search strategy and optimal paths need a good design.

Note that we have nodes, and we need somewhere to put them. The frontier must be stored. So, the search algorithm can easily choose the next node to expand according to its preferred strategy. The appropriate data structure for this is a queue. The operations in the queue are POP (Which removes the first element of the queue), INSERT (Which inserts an element and returns the resulting queue), and CHECK EMPTYNESS (to check whether none of the nodes are available in the queue).

The queue variants are available by the order in which they store the inserted nodes. The common variants are the FIFO (First in, first out queue), LIFO (Last in, First Out), and the priority queue.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)FIFO – which pops the oldest element of the queue.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)LIFO – which pops the newest element of the queue.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Priority Queue – These pops the ordered queue elements with the help of the highest priority calculation.
    

We must choose the best queue according to the problem statement and data structure decisions.

### 3.9 Problem-Solving Performance

The performance is crucial for search algorithms because of cost limitations. The details of the algorithm’s performance are available below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)One is completeness. We need to find whether the solution of the algorithm guarantees the completeness. When we design the algorithm, we need to analyse the worst case. We need to analyse the worst-case behaviour of the algorithm, like how this algorithm will perform during the worst-case situations. For any worst-case data, the algorithm should complete finding the solution.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If we analyse this in our exhibition hall example, the intelligent agent must use some algorithms with completeness as a priority. The worst-case situation will be that the agent needs to search for the bomb till the exit gate. If some algorithms are not helping the agents to find a solution when there is a solution is called incompleteness.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The second one is Time complexity. If we are trying to find the solution in a million node topologies, imagine how long the search and solution finding takes. How long does it take to find the solution?
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The third one is space complexity. How much memory space the agent needs to perform the actions with the help of this algorithm? It is essential because most algorithms need more than 1 Terra Byte storage. Moreover, the type of the problem decides this. For example, memory usage must restrict if we use millions of nodes in our searching and solution space.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The fourth one is optimality. We need to find whether the algorithm finds the optimal solution or not. Some algorithms are capable of finding optimality, and some algorithms are not capable of finding optimality.
    

Time and space complexity are also getting calculated with the help of the below quantities:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Branching factor – It is getting denoted as b. In the tree or graph, we can find the maximum number of successors of any node. Say in two-leaf trees, everywhere, the maximum leaf count will be 2.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Depth – It is getting denoted as d. It is referring the shallowest goal node. Dead end in trees.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Length – It is getting denoted as m. It is referring the maximum length of any path in the state space.
    

### 3.10 Searching

Problem-solving agent’s fourth phase is searching. Once the input is ready, our brain or intelligent agent formulates the goal and the problem. Then, we search for the solution within the problem set. We are going to group the search algorithms into two categories.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Uninformed search algorithms
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Informed search algorithms
    

Moreover, we are going to discuss these group algorithms one by one.

### 3.11 Uninformed Search Strategies

The uninformed search category refers to the situation in which no specific data is available to the agent. It is a blind search within the problem set. Additional data will not provide to the agent. For example, we do not provide any other data about the exhibition hall to our intelligent agent. The agent explores the step cost when entering the specific activation centers. If someone provides the exhibition hall map to the agent, this comes under the informed search category. The agent can plan the strategy smartly with the help of a route map. It can take some detailed data from the map. We can see that detailed data while discussing informed search strategies (This particular data is getting called heuristics). With the help of this remarkable data, the planning will be easy.

However, in the uninformed group, imagine we will not provide a map to an intelligent agent. We can explore the child nodes by going to the parent node. In our example, when the agent is in the exhibition office, the agent can look into the information about sub-nodes like a security gate and dance floor. Now the agent knows the distance between these sub-nodes (activation centers), like 471 meters and 551 meters; with these two data’s help, the intelligent agent needs to choose one of the child nodes for exploration. (Either the security gate or the dance floor). These kinds of algorithms are called blind search algorithms or uninformed search algorithms.

Many algorithms fall under this category. Those algorithms are

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Breadth-first search
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Depth-first search
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Uniform cost search
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Depth limited search
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Iterative deepening search
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Bidirectional search
    

Here, we will see all these search strategies one by one.

### 3.12 Breadth-First Search

In this search, initially, the root node will be explored. Then all children will be expanded next. After that, it will move into the next depth if and only if they cover all their child nodes. Once it finishes the second level, it will explore the third level and the super child of all the child nodes one by one from left to right.

![](Images/Image169170.png)

![](Images/Image169178.png)

![](Images/Image169187.png)

![](Images/Image169194.png)

![](Images/Image169201.png)

![](Images/Image169208.png)

Figure 3.4 Breadth-First search

Referring the Figure 3.4 above, we can understand the node movements. In our exhibition hall example, if our intelligent agent chooses a breadth-first search algorithm, it follows the below path. It first explores the exhibition office and then moves into the security gate. Post to the search at the security gate, it goes into a dance floor. Then it returns to the security gate and explores security gate’s next set of children.

Its uses FIFO Queue for frontier expansion of graph data structure.

Performance of breadth-first search algorithm:

Completeness: This algorithm is complete if we have finite depth d for the dead-end node (Shallowest node). It can ultimately find the solution even for worst-case searches. If the depth is infinite, this algorithm is not complete.

Optimality: This algorithm is optimal if all actions have the exact cost. If the path varies (Or in a non-decreasing fashion), this algorithm is not optimal. We must spend our energy wisely with high path cost nodes before reaching our goals.

Time complexity: The algorithm goes into all nodes of all depths. So that the time complexity will be O(bd), it is breadth power depth. When the depth count increases to 1, the power will increase as the nodes double from the previous depth.

Space complexity: Too costly because it keeps all the parent nodes in the frontier for future child node reference. So, O(bd) complexity will get maintained here. It is too bad. We need ten terabytes of memory usage even for a depth limit of 10.

### 3.13 Depth-First Search

In this search, initially, the root node will be explored. Then the first child on the left will be explored next. Then before moving into the child of the root node, the currently explored child’s child will be explored next. So, the deepest node in the frontier will explore first. Then, once it reaches the deep end (shallowest end), it explores the adjustment child node of the final dead-end row. So, the search “backs up” to the next deepest node with unexplored successors.

The primary purpose of the depth-first search is to reduce memory usage. Therefore, we kept many parent node details in the frontier of the breadth-first search. Here we can move the explored set quickly. So, we use the LIFO stack approach instead of the FIFO queue.

![](Images/Image169221.png)

![](Images/Image169230.png)

![](Images/Image169238.png)

![](Images/Image169247.png)

![](Images/Image169254.png)

![](Images/Image169261.png)

Figure 3.5 Depth-first search

We can understand the node movements by referring to Figure 3.5 above. In our example, if our intelligent agent chooses this depth-first search algorithm, it first explores the exhibition office and then moves into the security gate. Once it has finished searching at the security gate, it goes into the security gate neighbour’s mobile shop. Finally, it explores the mobile shop’s next successor and continues.

Its uses LIFO Queue (Stack) for frontier expansion in our graph data structure example.

Performance of depth-first search algorithm:

Completeness: It never completes in the tree. It completes the graph if the limit is finite because it eventually expands all the nodes in the graph. Also, it uses explored set. So quickly, we can complete if the limit is finite in the graph. It generates an infinite loop in the tree. So, it cannot compete in the worst cases.

Optimality: For both graph and tree, this algorithm is not optimal. We may be wasting in-depth exploration if we have the goal node in the nearest location.

Time complexity: This algorithm goes into all nodes in the first breadth and explores the second breadth. So, the time complexity will be O(bm). So, it is breadth power maximum length.

Space complexity: The cost of the memory is less in this algorithm because it removes the nodes quickly when the exportation completes. The space complexity will be O(bm) in this depth-first search algorithm.

### 3.14 Uniform Cost Search

A uniform cost search is trying to find the optimal path with the help of the lowest path cost within the solution. Usually, the breadth-first search explores to goal node from the start node by having empty details (Blind details). Then, it just checks the goal with the help of the goal test. The goal test will execute at every node in the breadth-first search travel.

To avoid blindness in the breadth-first search algorithm, we need to explore the nodes with the help of the step costs. Hence, it is called a uniform cost search. It tries to find the optimal cost by exploring the cheapest step cost nodes.

![](Images/Image169269.png)

Figure 3.6 Sub-goals in Exhibition Hall

Also, it just keeps history in mind and revisits the decisions through backward travel. It revisits the explored nodes if it finds the optimal cost from some of the explored nodes compared to the current node’s path. This comparison will keep happening even in the middle of the analysis. One more exciting feature of uniform cost search is that it will keep analysing the optimality of the nodes even after it finds the goal node.

The below example will clarify this. See Figure 3.6 above for one particular case of our exhibition hall example. The blue rectangle in the Figure shows this. Say this sub-solution needs to find the optimal path in-between the dancefloor and the bookstall.

The uniform cost search algorithm starts from the dancefloor. When it is on the dancefloor, the children’s distance details will be available to our agent. One path will take the agent to a cafeteria within 499 meters, and the other will take the agent to a pizza shop within 480 meters. For the agent, the cost of children will be available when the agent visits the parent node. Now the agent at the parent node will decide to go to the optimal child. Now our agent is following the uniform cost search algorithm in the exhibition hall, and initially, it decides to go pizza shop per plan because the cost is 480 meters. Once it moves to the pizza shop, it will have two more options to explore now. One is the dance floor, which was the origin \[From where we started the route\], and the other is the stationary shop, which is 497 meters from the pizza shop. It has only one option now. Before moving into the stationary shop, a uniform cost search will calculate the overall cost from the origin to the stationary shop. That means the total cost will be 480 + 497 = 977 meters. Now uniform cost search algorithm compares the distance between the cafeteria and the stationery shop and chooses the best one.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)To go cafeteria from the dancefloor is just 499 meters.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)To go stationery shop from the dancefloor it comes around 977 meters.
    

From the optimal cost point of view, it chooses the cafeteria. It goes in plan, and it chooses the cafeteria now. From the cafeteria, we have only one option to move. That is bookstall. It is the goal node. UCS (Uniform cost search) performs the goal test and confirms the goal. However, it will not end the algorithm here. It will calculate the cost of the current node. The cost is

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Going bookstall from the dancefloor takes 499 + 611 = 1110 meters.

When compared to a stationery shop, this is costly. So, it revisits the stationery shop node one more time. Moreover, it explores bookstall from stationery shops. Now it is reaching bookstall again. Now it will compare the below two paths.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)To go, bookstall from the dance floor, which is just 499 + 611 = 1110 meters. \[Via cafeteria\]
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)To go bookstall from the dance floor, which is just 480 + 497 + 501 = 1478 meters. \[Via pizza shop and stationery shop\]
    

The optimal choice is still the cafeteria route. So uniform cost search conforms to this path. So, it declares the optimal solution.

It is easy to see that those uniform costs are optimal in general. Imagine it finds the optimal path to the node. However, it is still revisiting. We have one issue because of this feature. Sometimes it will get struct with an infinite loop. It does not care about the number of steps on the path.

Performance of uniform cost search algorithm:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Completeness:
    
    ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Uniform-cost search is complete, such as if there is a solution, UCS will find it.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Time Complexity:
    
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Let C\* is the optimal solution cost, and ε is each step to get closer to the goal node. Then the number of steps is = C\*/ε+1. So here we have taken +1, starting from state 0 and ending with C\*/ε.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Hence, the worst-case time complexity of the uniform-cost search is O (b1 + \[C\*/ε\]).
        
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Space Complexity:
    
    ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)The same logic is for space complexity, so the worst-case space complexity of Uniform-cost search is O (b1 + \[C\*/ε\]).
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Optimal:
    
    ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Uniform-cost search is always optimal as it only selects a path with the lowest path cost.
    

### 3.15 Depth-Limited Search

The problem with depth-first search is the infinite depth or enormous depth. So, one more option is to limit the depth. For example, we can limit the depth limit as “l” in the algorithm even though we have an infinite depth or the costliest depth. So, this is called depth-limited search.

Performance of depth-limited search algorithm:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The depth-limited search algorithm must also be complete if we choose the limit before our goal node’s depth. It never finds the goal here. The DLS search algorithm is complete if the solution exceeds the depth limit.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Depth-limited search also is not optimal when we choose our limit “l,” which is bigger than the actual depth. (l>d)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Time Complexity: The time complexity of the DLS algorithm is O (bl), where b is the branching factor (number of children at each node) and l is the limited depth.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The space complexity of the DLS algorithm is O(bl), where b is known as the branching factor (number of children at each node) and l is the given depth limit.
    

### 3.16 Iterative Deepening Depth-First Search (IDDFS)

The depth-limited search also has some drawbacks, like we cannot find the goal if our goal is behind the limit. To solve this, we can explore one more idea. We can perform the depth-first search in a repeated way by increasing the limits from 1 to N. It means that the agent first performs the depth-limited search with a limit of 1. Once this assessment is complete, we can repeat the depth-limited search with a limit of 2. Then, we can reiterate the depth-limited search with a limit of 3. It will keep continuing until we find the goal.

Performance of iterative deepening depth-first search algorithm:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Space complexity is O(bd), and time complexity is O(d). However, ‘d’ should be finite here.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IDDFS gives us the hope to find the solution if it exists in the  
    tree.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The algorithm proves efficient and timely when the solutions are available at the lower depths, say n.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The great advantage of IDDFS is found in in-game tree searching, where the IDDFS search operation tries to improve the depth definition, heuristics, and scores of searching nodes to enable efficiency in the search algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Another significant advantage of the IDDFS algorithm is its quick responsiveness. The early results indications are a plus point in this algorithm. However, it will follow multiple refinements after the individual iteration completes.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Though the work done here is more yet, the performance of IDDFS is better than single BFS and DFS operating exclusively.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The time taken is exponential to reach the goal node.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The main problem with IDDFS is the time and wasted calculations at each depth.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The situation is better than we think, primarily when the branching factor is high.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The IDDFS might fail when the BFS fails. When we are to find multiple answers from the IDDFS, it gives back the success nodes and their path once, even if it needs to be found again after multiple iterations. The depth bound is not increased further and gets stopped.
    

### 3.17 Bidirectional Search

Finally, we will see Bidirectional Search in the Uninformed search algorithm category. Instead of searching the goal from the start node, why can’t we search it from the goal node toward the start node? It will help us to find the path quickly.

The bidirectional search algorithm triggers two threads in parallel. One thread starts from the root node and the other from the goal node. Therefore, we get the solutions quickly when we execute these two parallel simultaneous searches.

Performance of bidirectional search algorithm:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Completeness − Bidirectional search is complete if the breadth-first search uses both searches. In addition to this, the limit should be finite.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Optimality − It is optimal if the breadth-first search uses for search and paths have uniform costs. In addition to this, the limit should be finite.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Time and Space Complexity − Time and space complexity is O(b{d/2}) because 50% effort got reduced here.

### 3.19 Informed Search Algorithms

As mentioned earlier, will that make sense if we provide unique algorithms to the intelligent agent? Yes. Unique information will help the agent to decide on heuristic information. In our example, if we provide the exhibition hall map, it will be helpful for an agent.

Suppose we provide the map to an intelligent agent. In that case, the agent can get one heuristic function as a straight-line distance between activation centers. We usually call this heuristic function SLD (Straight line distance). Invariably, the shortest path between two nodes is a straight line. This information will be better than the step costs that we already have. On the other hand, our usual distance is not a straight line, which uses as step costs.

For example, from the map, our agent identified the below SLD’s in between our activation centers.

Table 3.2 provides the SLD heuristics (Straight line distance) of the bookstall from other activation centers of the Exhibition Hall.

![55](Images/55.jpg)

Table 3.2 Exhibition Hall SLD (Straight Line Distance) Heuristics to the bookstall

The general approach we follow with informed search strategies is called best-first search. The best first search will work for both tree and graph data structures. This algorithm will select the node for expansion using the evaluation function f(n). Any detailed information will fall under the heuristics category. This f(n) depends only on the state of that node.

F (n) = Estimated cost of the cheapest path from the state of node n to a goal node.

### 3.20 Greedy Best-First Search

The name itself tells that we can directly pick the nodes with an optimal heuristic cost (or heuristic information). If we say that the search function is f(n), we select f(n) for this section. We depend on the heuristic value, like straight line distance.

f(n) = h(n)

We will see the behaviour of these algorithms from our example. Imagine our intelligent agent is using a straight-line distance heuristic, which we call HSLD. If the goal is to reach the bookstall, we need to know all the SLDs, which we have mentioned in Table 3.2 above.

Based on the SLDs, we can choose the optimal value h(n) and move to the optimal node. Then further expansion will use the same logic again as previous expansions. We are moving here based on the SLD heuristics. The best-first search algorithm functions with the help of h(n), which is why we call this algorithm a Greedy best-first search algorithm.

In our example, a greedy best-first search will work like the one below.

If we aim to find the optimal path to “Bookstall” from the initial state “Mobile shop,” the Greedy best-first search algorithm will work like the one below.

Figure 3.7 Greedy best-first search algorithm

From the cafeteria, we go to the bookstall. It is how the Greedy best-first search algorithm works.

Performance of greedy best-first search algorithm:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Lack of Completeness: Greedy Best-First Search is a partial algorithm, meaning it may only sometimes find a solution if one exists. It can happen if the algorithm gets stuck in a cycle or the search space needs to be simplified.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Fast and Efficient: Greedy Best-First Search is a high-speed algorithm, making it ideal for applications where speed is  
    essential.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Low Memory Requirements: Greedy Best-First Search requires only a small amount of memory, making it suitable for applications with limited memory.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Flexible: Greedy Best-First Search can be adapted to different types of problems and easily extended to more complex  
    problems.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Efficiency: If the heuristic function used in Greedy Best-First Search is good for estimating how close a node is to the solution, this algorithm can be very efficient and find a solution quickly, even in large search spaces.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Heuristic Function: Greedy Best-First Search requires a heuristic function to work, which adds complexity to the algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Sometimes it could be more optimal because sometimes, it may fall into infinite loops.
    

### 3.21 A\* Search Algorithm

Instead of seeing only the heuristic cost (The particular function cost), If we add cumulative step cost (Existing travel cost from the start node) in our analysis, that is called the A\* algorithm.

Say, for example. If the agent adds the cumulative step cost g(n) with heuristic cost, we get the super optimal cost. In formula,

f(n) = g(n) + h(n)

The idea is simple. We are not only greedy and use only heuristic information. We also add the past step costs to decide on the expansion. Step cost means it is not only the recently expanded step cost. It is the full path cost from the start node to the current node.

Uniform cost search uses g(n) for expansion. The greedy best-first search algorithm uses h(n) for expansion. This algorithm uses both options together.

In our example, A\* search will work like the one below.

If we aim to find the optimal path to “bookstall” from the initial state “mobile shop,” the Greedy best-first search algorithm will work like the one below.

When expanding dance floor, restrooms & security gate, we add their respective heuristic cost (h(n)) from bookstall SLD and the previous path cost (g(n))

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Dance Floor = h(n) + g(n) = 274 + 540 = 814
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Rest Rooms = h(n) + g(n) = 350 + 518 = 868
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Security Gate = h(n) + g(n) = 395 + 475 = 870
    

Then, the agent will choose the optimal cost of 814.

![](Images/Image169480.png)

When it is expanding the mobile shop, cafeteria, exhibition shop & pizza shop, we add their respective heuristic cost (h(n)) from bookstall SLD and the previous path cost (g(n))

Mobile shop = h(n) + g(n) = 387 + 540 +540 (We add two previous step costs as path cost along with heuristics cost)

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Cafeteria = h(n) + g(n) = 197 + 499 + 540
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Exhibition shop = h(n) + g(n) = 401+ 540 +551
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Pizza shop= h(n) + g(n) = 214 + 540 +480
    

![](Images/Image169489.png)

![](Images/Image169497.png)

Figure 3.8 A\* Search algorithm

We repeat this procedure and expand till we find the goal.

### 3.22 Admissibility and Consistency

Admissibility and consistency are conditions that can guarantee the optimality of solutions in heuristic search algorithms.

A heuristic function is admissible if it never overestimates the actual cost of reaching the goal node from any given node. In other words, the heuristic function always returns a value that is less than or equal to the actual cost of reaching the goal node. If a heuristic function is admissible, any algorithm that uses it to guide its search is guaranteed to find an optimal solution.

A heuristic function is consistent (or monotonic) if the estimated cost of reaching a successor node plus the cost of moving from the current node to that successor is always less than or equal to the estimated cost of reaching the current node. In other words, the heuristic function is consistent if the estimated cost of reaching a node is always less than or equal to the sum of the estimated cost of reaching one of its successors and moving from the current node to that successor. If a heuristic function is consistent, any algorithm that uses it to guide its search is guaranteed to find an optimal solution.

Consistency is more necessary than admissibility, as any consistent heuristic function is also admissible. However, some admissible heuristic functions need to be more consistent.

Admissibility and consistency are essential for optimality in heuristic search algorithms, such as A\* search. Suppose the heuristic function used in the algorithm is admissible and consistent. In that case, we guarantee this algorithm finds the optimal solution with the lowest possible cost. On the other hand, if the heuristic function is not admissible or consistent, the algorithm may not find the optimal solution or take longer.

In summary, admissibility and consistency guarantee the optimality of solutions in heuristic search algorithms. An admissible heuristic function never overestimates the actual cost of reaching the goal node. In contrast, a consistent heuristic function ensures that the estimated cost of reaching a node is always less than or equal to the sum of the estimated cost of reaching one of its successors and moving from the current node to that successor. If a heuristic function is admissible and consistent, any algorithm that uses it to guide its search is guaranteed to find an optimal solution.

### 3.23 Iterative Deepening A\* Algorithm (IDA\*)

A\* uses huge memory like uniform cost search. However, using the iterative deepening A\* Algorithm reduces memory usage.

Iterative deepening A\* (IDA\*) is a graph traversal and path-finding method that can determine the shortest route in a weighted graph between a defined start node and any group of goal nodes. It is an iterative deepening depth-first search that adopts the A\* search algorithm’s idea of using a heuristic function to assess the remaining cost to reach the goal.

A memory-limited version of A\* is called IDA\*. It performs all operations that A\* does and has optimal features for locating the shortest path, but it occupies low memory.

Iterative Deepening A Star uses a heuristic to choose which nodes to explore and at which depth to stop, as opposed to Iterative Deepening DFS, which utilizes simple depth to determine when to end the current iteration and continue with a higher depth.

Points to remember for IDA\*:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It uses the graph traversal algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Using the path search algorithm, find the shortest path between the start and goal nodes in a weighted graph.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It is an alternative to the iterative depth-first search algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It uses a heuristic function, a technique taken from A\* algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It is a Depth-First Search algorithm, So it uses less memory than the A\* algorithm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IDA\* is an admissible heuristic because it never overestimates the goal’s cost.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It focuses on searching the most promising nodes and goes to different depths everywhere.

## Artificial Intelligence: Local Search

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 4.1 Introduction to Local Search

So far, what we have seen in the previous chapter is problem-solving. The procedure to solve any given problem is called problem-solving. In our exhibition hall example, how an intelligent robotic agent is trying to find the bomb is the key. How it identifies the shortest route between two different activation centers within the given exhibition hall? We ask the agent’s help to find the optimal and rational solution for the path-finding activity. Is this enough for an intelligent agent or an intelligent guy?

Just think about our real-time intelligence. No one gives the entire world’s details to us in real life. So, when we need to solve any problem, only some of the maps or the details about the world are available to us.

Imagine someone giving all the details about when we are born. It is easy to live here if all information is available before attempting solutions. With the help of this special database, our brain works ahead in uninformed and informed cases. Everyone works smartly on plan and execution when everything is available.

Nevertheless, it is not happening in the actual case. In the real case, everything is unknown. We do not have any heuristics or any data. Nothing is available. We have to search for elements and solutions within our neighbourhood only. The exploration will be there while traveling. Initially, we have to go individually, and we need to explore that. Therefore, the intelligence needs to explore the solution from the neighbourhood options.

Usually, the sequence will have surprising and unimagined outcomes in real life. Immediately, our brain considers the best solution with the calculated risks when we face problems. A similar situation comes to intelligent agents too. In many situations, the intelligent agent must make decisions only from the neighbourhood options.

Whatever the concept we have seen so far is called local search. The local search is always searching for solutions from the solution space itself. First, however, we must converge the solution in the solution world.

What is the difference between the general search we have discussed in the previous chapters and the local search we are discussing here? We can discuss this with the help of a simple example. Imagine our intelligent robotic agent is supposed to report the boss in five-star resorts in Yelagiri hills, Tamilnadu. Furthermore, all of a sudden, this call has been made. The robot needs to explore the roads and hills. It does not know anything. No one gives the map to the Intelligent agent. Even oral information also is not available. The agent is on the downside of the hill. Now it is seeing one direction board, which says that the right-side road leads to Bangalore by 130 kilo meters and the left-side road leads to Chennai by 250 kilo meters. Also, one more arrow mark says that the road is going towards Yelagiri by 15-kilo meters. No one has given any details so far, and the robot decides to go straight. Later after moving on the same road, the two roads get divided without any information. Many cars are going on one route where the other route is empty. Now robot must choose the best option to move. Here no one is available to help the robot. The robot needs to finish the task soon (As per the task, it needs to go top of the hill in 2 hours, and the robot’s owner is waiting for the robot at the top of the hill). A local search algorithm is considered the best option from neighbourhood options without knowing the subsequent route.

The robot must finish the travel at the top of the hill to reach the goal. That is the goal of robotic agents. The owner said he could not give any information about paths and routes. However, the goal is to reach that 5-star resort. So, it fixes the goal. After that, goal tests will be happening for every move.

Until the robot reaches the resort or the top of the hill, the analysis, goal test, and subsequent possible moves will continue in the robot’s travel. We are going to see this local behavioural search in this chapter.

See below Figure 4.1 for the solution space world.

The above Figure 4.1 depicts the local search. In our real life also, it is happening. Within this solution space only, the goals are there. However, we are entirely blind. We are still determining where we are now and what we are doing. We have only the neighbourhood options. In this solution space, we check the goal at every step. Once we find the solution in the solution space, our mission ends. However, sometimes, we see some solutions that may differ from the global maximum solutions we suppose to achieve. For example, suppose the owner has yet to give the resort details, and the goal is to reach the top of the hill; the robot may have six hilltop cases. It has solved if the robot reaches any of these six hilltops. However, this best solution is the global maximum solution, and other possible solutions are local maximum solutions.

Considering our real-life case, imagine we plan to reach out as a researcher in artificial intelligence. First, however, we need to learn how to get there. Currently, we can see only a few neighbourhood options, and these options are in front of us. For example, some business person is asking us to prepare the machine learning algorithm they need as a recommender system. Likewise, we have only a few options as neighbours. However, we are still determining whether it will take us to the solution we expect. We are considering the maximum option from our immediate neighbourhood. Finally, our travel ends in small-scale company owners or college professors. These are the leading local solutions we have received from the solution space. There must be more than these to reach the global maximum—most of the time. Our life will end in a local maximum.

Similarly, our robot also got struct. Many people on the reached hilltop said this is one of the tops on the hill. The robot gets confused and does not know whether the reached place is the global maximum. The robot did not reach the goal but completed it because the boss had just mentioned that it would be on the hilltop.

We never jump from the local maximum to the global maximum because jumping is not an option. If we must reach the global maximum, we must go down again. Refer the Figure 4.1 for details about this.

Most of the time, we also see the flat surface while moving in the neighbours’ steps. There will not be any improvement in our life, and our life is flat. We are frustrated by the flat improvement, which may be our bottleneck to reaching our goals. Example: We did not receive any improvement in our salary for the last five years in the job where we are working. Sometimes, we feel that our life also will go negatively, which is equal to going down the hill. Something like that, we lose money in life and start earning. This situation is climbing back case. We can see all these options in Figure 4.1

We will see some unique algorithms as part of local search. For example, we will see a hill-climbing search, simulated annealing, local beam search, and genetic algorithms. These are the powerful algorithms in local search. We will see all these one by one in this section.

Apart from connecting with the real-life example, the local search must be viewed from another angle. That is named objective functions with state space.

For example, in some problems and solution spaces, we need to improve our current state. We can measure this improvement from the objective function’s point of view. We can refer to one more example here to discuss more about this. Refer to the below Figure 4.2 for this.

We can consider one example here. There is one small town in India where the election will happen for the leader position in another ten days. We have only 16 vote-gathering employees available for one candidate to win the election. He has to gather the maximum votes from the available 250 streets. The limitation is just ten days. How to gather votes is challenging, and we need some brilliant ideas for this algorithm. He has hired our intelligent robotic agent for help.

Now the robotic agent is available in that town, planning to capture the maximum votes. The objective is to collect the maximum possible votes within ten days. We can correlate this with Figure 4.2. In that town, only 200,000 votes are there. Here the maximum of the objective function is to get 100,000 positive votes. Suppose all 16 votes gathering employees achieved collecting 100,000 votes; that is the global maximum. Here within ten days, if it is impossible to convince 100,000 votes and if it has reached 50,000 votes, this may be the local peak. That is the local maximum one. Nevertheless, there is a 100% probability of winning the election when we get 100,000 votes and only a 50% probability is there to win the election with 50,000 votes.

As per the algorithm, the intelligent robotic agent needs to design the best possible algorithm to achieve the goal with the help of 16 vote-gathering employees. How is it going to design the algorithm for this? Our robot is going to follow the local search algorithms. As per that, it asked all the 16 vote-gathering employees to go randomly to unique streets and start gathering the vote from any random house. However, after finishing vote gathering process, the employees ask the house members about the next possible house in the street where the employees get the maximum votes. After capturing this information, the individual employees will have more optimal options. These are neighbourhood options. Now the employee selects the maximum possible options by choosing many other factors like whether the house ideology belongs to the boss candidate, the exact thought process within all the family members, and whether it is easy to convince them.

So here, the objective function means vote count improvement. Once the vote-capturing process finishes, the employee will report it to the robot. The robot will add the vote count to the global count. So, it will increase the objective function. So constantly, the objective function will improve because the previous steps capture some votes.

There are options here like there will be a slowdown in the vote-capturing improvement from all the employees, and there will be flow movements where none of them reported the votes. (Flat surface in objective function). There may be degradation like people are rethinking the votes. Sometimes, if our local search algorithm is not an effective one, we may reach the maximum local hill and will get struct over there.

From an algorithm’s point of view in local search, artificial intelligence innovators have invented many algorithms like hill-climbing search, simulated annealing, local beach search, and genetic algorithms. We will see these algorithms one by one in this section.

### 4.2 Hill Climbing Local Search Algorithm

What is a hill Climbing local search?

As per Figure 4.2, if the algorithm makes the optimal decision from the neighbourhood options without any ahead knowledge, it is called local search. What is the outcome of these? The best amount the neighbours will get selected. From our example, the maximum number of possible votes is considered the optimal section of the house. This information the employee is getting from the current house. The current home representative may say that there are many characteristics in the street which are like the potential interests of the members for our candidate in the election. The employee selects the maximum values house from the maximum utility values for the next step. It will eventually increase the voting count. The total voting count increases.

Then the same activity gets repeated. The employee again captures the next set of possibilities from the next house and chooses the best one for the subsequent house selection. Optimal neighbour selection and execution move the state into hill climbing. The objective function will also grow toward the top of the hills with the blind objective. We aim to reach somewhere. In case we reach the maximum objective function, we are good. As mentioned earlier, the robot gathers data from all ten vote-gathering humans and accumulates the results.

This hill-climbing algorithm is also sometimes called greedy local search because this algorithm is getting executed without more thinking. It is just finding the optimal neighbour and just moving. So, it is a greedy one.

This hill climbing local search algorithm is getting struct in many situations. We can see these issues below.

Local maxima: The election is going to happen in 10 days. However, we have captured only 50,000 votes with the help of all 16 vote-gathering humans.

Local flat surface: Sometimes, after capturing the votes, no other votes are available in the next set of trials. With the help of all 16 employees, we could not achieve this. The possible reason might be anything else, like the targeted left-over people in the street belonging to some other parties.

Shoulder flat surface: In the middle of the improvement hill, we can struct with a local flat for a few moments. For example, only two neighbours are different from our ideology. The shoulder should not avoid the movements because we may go into global maximum after some time from this shoulder walk. Sideways is the solution for this issue. The algorithm will allow a few constant number steps before stopping the moves. The robot may tell employees to try for another ten steps with flat answers.

Reverse cases: From local maxima, we may go down due to negative campaigns. Alternatively, we purposefully make decisions to go down to restart again.

Local maximum to global maximum: Sometimes, we plan to go to global maximum from local maximum by coming down and going up. It may get looped.

There are other variants of algorithms also available for hill climbing.

One is stochastic hill climbing:

What is stochastic hill climbing means? Instead of selecting the best neighbour for the next move, we can get a random choice from the best neighbours. It means that when we have more similar or closely weighted top options, we choose anyone randomly instead of the best. It will help us to avoid selection time and analysis weightage. When we go in this mode, the convergence will not significantly improve, which will go in a zig-zag way. This zig-zag will increase the objective function and decrease it subsequently. This one touch all the maximum local hills in one shot. So, identifying the best route will get identified in the zig-zag movement. Revisit the logic one more time here. Instead of strictly selecting the best neighbour, we can select the best among the bests at random to avoid straight improvements. It may fluctuate but slowly converges to the highest hilltop.

The second one is first choice hill claiming:

The next one is the first-choice hill claiming. Imagine that one of the current house owners is saying that there are 50 good houses on that street, and all these houses provide good outcomes. Selecting the best one will not be possible because it is a costly operation in this movement. So, the employee can quickly select the first choice of neighbour instead of wasting time. We call this algorithm first-choice hill claiming. Here the convergence will go in a zig-zag way. Finally, however, it will reach the maximum one.

One last variety is random restart hill climbing:

Our core aim is to reach the global maximum or global solution. However, suddenly, if we find a local maximum, we want to be safe. So, we may restart the whole algorithm again by starting the game from another starting point. The starting point also matters. If we see in our example, the robot mentioned that the employee could choose any house as starting point when starting the algorithm. However, after reaching the local maximum, the employee might randomly restart the house from another street corner. It will help the thread reach the global maximum. We call this algorithm a random restart hill claiming.

### 4.3 Simulated Annealing

Whatever the zig-zag moments we have seen in the above algorithms belong to the regulated differential calculus approach only. The agent chooses only the optimal choice, and eventually, it ends up with a gradient decent approach. Instead of the optimal selection, if the agent performs the random selection with temperature probability, we call this a simulated annealing approach.

Note: What is a gradient decent approach? Converging into the optimal solution with the help of differential calculus is called the gradient descent approach. This approach belongs to the cost optimization part of mathematics.

So that we do not need to follow the gradient approach (Differential calculus regularity), and we can follow it like the ping pong game. Temperature probability introduces here to make the big zig-zag initially and slowly reduce the heat. It is straightforward, like a ping pong game. Initially, the temperature was high. So that more oscillation will be there. So slowly, the temperature will come down when the steps are moving. Based on the temperature, we decide the probability of selecting the randomness. From our example, we can discuss the above logic. Initially, the employee will choose the house randomly for the campaign. Then the heat is high, and the probability also is high. So, the next choice will be random. Slowly the heat will get reduced when the steps are moving. When the heat is getting reduced, the probability also will get reduced. Finally, the random choice will come down when the probability is less. Ultimately, the employee will follow the optimal choice instead of the random one.

What is the benefit of this algorithm?

When we oscillate our moves in all the areas of the solution space, we will reach the global maximum. So even though it delays, the probability of getting the solution (Global Maximum) is high.

### 4.4 Local Beam Search

Local Beam search is the next set of algorithms. So instead of only one state in the execution, we can keep K states. At a time, we can use K states in execution instead of only one state. That is the logic. In parallel, K states will move into the next successor and K information will get executed into the center. It means that it is not just the parallel random restart-based algorithm. We are not doing independent parallel threads in the execution. These parallel threads exchange information while in execution. It will make more benefits. Quickly the agents will change the path, based on the best route found. If we take our example, imagine we add more K helpers to our existing 16 people. These helpers will help in the same street. All helpers will choose random houses in the street. Once the first attempt finishes, all workers connect and discuss the route. They will find the best information and try attacking there. Suppose someone says that the north side of the street is better for campaigns based on the evidence, then everyone will choose that side for campaigns.

The next one is stochastic beam search. As with stochastic hill climbing, in beam search, if we choose random choices instead of the optimal ones, this is called stochastic beam search. We already know that beam search means ‘K’ helpers will do activities in parallel and exchange data at the end of the steps. So, everyone chooses the optimal path. However, even though it chooses the optimal path, it still maintains the optimal selection instead of a random one. It is the opposite in stochastic beam search. In stochastic beam search, there is a privilege for the ‘K’ workers to choose the random one from the best path. It means the discussions will be there after all the steps. Moreover, all ‘K’ workers will choose the optimal person’s route. However, they will not choose the best move in that path, and they will choose the random path.

### 4.5 Genetic Algorithms

Whatever we have seen above in stochastic beam search, the same logic will express one more additional way. This genetic algorithm is another variant of this. The stochastic beam search is the asexual reproduction algorithm. What does that mean? Instead of choosing a random move by one set of ‘K’ people, genetically, we can mix two sets of ‘K’ people’s movements and get the random child of logic. This child will have a decisive move. It is the subsequent move reproduction from the best move mix.

From our example point of view, imagine that all 16 employees, when they collect with their ‘K’ helpers, will exchange in-between data in the genetic algorithm and choose the best one in a reproduction way.

For example, first street helpers found that the north side has some clusters in the first street. Suddenly, the second street found a cluster on the north side of the second street and the south side. Here overall data will execute the robot. The robot will genetically reproduce the plan: it will ask first street helpers to mix their plan with the second street north cluster, too, instead of asking second street helpers to split. Likewise, our robot will ask many streets to mix their strategy. It will make good optimization in time and logic. As a result, the global maximum will get achieved.

Refer to Figure 4.3 below, explaining the logic behind this genetic algorithm. Also, we can discuss the steps of this algorithm in this example.
