## Statistical Methods: Statistics and Probability Basics

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

Statistics plays a vital role in both machine learning and artificial intelligence. The training and final production data in machine learning require massive statistical analyses. With statistics and probability, we can perform our machine learning algorithms. These are very important. In the Artificial Intelligence field, modern uncertainty analysis comes from probability analysis. Computer vision and other robotics-related analyses need statistics and probability. Initially, in the first chapter of this section, we can look into the basics of statistics and probability. Probability and statistics are helpful because it provides a framework for analysing uncertainty and variability.

### 10.1 Data and Data Visualization

Our life entirely depends on data bags. Data, Data, Data. Wherever we go, we are seeing a massive set of data. For example, Raja tries to understand how many eat nonveg in the city. It is an extensive set of data. This data collection is required for him because he needs to submit this data as a college project, which is part of his curriculum. One fine Sunday, he and his girlfriend Ruby are planning to discuss with a random sample of people in the city, and they are planning to collect statistics from them. It will help them to analyse data as part of their survey.

Initially, he goes to the petrol bunk to fill the petrol for his two-wheeler tank. One employee is coming there to serve them, and he is saying that the petrol rate is 110 Indian rupees. Raja’s girlfriend suggests skipping this petrol bunk because the rate is very high for her. The petrol bunk employee mentions that there are 120 petrol bunks in the city and the average petrol price for that day is 112. He also mentions that his price is the lowest in the city. Raja thinks about it, and his intelligence from his brain says that he can only consume petrol in this shop. He also accepts that, and the deal is over with that petrol bunk. However, his girlfriend says that this is a blind decision.

Raja says they do not have any relevant websites in the city that can provide details about the current petrol price information. Moreover, it takes time to go physically to all the petrol bunks and collect the price details for that day. So now his girlfriend is somehow convinced.

As mentioned above example, in real life, everything is data. We need data visualization for all the data dealt with in day-to-day life. Therefore, data visualization matters a lot. The collected possible data depicts using visualization form. These data presentation skills are essential for data analysis, and these data analyses are required to infer some crucial decisions.

For example, the pie chart below helps Raja’s girlfriend decide to purchase the right surprise gift for Raja’s birthday.

![](Images/2.png)

Figure 10.1 Example Pie Chart – Raja’s Interest

From the above pie chart (Figure 10.1), Raja’s girlfriend infers that movie tickets are the best choice for Raja’s birthday gift. So, she buys movie tickets for Raja. Likewise, we have bar graph tables, line graphs, Venn diagrams, and 2-way tables to present the data. All these graphs help us to infer the data.

### 10.2 Central Tendency

Now we want to start analysing the data set differently. This section will examine what we all measure of central tendency. Central tendency means the centre of the dataset—the average value of the given set of values.

### 10.3 Mean

“Mean” means average. Therefore, it is an arithmetic average or arithmetic mean. We usually use the below formula for calculation.

![](Images/Image170408.png)

Here µ is the mean, and N is the number of data elements in the data set. In the numerator, we are adding all the data set arithmetic values. We can also say like below.

“µ is calculated from the total sum of all the data elements by dividing with a count of data elements.”

It is a balancing point. We can discuss this with one example. If we have datasets 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, the mean value will be 11. In the same way, if we take another data set with the values like 9, 11, 13, the mean value will be 11. It means the mean value is the centre value of all the data values.

### 10.4 Median

The Median is another form of centre value. We are not just simply taking average value here. We are giving more importance to several data values of the data set instead of their arithmetic value.

Say, for example, in a class, ten people are studying. Two people got 100 marks in the maths exam. Two got 90 marks, one got 50 marks, and five got 0 marks. When we calculate the mean, we get the value  
below.

100+ 100 + 90+ 90+ 50+ 0+ 0+ 0+ 0+ 0 / 10 = 43

However, imagine if someone sees this mean value, they will say that this class is good, and the mean value of 43 guarantees that this school’s education is average and good. However, five people got zero marks. So, 5 out of 10 people got zero. This mean value does not show the exact middle representation of the data set because a few high values influence the mean value. The Median provides the average data value from the count perspective instead of an arithmetic average. See the below procedure for calculating the median value.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Convert the data values in ascending order.

For example, if we get the data set values like 90, 50, 100, 90, 0, 100, 0, 0, 0, 0 → We need to keep this dataset in ascending order like 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 50, 90, 90, 100. However, even descending order also is acceptable.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)From the total count, get the centre value by using the formula (n+1) /2
    
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Here in our case, the n value is 10, so the centre data value will be (10 + 1) /2 =5.5
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)What does this mean? Get the middle data position of the data set. Keep in mind that this is not the actual value of the dataset. It is the position value.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)If we have an odd number, the middle data-position will have an exact number instead of a decimal value. In case we have nine values in the data set, then the middle data-position will be (9+ 1)/ 2= 5
        
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Now, this is the final step.
    
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Suppose our middle data position is an integer; the median value is in the same data position as the ascending order.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)If our middle data position is decimal, the median value is between two numbers, surrounded by this data-position value.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)In our data set, the middle position value is 5.5. it means we need to consider both the data position values 5 and 6. Now average these two values.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)The fifth and sixth position values are 0, 50. If we average them, the median value is 25.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)So, the median value for the above example is 25. However, if we compare it with the mean, the mean value is 43.
        

Now we will take one more odd number data set as an example. The dataset values are 45, 34, 2, 130, –13

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 1: Keep the data in ascending order: –13, 2, 34, 45, 130
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 2: Middle data-position value is: (5+1)/2 = 3
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 3: Since this is an odd dataset, get the third data value from the ascending order set. It is the Median of the dataset. The value is 34.
    

### 10.5 Mode

When there is a repeated occurrence of the same data values multiple times in the data set, we must address that. So, the mode is the central tendency’s variety, empathizing with these multiple occurrences. It is called mode. It is like an election result. Whoever gets more occurrences in the election (By vote) will be the top person. Similarly, the most repeated data value is the mode of the dataset.

Suppose we have a dataset like 56, 67, –3, 0, 100, 0, 34, 8, 0, 0, 0; then the mode value is 0 because the value repeats four times. So, in the class, if multiple people got zero marks in the exam, then the class mode is zero.

Sometimes, two people get equal votes. The sample data set is this: 12, 12, 23, 23, 67, 0, 100. Here 12 also appears twice, and 23 also appears twice. We need to consider both these values here, and this data set is called the bi-model dataset.

Suppose we have three or more data values with equal data appearance count. In that case, this data set is the only referred dataset.

### 10.6 Measures of Spread

Whatever we have seen in central tendency only refers to centre values. However, we need to analyse the dataset from a spread point of view. The places where the most spread is going on and where less spread is going on will get analysed. There will be a cluster in the dataset, and we can analyse this with the help of spread. We also call these scatters and spread measures.

There are three types of measures of spread, which we will see in this section.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Range
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Interquartile range (IQR)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Variance and standard deviation
    

### 10.7 Range

The number system is infinite. However, any data set will have a limited and finite range within this infinite number system. We need to calculate this difference by separating the most significant value of the dataset from the lowest value of the dataset. It is called range. If we take the below dataset, the range value is 50.

34, 84, 45, 81, 50

The most significant value is 84, and the smallest value is 34. So, the range is 84 – 34 = 50

### 10.8 Interquartile Range (IQR)

If we divide anything into two parts and then divide both parts into another two parts, we get four parts of the entire element. These parts are quarters. Similarly, we can divide any dataset into four parts.

We need to divide the ascending-order dataset into four parts. Even though we virtually cut them by four parts, we get three intermediate positions. These three intermediate positions are called quartiles (Quartile 1 is in the 25th percentile, Quartile 2 is in the 50th percentile, and Quartile 3 is in the 75th percentile).

Here the interquartile range (IQR) is the difference between the Median of the upper half and the Median of the lower half. It means that IQR = value in Quartile 3 – value in Quertile1. We will see this procedure with one example dataset.

The dataset is this \[Here, we perform the Ascending order\]

1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 3, 235, 8000

If we divide these into three quartiles, we get

![](Images/Image170417.png)

Here IQR will be Q3 – Q1; we need to get the Q3 value. Q3 is actually 2 + 235 /2 = 118.5

Q1 is actually (1+1) / 2 = 1.

Now IQR = Q3 – Q1 = 118.5 – 1 = 117.5

It is straightforward. For any given dataset, get the median value. This median value is Q2. Then get the Q1 and Q3 values from the two-sub-division dataset by calculating the Median of the sub-divided datasets.

### 10.9 Data Set Value Changes and Outliers

When the entire dataset values get changed, what will happen to the central tendency and spread of the particular dataset? There are two possibilities in the data set changes

If we change all the values by adding one standard constant value or subtracting one typical constant value.

If we add one additional data value into the data set or remove one data value from the data set

Will see these two changes below:

### 10.10 Constant Addition or Subtraction

We will check the impact of constant addition or subtraction in the below example. Imagine we have a data set like below

1, 1, 1, 1, 2

If we calculate all the centre tendency and spread values, we get the below values.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The mean is 1
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The Median is. 1.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The mode is. 1.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The range is 2 – 1 =0
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The IQR is 1 – 1 = 0
    

Suppose If we add the constant value 100 to these datasets, we get the new dataset like below

100, 100, 100, 100, 102

We get the below values if we calculate all the centre tendency and spread values for the new dataset.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The mean is 100.4
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The Median is. 100
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The mode is. 100
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The ranges 2
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The IQR is 0
    

When we add or subtract the constant, the Mean, Median, and Mode value change. However, Range and IQR stay the same. It means the spread will not get changed. It applies to any data set.

Add new value into a dataset or remove data value from the data set

Adding or removing the data value will affect the balancing point for the mean value. For example, adding or removing a data value from one side of the mean will force us to move the mean if we want to stay balanced.

What will happen to the Median: When we add or remove data value in the data set, sometimes the Median will change. It depends on the value with which we are working.

### 10.11 Extreme Values

The number having massive power to change the data set mean is called the outlier. It is a number on the dataset’s extreme upper or lower ends. The outlier means extreme values which are not close to the cluster. It is also a highly odd value.

For example, if we consider the data set 1, 1, 1, 1, 8035

While seeing the data set itself, we can quickly identify the outlier. There are six values in the above dataset. In that, five are near some cluster. One value is extreme. So, this extreme value is an outlier.

We can take one more example. Say we have a data set 1, 3, 5, 9, 24, 8045

In this example, the fifth value is more significant than the first four. However, the sixth value is significantly higher. The outliers will be in the extreme left or extreme right. So, 8045 is the outlier in the above example.

In the real-time example, the stock market value will peak some days. Extreme peak (Extreme up or extreme down) will be odd in 365-day average calculations. That exceptional day value is a special moon day, which we must avoid in average calculations. Otherwise, we will not see the accurate value. These values are outliers.

### 10.12 Box and Whisker Plots

Box and whisker plots are an excellent way to represent the data set. It will help us to show the Median and spread of the data set at the same time.

Generally, a box and whisker plot will look like the one below.

![](Images/Image170424.png)

Figure 10.2 Box and Whisker plot example

The big rectangular in the centre is the box in Figure 10.2 above. The little lines extending out from the sides are the whiskers.

We can identify the Median, range, and upper and lower bounds while seeing it. So, the IQR is just a simple calculation.

Another representation is five number summaries. Five-number summary means just showing the five values from the dataset: the minimum value, Q1 value, Median value, Q3 value, and maximum value. In one shot, we can show these data sets. Hence, these are called five-number summaries. Suppose we take an example dataset as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. Then here, the minimum value is 1, Q1 is 2, Median is 3, Q3 is 4, and the max value is 5.

### 10.13 Sample Mean, Variance, and Standard Deviation

#### 10.13.1 Sample Mean

In this section, we will discuss the population and sample. In the real world or the actual case, we cannot take all the data values for calculations. For example, suppose Raja would like to understand how many men are going to the gym in the whole country. In that case, it is impossible because this country’s population is 1.3  
billion.

So always collecting the population data is impossible. In many cases, we need to go for sampling data. Instead of discussing with all 1.3 billion people, we can discuss with only 200 people. They are the sample of that population.

So, collecting the data from the sample is very easy. However, we need to ensure that this sample represents the population crowd. Therefore, we need to collect the random value innovatively. We will discuss more about this in the later sections. It means that we will discuss more about how we collect the data. However, now we will understand the difference between the population and the sample.

How will the mean get affected for both population and sample data? For example, collecting data from 200 men about their sleeping hours will only be a small part of the population. For example, if we get the mean of all the 200 people as 7 hours, it will not be accurate to the population mean. Here, how we collect the sample also matters. If we collect the proper population spread as a sample, this sample will be correct.

So, in the statistics world, we donate the mean in two different ways. One is the population means, and the other is the sample mean. The “Population mean” gets denoted as µ, and the “sample mean” gets denoted as ![](Images/Image170431.png).

![](Images/Image170439.png)

![](Images/Image170447.png)

#### 10.13.2 Variance

Variance measures how far the data is getting spread from the mean. For example, population variance depicts as by σ2.

Why do we mention population variance as σ2? We will come to know about this at the end of this section. Before discussing variance, we will first see the variance formula. The formula for the population variance is this.

![](Images/Image170454.png)

In the above example, µ is a population mean. When we say (xi – µ), it is the distance between µ and other points. How is the deviation getting spread at each point? Then (xi – µ)2 is the squared deviation. Why do we need squared deviation?

The deviation is always getting spread in both positive and negative lines. It means that the mean is in the middle. The left side of the mean always gets negative values when we calculate the mean (xi – µ), and the right side always gets positive values when we calculate the mean (xi – µ). We add these negative and positive values together and get zero as a deviation result. It will happen all the time. Then here, how do we visualize the spread? It is the reason we square the variance. When we square the deviation, we get a positive number. When we square the negative values, we get a positive number. The positive deviation values will get a positive number when we square them. So, we add both positive values and get the total positive number as squared deviation.

Calculating the sample variance is also a similar process to population variance. We have one minor change in it.

![](Images/Image170463.png)

As the sample mean, the squared deviation also uses a small “n” value. We also call this a sample value. The denominator “n” refers to the sample count. The researchers also found that the above sample mean formula needs to be corrected. As per the research theorem, we must add degrees of freedom in the above formula. Instead of dividing the count by “n” values, we need to use “n-1” values. This n-1 will provide the accuracy value between the sample and population mean. We will discuss this degree of freedom algorithm sometimes nearby in this book. Otherwise, we will cover it later. We can understand that the sample deviation needs to be divided by n-1. So that one last number we keep as the degree of freedom. That DOF’s actual deviation will depend on the other n-1 values compared to population deviation. It is sufficient now. There is a theorem and proof for this, and that theorem is interesting. We call this n-1 formula an unbiased standard deviation. The previous one is a biased deviation. However, this unbiased variance provides accurate results in sample variance.

![](Images/Image170473.png)

Refer to the below example, which refers to variance.

For example, consider this data set 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. The mean of the data is 3, and the variance of this data set will look like below.

![190](Images/190.jpg)

Table 11.1 Variance calculation sample

So S2 value will be 10/5 = 2. \[Population dataset\]

If these are sample datasets, the sample variance will be 10/4 = 2.5

#### 10.13.3 Standard Deviation

Standard deviation measures how much data in a dataset varies from the mean. The larger the standard deviation value, the more the data in the set varies from the mean. Conversely, the smaller the standard deviation value, the less the data in the set varies from the mean.

Population standard deviation denotes as σ, and the value is the root of the population variance value (σ2). The root of population variance will provide a population standard deviation. Similarly, the sample standard deviation will get calculated from sample variance S2, and we denote this as S. Just the root of sample variance (S2) will provide the sample standard deviation S value.

![](Images/Image170580.png)

![](Images/Image170589.png)

Here is a table that summarizes the formulas from the above  
section.

| Total values in the data set || N || n |
| Mean || ![](Images/Image170612.png) || ![](Images/Image170624.png) |
| Variance || ![](Images/Image170634.png) || ![](Images/Image170642.png) |
| Standard Deviation || ![](Images/Image170651.png) || ![](Images/Image170661.png) |

A frequency histogram is just collecting the data and making the frequency occurrences.

When the density curve is perfectly symmetric, the mean and the Median are precisely the same, and both are at the centre of the distribution.

The Mean and the Median of the symmetric distribution will always be equal when we compare it from left and right. Figure 10.6 is an example of symmetric non-normal distribution.

![](Images/Image170697.png)

Symmetric distribution will have many flavours in shape. Usually, we denote the bell curve-based distribution as a normal distribution. We will discuss more about these normal distribution properties in the next topic. ### 10.16 Skewed Distributions

The skewed distribution is not symmetric. Either it will be leaned on right or left sides. We will look at negatively skewed distributions (also called left-skewed distributions or left-tailed distributions), and positively skewed distributions (also called right-skewed distributions or right-tailed distributions)

As shown below in Figure 10.8, the tail is on the right. The mean resides left to the Median here. Both are not the same. The mode will be in the peak value. It is called negatively skewed distribution.

As shown below in Figure 10.9, the tail is on the left. Here the mean is always on the right side of the Median. Both are not the same. The mode will be in the peak value. It is called positively skewed distribution.

How to identify the outliers from the given data set? We use the below formulas to identify the outliers. It derives from Inter Quartile Range values. ### 10.18 Normal Distribution

We already mentioned Normal distribution in the previous section. It is a bell-shaped curve where the mean and Median are always equal. The spread also is in a bell-shaped curve. The spread is smooth, and all options will be available in this distribution. If we take an example of any actual distribution in life, it will usually be a normal distribution. Consider any school or college classroom. All categories of rank holders will be there in the mixed classrooms. There will be few poor performers, and there will be few top performers in the classroom. Most of the people will be in the middle of the distribution too. The normal standard distribution curve has been visualized below in Figure 10.11.

![](Images/Image170738.png)

Figure 10.11 Normal distribution

We will discuss the essential properties of normal distribution in the below section. In statistics and probability, the normal distribution is significant. The normal distribution also has an empirical rule. It is called the 68-95-99.7 rule. The rule tells us that a 68% chance of data points falls within “One” standard deviation of the mean in the normal distribution. 95% chance of data point falls within “Two” standard deviations of the mean. Moreover, a 99.7% chance of data point falls within “Three” standard deviations of the mean. Graphically if we want to see this distribution percentage in the normal distribution, refer the below Figure 10.12

![](Images/Image170746.png)

Figure 10.12 Normal distribution with SD distributions

While seeing the above Figure 10.12, we can understand the spread of the data in the bell curve. If we need to understand how much percent of the data set is getting spread in each block, like the left side of the mean and right side of the mean. We can get that. That information is specified below. Here “One,” “Two,” and “Three” standard deviations are spread on both sides with an equal percentage split.

![](Images/Image170754.png)

Figure 10.13 Normal distribution with SD distributions

We deal with percentile a lot with normal distribution. What is a percentile, and how will it be used in a normal distribution?

The percentile is significant because, in statistics, we will refer to this percentile everywhere. The nth percentile is the value such that the ‘n’ percent of the values lie below this range. Conversely, a value in the 95th percentile is greater than 95% of the data. The Median is always the 50th percentile mark. The percentile at the rightmost corner is 100%, and the leftmost corner is 0%. Now we understand what the percentile concept is. Refer to below Figure 10.14 for percentile visualizations.

![](Images/Image170761.png)

Figure 10.14 Normal distribution with SD distributions

### 10.19 Z-Score

The Z-score helps us identify the specific point’s standard deviation from the mean in normal distributions. We need to know this data while working on the analysis. For example, we have one particular point in normal data distribution and need to know the percentile of the data set, which falls under this point. Also, we want to know how much percentile of the data set falls above these points. To know this percentile, we need a z-score of that point. We will discuss in the next paragraph why we need a z-score for percentile data. At present, understand this z-score value. Then, we use the formula to calculate a z-score for normally distributed data (normal distributions).

![](Images/Image170769.png)

Here x means our data point where we need a standard deviation z-score. μ is the mean, and σ is the standard deviation. Z-score is simply calculating how far this point is from the mean.

We usually subtract the mean from our data point and divide that answer by the standard deviation of that point. Here we will get a z-score. We have z-tables for reference. We refer to this table for any z-score value, which will provide the percentile data. It will say how much percentile data is below this z-score point. We have two z-tables. One is a negative z-score table, and the other is a positive z-score table. Negative table means which falls left side of the mean because any data point will be lesser than the mean. A positive table means it falls right side of the mean because any data point will be greater than the mean.

Refer to the below Figure 10.15. If our z-score value is 1.96, we can look at this table for a 1.96 z-score value. The value that’s shown here is the percentile. So, the 97.5th percentile data set falls under this z-score. So, the right side will be 100-97.5 = 2.5% \[The percentage data above this z-score point\]

![](Images/Image170778.png)

Figure 10.15 Z-score Values

To find the percentage of data above our data point, we have to subtract the z-score’s value from the hundred.

### 10.20 Probability

Now it is a time to discuss more about probability. Probability is significant in Mathematics. It is getting used in several logics and algorithms. In Artificial Intelligence and Machine learning, probability plays an important role.

What is probability? Probability is how likely it is that something will occur. We will write the probability in fractions, decimals, or any percentage form. However, the probabilities are always between zero and one.

The probability of zero means a 0% chance of that occurrence in that event. The probability of 1 means there is a 100% chance that it will happen. A probability between 0.5 and 1 means it is likely to occur. A probability of 0.5 is fascinating and has an equal chance for that event. Typically, in probability classes, mathematicians discuss flipping a coin. Alternatively, they will talk about rolling the dice. Otherwise, they will talk about pulling the card from the playing cards. In general, everyone uses these three examples.

How likely will we get heads when we flip a coin?

Everyone knows that we always get a 50% chance to get heads while flipping the fair coin.

How likely will the queen be there when pulling a card from the deck? How likely will things happen as compared to other neighbour events?

Here we usually denote the probability of the event as P of the event. So, we can say P(event). We refer to the probability of getting heads as P(Heads) and the probability of getting tails as P(Tails). We refer to the probability that a queen is paying cards as P(Queen).

Simple Probability: We must check the probability against the total possible outcomes.

P(event) = Outcomes that meet our case / all possible outcomes

Here all possible outcomes mean sample space. It is the entire world of possibilities. For the flipping coin example, the sample space is heads or tails. The sample space is getting denoted as

S = {H, T}

So, only two possible outcomes are there. When we try to calculate heads alone for probability measurement, we get a probability like the one below.

P (Heads) = ½. There is a 50% chance that we will get heads when we flip a coin one time.

We can also denote the actual outcomes as all possible equally likely outcomes.

P(event) = Outcomes that meet our criteria/All possible equally likely outcome

Example: The probability of pulling a queen card from the total possible 52 cards in the playing cards game will be like below

P(Queen) = 4/52 = 1/13 (4 different flowers of the queen will be there)

The probability also has two flavours.

Theoretical probability: We know the probability before executing any experiment. It is called Theoretical probability.

Experimental probability: The reasonable probability, and this is getting calculated from the recent experiment.

Example: Theoretically, when we flip the fair coin, the probability of getting “Heads” will be 0.5 (½). In the experiment, we may get heads when we flip the coin. From the experiment, the probability value will be 100% for the event that just happened. When we keep conducting experiments, after five experiments, we get the outcomes like this: H, H, H, T, H.

Here the experimental probability of heads will be 4/5 = 80%. Usually, the experimental probability will come closer to the theory after massive trials. Slowly the accuracy will come after an extended period, like 1 million trials. However, it differs between cases.

### 10.21 Probability – Addition, Union, and Intersection

Most of the time, we must find the probability from two different events. It is because two different events occur together within one experiment. Event is the specific collection of outcomes from the sample space. For example, what is the probability of getting the sum value as an actual value when we die a pair of dice?

There are 36 possibilities as the total outcomes when we die a pair of dice. Out of these 36 possibilities, we have 18 getting even sum values.

The below specified Thirty-six possibilities are total outcomes. In that, 18 values refer to even sum values (when we add two dice values, we get the sum).

((1,1), (1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (1,5), (1,6),  
(2,1), (2,2), (2,3), (2,4), (2,5), (2,6),  
(3,1), (3,2), (3,3), (3,4), (3,5), (3,6),  
(4,1), (4,2), (4,3), (4,4), (4,5), (4,6),  
(5,1), (5,2), (5,3), (5,4), (5,5), (5,6),  
(6,1), (6,2), (6,3), (6,4), (6,5), (6,6))

We can draw in Venn diagram if we have two different events from the same sample space. The total space has 36 possibilities. However, adding two events within the same space might have some relationships. We can see those relationships here.

For example,

Event 1 → when we analyse the probability of getting the sum value as an even value, we get 18 possibilities.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)18/36

Event 2 → when we analyse the probability of getting one from either of the dice, we get 11 possibilities.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)11/36

![](Images/Image170789.png)

Figure 10.16 Probability addition

The above Venn diagram (Figure 10:16) shows both events. These events are mutually exclusive. Mutually exclusive means both events get the same outcome. For example, event one also gets a (1,1) outcome. When we say sum value as even, the same value pair (1,1) will fit under this event two probability. These are called mutually exclusive.

Addition rule

Probability addition is constantly coming under the addition rule or the sum rule of the probability that

P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A and B)

If there is no overlap between A and B, then we call those events mutually exclusive. Then P (A and B) will be 0.

Now what happens if there is no overlap between A and B? In that case, A and B are mutually exclusive (or disjoint), and P (A and B) will be 0. It means the addition rule will simplify this way: P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A and B)

P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − 0  
P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B)

![](Images/Image170798.png)

Figure 10.17 Probability – Mutually exclusive events

Union and intersection

In the addition rule, we have seen that we have “or” and “and” keywords, which can represent “∪”, “∩” These are called unions and intersections.

P (A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A ∩ B)

P (A ∪ B) is called the union of A and B; (Add both sets of outcomes here. It is the addition.)

P (A ∩ B) is called the intersection of A and B; (Both A and B will have the outcomes)

## Statistical Methods: Independent probability

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

When we say independent events, which do not affect one another, for example, if we toss the coin two times, the results of the first toss do not affect the second toss. So, these tosses are independent events.

If we get a head on the first toss, the second toss is equally likely. So, the second event has a chance for heads or tails. It is independent of the first event results. So, these are called independent probability or independent events.

### 11.1 The Multiplication Rule

If we want to find the probability of multiple independent events or the joint occurrence, we multiply the probabilities. It is called the multiplication rule. The probability that we get heads twice when we flip a coin.

P (A and B) = P(A) ⋅ P(B)

The formula mentioned above is the multiplication rule. We can multiply two independent events (which are sequential) to find the total probability.

We can take a simple example of this. The probability of getting heads in the first coin toss is 1/2. The probability of getting heads in the second toss also is 1/2. There are independent events.

The results of the first toss do not affect the other toss events. Therefore, we can calculate the probability of getting two heads from the two independent events by multiplying the individual probabilities to see the overall probability of the two events. For the above example, the total probability will be ¼. We got this from the multiplication rule.

(1/2) \* (1/2) = (1/4)

We have four options from two independent events. Those are {HH, HT, TH, TT}. From this, we have only one option when we need only heads from both events. That is {HH}. That is why we are getting (1/4) as the overall probability for getting “Heads” in both trails.

Here multiplication rule tells us that when we have an independent event multiple times, we can multiply all the events.

For example, the probability of getting king from 52 playing cards will be 1/13. Again and again, when we replaced the card, we picked it with card reck and tried the same experiment again; this comes under the independent probability category. So, we can multiply the probabilities.

If we try for five trails, we calculate the probability of all five trails like below.

\= (1/13) \* (1/13) \* (1/13) \* (1/13) \* (1/13)

### 11.2 Dependent Probability

It is opposite to the independent probability. We call this dependent probability if the event entirely depends on one previous event. For example, suppose the second subsequent event depends on the first event’s outcome; this is called dependent probability. For example, we get the king card when we pull a card from 52 deck cards. Here imagine that we need to replace the card. Then the second subsequent event probability and outcome are ultimately affected by the first outcome because now we have only 51 cards and three kings out of 51 cards. So, when we calculate the probability for the king in the second attempt, we will get the probability as 3/51. So, this second probability is called conditional probability. We usually denote the conditional probability as P(A | B). So, we need to call this probability of A given B.

Using the multiplication rule, if we calculate the overall probability for two subsequent events of King cards under this dependent probability category / Conditional probability category, we can get the probability like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (King from event 1) = 1/13
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (King from event 2 when we did not replace king from event 1) = 3/51=1/17
    

Then multiplication rule will be

(1/13). (1/17) = 1/221

In general, this multiplication rule formula will be like below

P (A and B) = P(A) ⋅ P(B|A)

Here P(B|A) is the probability that B occurs given that A has already occurred. If we recall the activities again, imagine that we have already completed the first event. Then, we use this first event’s experimental outcome while calculating the second event. So, the second event is purely in the new condition world (i.e., within the outcome, our unlimited options got reduced for the second event. so, the total outcomes got reduced due to the first outcome).

### 11.3 Bayes’ Theorem

This theorem tells us the probability of an event, given prior knowledge of related events that occurred earlier. Bayes’ Theorem is

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)

Bayes’ theorem is an excellent tool for identifying conditional probability with prior knowledge and new evidence. We can also use the tree method, which is a simple one. The tree method can be used for minor problems and cannot work with the tree method on big problems. That is why Bayes’ theorem is a very effective one. We will look into one more example. In that example, we will solve the problem with both methods. Both methods will have the same answer.

Example:

Raja is buying one lottery sheet which belongs to one famous lottery system of that city. In that lottery sheet, one crucial secret number is on the backside. It has four flavours (1, 2, 3, 4). There are two lottery shops in the street. One is fair shop. Another shop is unfair. The fair shop’s lottery sheet will always have an equal probability of getting the four-flavoured secret number. It means we get a lottery sheet with a random number with a probability of (For 1 = ¼; For 2 = ¼; For 3= ¼; For 4=¼).

However, if anyone enters the unfair shop, 50% of the sheets have one as a secret number. So, it is a fake sheet. However, 50% of sheets are valid, and it has an equal probability of getting the other three secret numbers (2, 3, 4).

Raja needs to find out which shop is a fair shop and which is an unfair one. He has chosen one shop and bought one lottery sheet already. He got a secret number as 1. What is the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop?

Solution through Bayes’ theorem:

Since we are looking for the probability that the shop is unfair, given that the lottery sheet’s secret number is 1, we are looking for P (unfair shop /1). In this case, P(A)=P (unfair shop) and P(B)=p (1). So, we need to find the following values and put them into the formula

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (unfair shop /1)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (unfair shop)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (1)
    

We know from the problem that P (1/unfair shop) = ½, and since there are two shops in the street and each choice is equally likely P (unfair shop) = ½

The probability of getting one as a secret number is the probability of choosing the unfair shop and getting one as a secret number or the probability of choosing the fair shop and getting one as a secret  
number.

Let us first find the probability that the secret number is from the unfair shop, and we get one as a secret number.

P (unfair shop) = 1/2, and the probability of a one on the unfair shop is 50 %. So, the probability of unfair shop and 1 is

P (unfair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/2) = ¼

Now let us find the probability that the shop is fair, and we got one as a secret number.

P (Fair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/4) =1/8

Therefore, the probability of getting one as the secret number is

P (1) = 1/4 + 1/8  
P (1) = 2/8 + 1/8  
P (1) =3/8

Putting these values into Bayes’ Theorem, we get

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)  
P(A/B) = ½. ½ / 3/8  
P(A/B) = ¼ / 3/8  
P(A/B) =1/4.8/3  
P(A/B) =8/12 = 2/3

Solution through tree method:

How do we get this? Do we need to reconfirm from the tree view? Let us work on that now; we need to show branches in our tree for every event in our problem. Initially, Raja picked the shop. The shop can be fair or unfair, and we have an equal chance of picking each one.

![](Images/Image170806.png)

Each shop can lead to four possible outcomes, so that we will show these in our tree. However, we also know that the unfair shop will land on one 50 % of the time and that all three other outcomes are equally likely, which means we could think of this as three possible outcomes leading to 1 and one possible outcome leading to each of the other numbers.

![](Images/Image170815.png)

However, we always need to make sure our tree is balanced. We cannot have six branches from the unfair and four from the fair shop side. We have to have an equal number of branches on both sides so that we will scale up both sides to the least common multiple, 24. That means we need to scale up all the unfair shop branches by four and all the fair shop branches by 6.

![](Images/Image170822.png)

The next part of the problem tells us that Raja has chosen one shop and got a 1. So, we will trim all the branches of our tree that lead to results that did not occur.

![](Images/Image170829.png)

Now we want to know the probability that Raja chose \[Given the unfair shop\]. First, remember that each branch on the unfair shop side represents four possibilities, so 12 branches come from the unfair shop side. On the other hand, the branch on the fair shop side represents six possibilities, so six branches are coming from the fair shop side.

Therefore, the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop is all the outcomes coming from the unfair shop side, 12, divided by all branches in total, 18: P (unfair shop) = 12/ (12 + 6) = 2/3.

So, we matched Bayes’ theorem and standard tree methods. Both the values are the same. It is a simple example. If we are working on a big problem, imagine how helpful Bayes’ theorem is. It is very effective in Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning.

## Statistical Methods: Discrete Random Variables

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 12.1 Discrete Random Variables and Probability Distributions

A discrete random variable is a variable that can only take on discrete values.

What is discrete?

Any fixed set is discrete. The opposite of discrete is continuous. We cannot say that the finite option is continuous. The accurate height of a human being can be anything like the one below.

“5.1 feet,  
5.111 feet,  
5.11111 feet,  
5.111111 feet,  
5.11111111 feet,  
5.111111111 feet”

Do we see the standard height in the above example? From accuracy, we can go any deep in height. However, when we say all the class members have a range of heights between 5 and 6.2, this is called a continuous dataset. So, we cannot make precisely finite the options in continuous.

Opposite to this, discrete will have an finite set. So, it can have either 1000 or 2 trillion data items. However, it is finite.

For example, if we flip a coin twice, we can only get heads zero times, one time, or two times. We cannot get heads 1.5 times or 0.31 times. The number of heads we can get takes on a discrete set of values: 0, 1, and 2.

In probability distributions for all random variables, the probabilities of each possibility have to sum to 1, or 100 %.

It means that flipping the coin three times will get eight possible outcomes (TTT, TTH, THT, THH, HTT, HTH, HHT, HHH)

Suppose we take only heads as random variables; then we will have a headcount option as 0 Heads, 1 Head, 2 Heads, and 3 Heads. If we add all these probabilities, The total will be 100%. It is an excellent discrete probability distribution.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Say 0 Heads = 1/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)1 Heads = 4/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)2 Heads = 2/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)3 Heads = 1/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)So Total 1/8 + 4/8 + 2/8 + 1/8 = 1
    

The total should always have 100%, and the probability will be 1. So, any probability of the random variable will be between 0 and 1.

### 12.2 Additional use of Random Variables

In case we want to know the probability for three heads in the above example (In case we do not have that information), then other possible probabilities count will help us to decide because all the possible values in the random variable should be 1 (100%)

Say,

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0 Heads = 1/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)1 Heads = 4/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)2 Heads = 2/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)When we add these options 1/8+ 4/8 + 2/8 = 7/8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)To find the 3 Heads, we can calculate by 1-7/8 = 1/8.
    

We can visualize the exact details in graphs. Please refer to Figure 12.1.

![](Images/Image170837.png)

Figure 12.1 Example – Random variable

### 12.3 Expected Value

Once we have a probability distribution for a discrete random variable, X, we can calculate the expected value E(X), the mean of X. We often refer to the expected value as the “long-term average.” When we run an experiment repeatedly, this is the mean we expect to find. To find this value for a discrete random variable, we must “weigh” each value.

For example, we want to find the expected value for the number of heads when we flip a coin three times. In that case, we will multiply each value of X by the corresponding value of P(X) and then add them all together.

| X || Probability – Random Variable for Getting Heads When Flip the Coin: P(X) |
| --- | --- |
| 0 || 0.125 |
| 1 || 0.5 |
| 2 || 0.25 |
| 3 || 0.125 |

Table 12.1 Example – Random variable – P(Heads)

Therefore, the expected value is

E(X) = μX = 0(0.125) + 1(0.50) + 2(0.25) + 3(0.125)  
μX = 0 + 0.50 + 0.50 + 0.375  
μX = 1.375

Therefore, on average, we will expect to get 1.375 heads when we flip a coin three times. We can then extrapolate this to guess that, for example, we should get 1375 heads when we flip a coin 10000 times.

### 12.4 Variance and Standard Deviation

We can also find the variance and standard deviation for discrete random variables. To find the variance, we will take the difference between X and the mean, μX, square that difference, then multiply the result by the probability of X, called P(X).

We will do that for each value of X and then add all those results to get the variance, σ2X.

![](Images/Image170909.png)

Let us find the variance when X is the number of heads we get when we flip a coin three times, remembering that we already found E(X) = 1.375 for this probability distribution.

σ2X = (0 – 1.375)2 (0.125) + (1 – 1.375) 2(0.50) + (2 – 1.375) 2(0.25) + (3 – 1.375) 2(0.125)

![](Images/Image170916.png)

Table 12.2 Example – variance

σ2X = 0.734

We can also find the standard deviation of X, σX, which is just the square root of the variance.

σX = ![](Images/Image170924.png)

σX ≈ 0.856

### 12.5 Transforming Random Variables

We discussed shifting and scaling the entire dataset values during the central tendency and spread topic.

Shifting the data set by a constant c means adding c to every value in the data set or subtracting c from every value in the data set. On the other hand, scaling the data set by a constant c means multiplying or dividing every value in the data set by c.

As discussed already, mean, median, and mode usually change according to the change in the dataset. On the other hand, range and IQR will stay the same even though we scale or shift the entire dataset.

For example, in the original dataset, if we have values like below

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mean = 10
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Median = 12
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mode = 14
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IQR = 8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Range = 20
    

Then the shifted values will be like below

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mean = 10 + c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Median = 12 + c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mode = 14 + c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IQR = 8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Range = 20
    

Along with the above data, the standard deviation also remains the same.

The standard deviation (σ) will look like this below (Figure 12.2). The location of the data distribution changes from one location to another when we add constant c. However, the standard deviation never gets changed within the data distributions.

![](Images/Image170933.png)

Figure 12.2 Shifting the data set.

However, when we shift the dataset by multiplication and division, IQR, Range also changes. As a result, the standard deviation also gets changed.

For example, in the original dataset, if we have values like below

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mean = 10
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Median = 12
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mode = 14
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IQR = 8
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Range = 20
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Standard Deviation = σ
    

Then the scaled values will be like below (Ex: Multiplication)

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mean = 10c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Median = 12c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Mode = 14c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)IQR = 8c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Range = 20c
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Standard Deviation = σc
    

Because the scaling activity will shift the variance and deviation accordingly, Figure 12.3 shows that spread changes when we scale the dataset.

### 12.6 Linear Combinations of Random Variables

Whatever we have seen in the previous section was related to scaling or shifting the dataset with constant C. Entire data will change when it scales or shifts. Now we will see how two or more datasets get combined. It is the combination of two or more datasets.

For example, we can take one famous restaurant in Bangalore, India. After collecting all the 365-day data \[A year\] for the number of breakfast packets sold, we got a dataset. We can get the mean of the dataset and the standard deviation of the dataset; it will look like this below.

μB = 250 and σB = 0.2

At the same time, we also collected the dataset for the quality of the lunch packets sold. We measured the mean and standard deviation of this dataset too.

μL = 150 and σL = 0.1

However, the restaurant owner wants to understand the combined mean and standard deviation. Therefore, he calculates the average of the total packets sold for breakfast and lunch with the help of the below formulas.

We can denote the mean of these combined calculations like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μA = μB + μL
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μA = 250 + 150
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μA = 400
    

However, Standard deviation cannot be added just like the mean. We need to use the formula below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)σA2 = σB2 + σL2
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)So, σA2 = (0.2)2 + (0.1)2
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)σA2 = 0.04 + 0.01
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)σA2 = 0.05
    

Then to find the standard deviation for total activity, we take the square root of both sides. σA2 = 0.05

σA ≈ 0.22

In addition, we can also calculate the subtraction (The subtraction between two data sets)

We can denote the mean of these different calculations like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μD= μB – μL
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μD = 250 – 150
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)μD = 100
    

Standard deviation is the same as addition. No further changes.

When we combine normally distributed variables, the distribution will also be normal for the combinations.

So, we get the mean and standard deviation of two normally distributed variables. In that case, we can calculate the mean and standard deviation of the new combination.

However, since the distribution is normal for the combination, we can use what we know about the probability under normal distributions to answer probability questions about the combination.

![t\_12\_3](Images/t_12_3.jpg)

Table 12.3 Linear combinations of the data set

### 12.7 Permutations and Combinations

We need permutations and combinations of mathematics for many probability and statistical calculations. Many of us would have studied during our school days. So, we will revisit them here in this section.

A permutation means unique order-based combinations. How many numbers of ways can we arrange the set of data elements by keeping particular order in mind?

The formula for a permutation is

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)n P k = n! / (n − k)!

Here n is the total items in the data set, and k is the number of specific data items we need to arrange.

Example:

There are four shops in the street. Raja has time to visit only three shops in the street. How many different ways can he get it for his visit plan? (Possible order)

He may have ordered the visit plan something like this 123, 124, 234, 231 (If we denote the shop numbers as 1, 2, 3, 4, these are permutations)

So as per permutations calculations,

4 P 3 = 4! / (4 − 3)!  
4 P 3 = 4! / 1!  
4 P 3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1 / 1  
4 P 3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2  
4 P 3 = 24

So, there are 24 unique orders

Another measurement is a combination. Particular order does not matter here. Just combinations of the available data elements will get calculated.

n C k = n! / k! (n − k)!

Here n is the total items in the data set, and k is the number of specific data items we need to arrange.

Example:

Consider our old example with combinations in mind. There are four shops in the street. Raja has time to visit only three shops in the street. How many different combinations of shops can he visit there?

So as per combinations calculations,

n C k = n! / k! (n − k)!  
4 C 3 = 4! / 3! (4 − 3)!  
4 C 3 = 4! / (3!) (1!)  
4C3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1 / (3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1) (1)  
4C3 = 4 / 1  
4C3 = 4

Just 4. Yes. The combinations are (123, 124, 234, 134). Whatever the order we make, it does not matter here in combinations. Just how many combinations we can make matters.

### 12.8 Binomial Random Variables

Bi means two. So Binomial random variable means it can take only two exact values. It cannot take more than two. The coin will give either heads or tails. Only two values are possible in a coin. So Binomial random variable will work for this.

The die example has six values, and the card example has 52 cards. These cannot fit with binomial random variables. Here binomial random variable will work for any event for which we have only outcomes like “Success” or “Failure” with 50% probability.

### 12.9 Binomial Random Variable Characters

For a variable X to be a binomial random variable,

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)X should participate in multiple trials of the same action.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Each trial must be independent.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Each trial can be called a “success” or “failure,”
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)There are a fixed number of trials, and
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The probability of success on each trial is constant.
    

We will take one example; Let X be the random variable, and it gets counted as how many heads we can get when we flip the coin 100 times.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Here each trial is independent because we get the same coin each time without any change from the previous trial. The coin is a coin that never gets modified when we make trials.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Each time we get tails, we say it is a failure. If we get heads, we say that it is a successful one.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Fixed number of trails we are trying. Here, 100 times we are tossing the coin.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The probability of getting heads will be 50%, the tails also the same, and the probability of getting tails also will be 50%.
    

All five characters are satisfied here. So, the above example can fit under binomial random variable distribution.

Conversely, we can take another example, different from a binomial random variable.

Let us think about random variable K as how many kings we get when we pull three cards from a deck without replacing the card after each pull.

Here each trial is not independent because we are not replacing the card; whenever we remove one card, the next event will depend on the previous event.

Each time when we get king, it is called success. Otherwise, it is a failed case. This point is satisfying.

We calculate with the help of the fixed number of trails. It is 3. This point also is satisfying.

The probability is not constant. For example, 4/52 is the initial probability, which is not constant.

Condition 1 and condition 4 are not satisfied. So, we cannot use binomial random variable calculations for this random variable.

### 12.10 Binomial Probability

How do we calculate the probability in binomial? We have a specific formula to calculate the probability of success in fixed attempts. The formula is specified below.

![](Images/Image170985.png)

Here, Combination n C k will get calculated for the binomial coefficient.

![](Images/Image170993.png)

Example:

Three pens are in a bag: 2 are Ball-Point pens, and 1 is Ink-pen. We will do five trials where we pull a pen, note the type, and then replace the pen. What is the probability that we get the Ink-pen precisely three times?

First, we need to confirm the characters of binomial random variables for the above example.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It is independent because we are replacing the pen. This condition is satisfied.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ink pen means success. Ball-point pen means failure. So, we have only two options (Success/failure). This condition is satisfied.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Trails are 5. Fixed one. This condition is satisfied.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The probability is constant because we are replaying the pen. This condition is also satisfied.
    

So, it is a binomial distribution only. All four points are satisfied. So, we will use the probability calculation using the formula mentioned above.

The binomial coefficient will be

5C3 = 5! / 3! (5 − 3)!  
5C3 = 5! / 3! 2!  
5C3 = 10

Using formula

![](Images/Image171002.png)

P (3 Ink-pens in 5 Pulls) = (10) \* (1/3)3 \* (2/3)2 = 440/243 = 16.5%

We can also create a probability distribution for binomial random variables. For example, if we keep the random variable as only Ink-pens, we will have the following options in 5 trials.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (0 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.1350
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (1 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.3325
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (2 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.3275
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (3 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.1613
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (4 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.0397
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (5 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.0039
    

### 12.11 Poisson Distributions

Poisson distribution is another set of models for the discrete random variable. This one is beneficial for finding the probability that a specific number of events will occur in a given period.

We need to understand the poison process before we move into poison probabilities.

The Poisson processes

A Poisson process calculates the number of times an event occurs for a specific period. It could be in a particular area, or some particular distance, or within any other measurement. This process also has some characteristics. We will discuss these characters below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If we keep multiple intervals, the mean should be equal for all the intervals.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The experiment counts the number of occurrences of an event over some other measurement.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Interval-wise, independence will be there. There is no dependency between the intervals.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The intervals should not overlap.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The probability of the event occurring is proportional to the period.
    

Example of poison process and distributions

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The number of visitors to a movie theatre for the month. We need to collect the statistics for the day-wise count.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The number of trees in an acre of forest.
    

We will take one exponential poison example and check whether the above characters satisfy this example. We will calculate the number of visitors to a movie theatre example.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)We are counting the visitors for every show. The interval is the day. So, the mean number of visitors of the day is 300. Moreover, the same mean is getting maintained for the whole month. So, this is satisfactory.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)When we say interval as day, we can calculate the event. The event is a show. So, a second condition is also satisfied.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Intervals are independent because shows are unique. So, a third condition is also satisfied.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Movie shows do not overlap. So, the fourth condition is also satisfied.
    

The probability of the event occurring is propositional to the period where it matches. For example, if 100 people attend the theatre (C) for one show, it will be 200 (2C) for two. So, the probability is proportional to the specific period.

Since we met all the conditions, we can use the poison process and poison probability for further calculations.

Probability formula for Poisson:

Suppose we have precisely x occurrences in the particular event. In that case, the probability of that event will get calculated from the below formula. Here, the mean number of occurrences in the interval is λ, and we need this data for calculation.

P(x) = λx e−λ / x!

So, if we believe the mean number of visitors to that movie theatre for the day is 15, we can denote this as λ = 15. If we want to know the probability that x = 13 visitors will come the next day, then this probability will be

P (13) = 1513e\-15 / 13!  
P (13) = 9.56%

So, there is an approximately 9.56 % chance that 13 visitors will arrive the next day.

We generally use the Poisson formula to calculate the cumulative probabilities. For instance, if we want to know the probability that at most seven visitors arrive for the day at a movie theatre, we calculate that as

P (x ≤ 7) = P (x = 0) + P (x = 1) + P (x = 2) + P (x = 3) 182 +P (x = 4) + P (x = 5) + P (x = 6) + P (x = 7)

P (x ≤ 7) = 150e\-15 / 0! + 151e\-15 / 1! + 152e\-15 / 2! + 153e\-15 / 3! + 154e\-15 / 4!

\+ 155e\-15 / 5! + 156e\-15 / 6! + 157e\-15 / 7!

P (x ≤ 7) ≈ 0.018

So, there is an approximately 1.8 % chance that seven or fewer visitors will arrive at the movie theatre.

Poisson distribution to approximate the binomial distribution:

We already know how to calculate binomial probabilities, but using the Poisson distribution instead can save us some time.

The Poisson distribution very closely approximates the binomial distribution when the number of binomial trials n is at least 20, and the probability of success p in the binomial trial is at most 0.05.

If those two conditions meet here, then using the Poisson probability formula will give us an excellent approximation of the binomial probability. Moreover, the math for the Poisson probability is more accessible than the binomial probability, so that we will save some time. The probability of x successes in n attempts, given by the Poisson formula, is

P(x) = ((np) x e−np) / x!

Notice that we have substituted λ = np into the original Poisson formula.

“At least” and “at most.”

Most of the time, we say “at least” and “at most” in probability. Let us say that at least this random variable gets five as a sum. We calculate the probability behind all the options in that random variable. If we say, at most, this random variable gets five as the sum, we calculate the probability for all the below options in that random variable.

We have seen this example in the previous poison example.

For instance, if we want to know the probability that at most seven visitors arrive for the day at a movie theatre, we calculate that as

P (x ≤ 7) = P (x = 0) + P (x = 1) + P (x = 2) + P (x = 3) 182 +P (x = 4) + P (x = 5) + P (x = 6) + P (x = 7)

P (x ≤ 7) = 150e\-15 / 0! + 151e\-15 / 1! + 152e\-15 / 2! + 153e\-15 / 3! + 154e\-15 / 4!

\+ 155e\-15 / 5! + 156e\-15 / 6! + 157e\-15 / 7!

P (x ≤ 7) ≈ 0.018

Mean, variance, and standard deviation

The mean of a binomial random variable X expresses as μX.

The mean is also called the expected value, which denotes E(X). The means are specified below

μX = E(X) = np

where n is the fixed number of independent trials, and p is the probability of success. The variance of a binomial random variable X is

σ2X = np (1 – p)

Standard deviation is the square root of the variance and is therefore given by

![](Images/Image171009.png)

### 12.12 Bernoulli Random Variables

Bernoulli’s random variable is the same as the binomial random variable except for several trials. We have this significant difference here. Bernoulli random variable means it deals with only one trial. We hope the reader remembers that binomial random variable deals with multiple trials. Rest all conditions are the same in Bernoulli. Only two options must be there as options (Success or Failure)

Bernoulli Distributions

Imagine, when we go to any cricket stadium, we can see that all the fans will support either one of the teams. Considering that no one is neutral, the complete probability is 100% (the probability for team A and the probability of team B) within the stadium.

If we take the survey, 25% of fans support Team A, and 75% support Team B. This distribution is called the Bernoulli distribution. The visualization of the Bernoulli distribution is specified below in Figure 12.4.

![](Images/Image171016.png)

Figure 12.4 Example – Bernoulli Distributions

Mean, variance, and standard deviation for a mean of Bernoulli Distributions

We can take the probability-weighted sum of the values in our Bernoulli distribution for the mean. If we take the above cricket stadium example,

μ = (percentage of failures) (0) + (percentage of successes) (1)

μ = (0.25) (0) + (0.75) (1)

μ = 0 + 0.75

μ = 0.75

It is the mean of the Bernoulli distribution. Notice how the value we found for the mean equals the percentage of “successes.”

In general, it is success probability.

μ = p

For variance, also the similar way we get the output

σ2 = (0.25) (0 − μ)2 + (0.75) (1 − μ)2

σ2 = 0.1875

After taking root of the above variance, we get the standard deviation. The standard deviation value will be

σ = 0.4330

In general, as with the mean in Bernoulli Distributions, the variance can be calculated only from the success probability of that event, as below.

σ2 = p (1 – p)

The standard deviation for Bernoulli Distributions is:

![](Images/Image171024.png)
