## Statistical Methods: Sampling

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 13.1 The Art of Collecting Statistical Data

Whatever we have studied so far is very important in statistics. However, all those logics will be perfect only if the data set is appropriately collected. The study of data must be accurate. We are collecting excellent and reliable data from the samples. In most cases, the population data is vast in numbers. Therefore, we usually collect samples, make decisions from sample statistics and derive the answers for population data statistics.

### 13.2 The Goal of Collecting Samples

The purpose of statistics is to gather information (data) about the world around us and analyse it to help make sense of it.

As we mentioned in the previous paragraphs, collecting the population is impossible. Therefore, we chose a smaller sample of the large population and then analyse the data for the collected samples. Finally, we hope that our results will translate to the larger population.

Usually, mean and standard deviation are very important. These characteristics are called sample statistics from a sample perspective. A parameter is the corresponding characteristic of the population that the statistic is trying to estimate. So, we could choose a sample, calculate the sample mean (a statistic), and then use what we know about the sample mean to make inferences about the population mean (a parameter)

It means that we collect the sample data, calculate the sample mean and infer the population means with the help of some effective techniques.

So, before all, we need to know how we collect the samples.

### 13.3 Observational Study and Experimental Study

An observational study is a method that will look into the information already there. We are not modifying anything in the population or sample data set. Instead, just like the third-party statistics collector, we collect the data. This method is called an observational study.

For example, go to some city streets and collect statistics from people about their interest in mangos. Since this street is the sample, whatever we collect will replicate the entire city population (population data). However, we are not modifying anything and just observing the data.

Opposite to an observational study is an experimental study. In an experiment, we manipulate what is happening and try to establish causality, not just correlation.

For example, people get sample statistics for innovative medicine in the medical field. Then, we inject innovative medicine into the sample people. It is called an experimental study.

### 13.4 One-Way Tables and Two-Way Tables

As mentioned above, most of the time, single data will get collected, like collecting the mango’s interest in the sample street. These are called one-way tables.

Instead of this, if we collect two data from samples and connect or correlate both data in statistical studies and analysis, these are called two-way tables.

In the one-way table also, the unbiased collection is mandatory. The representations must be random. For example, if we had collected the statistics from only above 50 ages, this would be biased because 50% of these 50-age people will have diabetic complaints. Diabetic people will not consume mangos. Then this needs to be more objective, and the statistics employees must collect from random people.

Also, we do not observe anything else in the one-way table, other than single inference.

Instead of this one-way table approach, if we additionally collect more data from people, like whether they are diabetic compliant, it will be good. We may correlate the mango’s interest and diabetic complaint. We need to observe this correlation from the sample studies.

Two variables are correlated when they move together predictably. The variables are positively correlated when they increase together or decrease together. Variables are negatively correlated when they increase and decrease in opposite directions: one goes down while the other goes up or up while the other goes down.

On the other hand, causation means that one variable causes another variable to change. However, just because we show correlation does not mean we have proven causation. If we want more details about this paragraph, do not worry. We are going to discuss this in the next chapter.

### 13.5 Exclusive to Experimental Studies

For any experiment, we need to assign people into two groups. So, we use random sampling techniques. It means we will choose people from all categories (regardless of age, sex, or religion). So that groups are not biased.

One group is a control group, and we are not doing anything with them. It means that we will not conduct any experiments with them. Another group is the actual experimental group. With this group only, we do the experiments. Why do we need a control group? It is required to make people blind. Nobody knows whether we have given the sample group vitamin tablets or natural medicine. So that no one will influence the results, the little influence will be there with patients, doctors, and all parties in the experiment. For example, suppose the patient says he got a negative effect due to the experiment. In that case, the doctor will also favour him from the mind. Here, we need some confidential data from everyone during the experiment. That is why we have a control group.

### 13.6 Sampling and Bias

Picking random data is very important. If we take our previous example, we can understand that we need to collect statistics from all diversified people. We will always have a bias when we collect the data. If we precisely match the population perfectly, that is called unbiased sample collection. Bias also has many reasons. Random sampling agents must avoid this.

### 13.7 Sampling Techniques

Simple random sample: If we directly divide the groups from samples, this is called a simple random sample. For example, when we enter the street, we randomly send two groups of samplers to houses. However, finally, at the end of the sampling activity, we found that group 1 collected the details from 95% of men. In this case, this random fashion is not correct. They got this bad split from the random houses. It is a big issue with simple random techniques.

Stratified random sample: Instead of direct random, we can put some parameters in control for Stratified random samples. Say when we send groups for sampling, ask them to collect the data in the Stratified controls (Sample Stratified: 50% men and 50% women; 20% child and 30% youth; 30% mid-age and 20% old age; Rich houses: 50% and poor houses:50%). Practically they collect the samples from this category with the specified ratio.

Clustered random sample: Imagine we need to collect samples from school students about traffic jams. If we randomly collect the samples from village schools, it does not matter. So even for population data, there are some clusters. If the population identifies clusters naturally, we perform the clustered random sample method. Here Stratified random methodology can be used.

### 13.8 Sampling Distributions of the Sample Mean

As we discussed multiple times, collecting the entire population data is difficult. We usually collect the samples and measure mean, variance, and standard deviation from this sample data. However, these results are different from population results.

For example, if we collect the height of the eleventh-grade students from one school, the mean value will be 5.1 feet. However, it will not be with the actual population mean if the population is the entire country. Then the sample data will not get matched.

‘N’ is the population count, and ‘n’ is the sample count. So even though we collect the perfect representative from random sampling, the mean value will need to be more accurate.

We can discuss this sample-population issue with one minor dataset.

In one particular class, we have 40 students with a mean height of 65 inches. Consider these 40 students as a population. However, we plan to collect the sample survey only from 4 students. Initially, we get the data from the tallest girls. The sample’s mean would not be a reasonable estimate of the population mean because the mean height of the sample is 217 inches which is very far from the population means. Similarly, if we instead happened to choose the four shortest girls, the sample mean would be much lower than the actual population means.

So how can we correct this issue?

Instead of only one sample, we can take multiple samples from the population. For example, instead of picking only four students as one sample here, we can pick multiple sample groups. The total number of possible samples is (Nn), where N is the total population from which we take our samples, and n is the sample size.

In our example, Sample ‘n’ is 4, and the population ‘N’ is 40. If we calculate this, we get a total of (404) = 2,560,000 possible samples that can be taken from the 40-member class. Consider that this example is talking about sampling with replacement. It means we are replacing the samples with the population and collecting the following sample. The same sample elements may reappear in the new sample combination.

The fascinating thing is this. Imagine if we are collecting all the possible 2,560,000 samples and get the sample means. These means fit in a normal distribution. It means that this normal distribution is not related to our actual values. This normal distribution is for the mean value of samples. It is called the Sampling distribution of sample mean (SDSM).

![](Images/Image171032.png)

Figure 13.1 Sampling distribution of sample mean (SDSM)

Refer to Figure 13.1 for the sample mean (SDSM) sampling distribution. Just think about the sampling distribution of the sample mean as the probability distribution of all possible values of the sample mean ![](Images/Image171039.png).

We do not need to take all possible sample combinations for calculations. Instead, we can take enough samples to calculate the statistics. For example, suppose we assume the population is the normal distribution and use a sample size more significant than 30 (n ≥ 30); we can take the sampling distribution. Here, the sampling distribution of the sample mean is average. From this sampling distribution statical calculations, we can answer any probability questions.

Central limit theorem

The sampling distribution of the sample mean is normal only if the original population fits under the normal distribution. Here sample size does not matter. Even though the sample size is small, the sample mean (SDSM) sampling distribution will fit under the normal distribution. It is happening because the original population itself fits within normal distribution.

Most of the time, the original distribution will not fit under the normal distribution. In that case, the Sampling distribution of the sample mean (SDSM) will fit under normal distribution for more extensive size samples (n ≥ 30). Otherwise, if the sample size is below 30, SDSM will not fit under normal for the non-normal population.

Most populations (about 90%) do not fit under normal distributions. Normal distribution means exact bell-shaped distribution. It is only possible in some cases. Most of the population fits under non-normal distributions.

The central limit theorem tells us to convert the non-normal population distribution into normal sampling distribution of the sample means using the abovementioned logic. That is, taking a sample count of more than 30 will help us to get the normal distribution for SDSM (n ≥ 30). If we take a sample count of more than 30 for multiple samples, automatically, these sample mean fits under normal distributions.

So, the Central Limit Theorem is helpful because it lets us apply what we know about normal distributions, like the properties of mean, variance, and standard deviation, to non-normal populations.

Mean, variance, and standard deviation

The central limit theorem also tells us that the mean of all the sampling distributions of the sample mean will always be the same as the mean of the corresponding population. It means that

![](Images/Image171047.png)

Coming to variance analysis, we can calculate the SDSM variance by using the following formula only if we know the population variance. It will work if we replace the sample with the population for all sampling. Also, it will work when the population is infinite.

![](Images/Image171056.png)

However, most of the time, we will not get the population variance. No one will give this information. In these cases, we will measure the sampling variance. We need to consider this sampling variance as population variance.

![](Images/Image171067.png)

We call the standard deviation of the sampling distribution for the sample mean (SDSM) as sampling error. Refer to Figure 13.1. It means the standard deviation of sample means. We also call this a standard error of the mean. The formula for calculating that is as

![](Images/Image171076.png)

The above formula will work only when we use population standard deviation σ. Here n is the sample size. We can still use the sample standard deviation if we do not know the population standard deviation. It will help us to match the population standard deviation.

![](Images/Image171084.png)

The standard error helps us to match the sample mean with the population mean. Here we mention the same error because we say how this sample deviated from the population. For example, 40 students with standard heights will be in the class. If we take the sample of 4 incredibly tall students, then this sample has completely deviated from the average crowd. So, this sample means deviated from the population crowd. So, we call it a significant error from the population average.

When the standard error is more extensive, the sample means in the SDSM are more spread out, making it less likely that any given sample mean accurately represents the actual population mean. However, when the standard error is minor, the sample means are less spread out, making it more likely that any given sample mean accurately represents the actual population mean.

Usually, the standard error will get an immense value when the population standard deviation is significant. Also, the standard error will get a considerable value when the sample size is small. Say less than 30.

Conversely, the standard error will get a small value when the population standard deviation is slight. Also, the standard error will get a small value when the sample size is big. Say more than 30.

Finite population correction factor:

The value of the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample mean (Standard Error) is

![](Images/Image171093.png)

The above formula needs to be more accurate. We need to correct something to make it perfect.

![](Images/Image171100.png)

The finite population correction factor must be added here in the standard error formula. However, this factor needs to be added only in a few conditions. The conditions are specified below.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)When the population is finite, we use a sample size of more than 5% of the finite population.

We only replace the samples after taking the subsequent samples.

In all other cases, we do not need this correction factor, and we can directly use the formula without this correction factor.

Only some examples are infinite population, sampling replacement cases, and small samples from population count.

As with standard deviation, variance also gets a similar correction from the Finite population correction factor.

![](Images/Image171107.png)

Conditions for Sampling distributions of the sample mean (SDSM) inference:

The central limit theorem tells us we can use SDSM for population analysis. We need population data analysis. These samples and sampling distribution help us to make inferences very clearly.

For example, we want to know the population mean. In that case, we can take the sample mean and fit the sample mean in the sample distribution of the sample mean (SDSM). When comparing our sample mean against to mean of the sample distribution of sample mean (SDSM). This comparison will help us in population mean analysis.

However, we must satisfy the conditions below for accurate analysis and inference. These are valid, and they will make valid results against to population.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample count should be more significant. It should have more than 30 counts.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample size should be more than 10% of the population count.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample should be random.
    

The sample count should be more significant. It should have more than 30 counts:

The sample count should be more than 30 for the non-normal distribution of the population. However, if it is under normal distribution, we do not need to be strict with n>=30.

We also have a finite population correction factor in case the sample is more significant than 5% of the finite population.

The sample size should be at most 10% of the population count:

This condition is mainly applicable when we have sampled with replacement. The samples should be independent. That is the main reason we are keeping this rule. It is applicable when we have a finite population.

Z-Score analysis for SDSM

We already know that our SDSM is a normal distribution at any cost. We are confirming this from the central limit theorem. Therefore, we have normal or non-normal distribution; we will have a normal distribution for SDSM. The only requirement is sample size should be more significant than 30 for non-normal population distributions.

Suppose we meet all the SDSM conditions we have discussed before this section. In that case, we can use the sampling distribution of the sample mean to answer questions about the probability that any given sample mean will fall within some distance of the population mean. For these types of problems, we will need to use

![](Images/Image171115.png)

We examine the Z-score values to find the probability of the sample mean’s presence against the population mean. It just gives the valid probability range for us while analysing the population means.

### 13.9 Sampling Distribution of the Sample Proportion (SDSP)

As with the mean, proportion also gets calculated in the same way. It means that we can find the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP) in the same way that we have calculated the sampling distribution of the sampling mean (SDSM).

Here we are working on population proportion p instead of the population mean µ. To get this answer as the sampling distribution of the sampling mean (SDSM), we are working on the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP).

Sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP)

Usually, proportion means how many subjects in the survey meet some specific condition. For example, In the street, the statistical engineer found that 50 people liked mangos from 100 people crowd, and he took this data from the sampling survey. Sample proportion usually uses the below formula.

![](Images/Image171123.png)

Where ![](Images/Image171130.png)is the sample proportion, x is the number of people in the street, and n is the street’s sample size from where we have taken the survey. Here the entire country is the population in mango’s  
analysis.

Usually, after collecting the sample, as with the sample mean, sample proportions will fit under the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP).

We create the sampling distribution of the sample proportion (SDSP) when we take every possible sample from our population, calculate the sample proportion for each sample, and plot all of those proportions into a probability distribution.

Therefore, the SDSP is the probability distribution of all possible sample proportions ![](Images/Image171138.png).

Central limit theorem

In proportion, everything is binary—something like “whether this proportion succeeded or failed”. So here, we are not going to use the normal distribution. Instead, since this is discrete, we will use binomial distribution.

Even though the population proportion uses binomial distribution, our central limit theorem still works for samples. It means the samples will maintain the normal distribution in the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP). One condition is that the sample size should be more than 30.

Mean, variance, and standard deviation

The central limit theorem also tells us that the mean of all the sampling distributions of sample proportion will always be the same as the mean of the corresponding population. It means that

![](Images/Image171147.png)

The standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample proportion is ![](Images/Image171159.png). As with the mean’s standard error, we calculate the proportion’s standard error below. This standard error tells how bad or good this sample represents from the population. For example, 90% of people may not be interested in mangoes on some streets because many diabetic hospitals are there. When we compare the entire country, this sample is an extreme one in the normal distribution. This sample could be better compared to average proportions. So, the standard error must point out this badness.

![](Images/Image171168.png)

In the above definition, p is the population proportion, and ‘n’ is the sample size. We must use this formula only when we deal with an infinite population, and the sample is within 5% of the total finite population.

As usual, the variance also gets calculated like below.

![](Images/Image171176.png)

Whatever we discussed so far is about the known population. When we have a known population, we deal with the above formulas. However, as like mean, most of the time, we deal with the unknown population. Therefore, we can use sample proportion ![](Images/Image171185.png)directly for standard error and variance calculations. It will represent the statistics for the population proportion.

![](Images/Image171192.png)

![](Images/Image171199.png)

As with the mean topic, here, we also need to use the finite population correction factor when the population is finite and use a bigger sample size (More than 5% of the finite population). See below the formulas for this usage. Again, the standard error and variance use the finite population correction factor.

![](Images/Image171208.png)

![](Images/Image171216.png)

If the population proportion is unknown, we approximate it with the sample proportion, and our formulas for standard error and variance with the finite population correction factor are

![](Images/Image171223.png)

![](Images/Image171231.png)

Conditions for sampling distributions of the sample proportions (SDSP) inference

As with Sampling distributions of the sample mean (SDSM), the SDSP also gets satisfied with a few conditions to get an accurate analysis of population proportions.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample count should be more significant. The “np” count should be more than 5.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample size should be at most 10% of the population count.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The sample should be random.
    

The sampling distribution of the sample proportion will have perfection if np >=5 and n(1-p) >=5. So here, ‘n’ is the sample size, and ‘p’ is the population proportion.

If np ≥ 5 is true, it tells us that we can expect to have at least 5 “successes” in the sample, and if n (1 − p) ≥ 5 is true, it tells us that we can expect to have at least 5 “failures.

So, if our sample size is n = 1000 and the population proportion is p = 20 %, then we multiply 1000 by 0.2 and 1 − 0.2 = 0.8 to ensure both values are at least 5.

1000 ⋅ 0.8 = 800 > 5 and 10 ⋅ 0.2 = 200 > 5

The sample size should not cross above 10% of the population  
count.

This condition is mainly applicable when we have sampled with replacement. The samples should be independent. That is the main reason we are keeping this rule. It is applicable when we have a finite population.

Z-Score analysis for SDSP:

As with SDSM, if we meet all the SDSP conditions we have discussed just before this section, we can use the sampling distribution of the sample proportion to answer questions about the probability that any given sample proportion will fall within some distance of the population proportion. For these types of problems, we will need to use

![](Images/Image171241.png)

We examine the Z-score values to find the probability of the sample proportion’s presence against the population proportion. It just gives the valid probability range for us while analysing the population proportion.

### 13.10 The Student’s t-Distribution

![](Images/Image171252.png)

Figure 13.2 The student’s t-distribution

We have already seen that standard normal distribution is helping us a lot in many aspects. The standard normal distribution is also getting called z-distribution. It is symmetrical bell-shaped distribution, and we have mean µ and standard deviation σ.

We also saw that we need to use z-tables to get the percentile values using z-score values. The normal distribution also has a little standard deviation in the bell curve. By the Empirical rule, about 68 % of the area under the standard normal distribution is found between the z-scores z = − 1 and z = 1, about 95 % of the area resides between z = − 2 and z = 2, and about 99.7 % of the area resides is between z = − 3 and z = 3. Here Z-scores mean standard deviation.

As with z-distribution, we also have one more similar distribution named t-distribution. T-distribution is an excellent alternative to z-distribution. From many interesting cases, t-distribution will help us a lot. This section will look at all the features and benefits individually.

The student’s t-distribution is similar to the standard normal distribution (i.e., z – distribution) in that it is symmetrical, bell-shaped, and centered around the mean μ = 0.

However, the significant difference in t-distribution is the edges. However, it is flatter and wider. Wider means it never lies down. Slowly it got down due to the main reason. The standard deviation is profound. Both these properties help us to handle any data. Even with the small sample sizes, we can work with t-distribution (Even if n>=30).

More areas in T-distribution get speared in the tail part. Please refer to Figure 13.2 for the distribution split and the difference. Also, most of the time, we call this distribution a T-distribution.

Another exciting part of t-distribution is that we do not need to have only one generic distribution like Z-Distribution. We use the degrees of freedom concept here. It is n-1, where n is our sample size. This degree of freedom is getting used to changing the T-distribution’s broader pattern. If DOF is 1, the T-distribution’s more comprehensive range will be too deep. When the DOF is 30, the wider T-distribution will equal the Z-distribution. Slowly the broader curve will come to normal distribution based on the DOF (From 1-30). For all above 30 DOF, the T-distribution will get used as Z – distribution. Please refer to Figure 13.3 to understand T-distribution in deep. So, the t-distribution for a sample size n = 15 (with n − 1 = 15 − 1 = 14 degrees of freedom) will look different than the t-distribution for a sample of size n = 25 (with n − 1 = 25 − 1 = 24 degrees of freedom)

![](Images/Image171262.png)

Figure 13.3 The growing t-distribution

We must mainly use T-distribution when our sample size is less than 30. if we use a sample size n < 30, we should calculate a t-score and look up that t-score in a t-table instead of calculating a z-score and looking up that z-score in a z-table.

If n ≥ 30 → Find a z-score, look it up in the z-table

If n < 30 → Find a t-score and look it up in the t-table. As mentioned above, both T-distribution and Z-distribution will become equal after we use a sample size of more than 30.

Finding values in the t-table:

Looking up t-values in a t-table is similar to looking up z-values in a z-table. To find values in the t-table, we need to know the degrees of freedom (n − 1, when the sample size is n) and either the upper-tail probability or the confidence level.

![](Images/Image171270.png)

Figure 13.4 The t-distribution score table

### 13.11 Confidence Interval for the Mean

How confident we are with the sample mean and sample proportion values. Even though we use the central limit theorem and perfect probability-based lookup, we get a single-point outcome. It is because we usually follow all the pre-conditions and calculate using Z-tables and T-tables to gain confidence. However, as we mentioned before, a sample mean or sample proportion might be an excellent estimate of the population parameter or a terrible estimate. So, it would be beneficial to say how confident we are about how well the sample statistic estimates the population parameter. That is where confidence intervals come in.

Instead of the single point, we can calculate the confidence intervals for the population analysis; It means we can calculate the population means and population proportion with some confident interval range.

Usually, the point estimate for the population mean, μ, is the sample mean ![](Images/Image171281.png), and the point estimate for the population standard deviation, σ, is the sample standard deviation, s

Point estimation is easy to get calculated. Interval estimation is more complex than point estimation. However, this will give confidence. A point estimate does not tell us how good or bad the estimate is.

Interval confidence → Instead of saying that this is the population mean and proportion, we can confidently say the interval data, like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)“We are 98% confident with the population mean, which fits in the interval value (X, Y)”.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)“We are 99% confident with the population proportion, which fits the interval value (X, Y)”.
    

The confidence interval is the probability of the population parameters. It is most common to choose 99 %, 98 %, or 99.5 % as the confidence level and then find the interval associated with that confidence level.

Alpha α and the region of rejection: For example, we will get a 95% confidence level for our population calculation. It means that 5 % of the confidence intervals we find will not contain the population parameter. This 5 % (or 10 % for a 90 % confidence level, or 1 % for a 99 % confidence level) is called the alpha value, α. We also call α the significance level or the probability of making a Type I error (more on Type I and Type II errors later).

Therefore, α is always mentioned or calculated as a “1 − confidence level”. It means that the 1 – α confidence interval has a significant level of α.

We can visualize α as the total area under the normal distribution outside the confidence interval. For instance, given a 95 % confidence level, the alpha value is α = 1 − 0.95 α = 0.05.

Since the confidence interval is always surrounding near the mean of the normal distribution, we can show the centre 95 % of the distribution, with half of α in the lower tail to the left of the confidence interval and the other half of α in the upper tail to the right of the confidence interval. See below Figure 13.5 for this distribution.

![](Images/Image171288.png)

Figure 13.5 Example – Confidence interval for the mean

At a 90 % confidence level, we can expect the smallest 2.5 % and largest 2.5 % values to fall outside the confidence interval because α is split evenly into the upper and lower tails, and α/2 = 0.05/2 = 0.025.

Using a z-table, the z-values associated with −0.025 and +0.025 are −1.96 and +1.96, respectively. It means the boundaries of the 95 % confidence interval are −zα/2 = − 1.95 and zα/2 = + 1.95.

After getting the above values, we can calculate the region of rejection. ±zα/2 are the boundaries of the region of rejection. For 95% confidence levels, the outside of ± 1.95 Z-values are getting the confidence.

The confidence interval when σ is known:

When population standard deviation σ is known, the confidence interval will be in the range of (X, Y), and the confidence interval can be calculated by

where (X, Y) is the confidence interval,

![](Images/Image171303.png)is the sample mean,

z\* is the critical value (which is the z-score for the confidence level we have chosen),

σ is the population standard deviation,

n is the sample size.

Since the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample mean (standard error) is

We also alter the formula like the one below.

![](Images/Image171318.png)

In the formulas mentioned above, sometimes we can use zα/2 Instead of Z\*. The sample consistently gives the confidence interval mean![](Images/Image171303.png), plus or minus the margin of error, so the margin of error is

When σ is unknown and/or we have a small sample When σ is unknown: We will use t-distribution instead of z-distribution. The below formula can be used for these lower sampling values.

## Statistical Methods: Hypothesis Testing

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 14.1 Inferential Statistics and Hypotheses

Whatever we have seen in statistics, all are related to solutions. However, before the solutions, we need to define the requirements. Isn’t it?

We are discussing it in reverse. We have discussed the solutions so far. However, the query matters a lot. We need to toss one statement about the population and verify whether this statement is true. That is the process. To prove that statement, we need to pick some samples from the population, prepare the statistics and conclude whether the statement is valid for our population.

For example, we are tossing this statement “40% middle age men in India have a beard”. First, it is just a statement. To confirm this statement, we cannot survey the entire population. So, we go for samples. Samples will teach us some probabilities, and finally, we will analyse the statement against sample statistics.

This entire process is called hypothesis testing. From now on, we will see about this hypothesis testing in detail.

There are five steps for hypothesis testing:

1.  State the null and alternative hypotheses.
2.  Determine the level of significance.
3.  Calculate the test statistic.
4.  Find critical values and determine the regions of acceptance and rejection.
5.  State the conclusion

We call this process inferential statistics because we use the information about the sample to make inferences about the population.

If we revisit our example, which we have mentioned above, “40% middle age men in India have beards” is the hypothesis statement. We could take random men for a survey and look at their appearance (whether they have a beard or not). If the percentage in middle age beard category comes to around 39%, then build a confidence interval to state how confident we are with this sample, which produced ![](Images/Image171386.png)\= 0.39, supports the claim that p = 0.40

Proving the claim is different from providing support. We always need to figure out sample statistics data. We are just providing some data to support the statement. The confidence interval gives little confidence about the data. That is why we call this hypothesis testing.

### 14.2 Hypothesis

In the hypothesis world of statistics, we have two opposing statements. One is the Null hypothesis, and the other is the Alternative hypothesis. We denote the Null hypothesis as H0 and the alternative hypothesis as H1 (Some people call it Ha)

Here alternate hypothesis means abnormal to general belief. For example, people generally believe that 80% of students study well in that school. Suppose we toss the alternative hypothesis that the school students are not studying well and the good students are 80%. In that case, everyone will shock because we are tossing up new, unusual statements. (When Galileo mentioned that the earth was spherical, everyone was shocked).

The alternative to alternate hypotheses is the null hypothesis. – Which is the current one or which everyone believes!

For example, let us say the good student ratio is below 80% in that school. Then, the null hypothesis will be “80% or Above 80% of students are studying well”.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H1: The excellent student ratio is below 80% in that school
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ho: 80% or Above 80% of students are studying well
    

We always test the null hypothesis H0, not the alternative hypothesis H1. If our sample gives us enough evidence, we can reject the null hypothesis and provide evidence supporting our alternative hypothesis.

We will focus on hypothesis tests about two population parameters:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The population mean μ
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The population proportion p
    

### 14.3 The Population Mean μ

It is straightforward. If we discuss population, we can also talk about its average value. The average life span of a human being is 82. Suppose we provide the alternate hypotheses statement that the average life span of a human being is below 82. In that case, the null hypothesis will be

“Ho: 82”.

Alternative or null hypotheses can claim the rules with any of the below values.

≤, =, or ≥

The null hypothesis always says that the population mean (or parameter) is normal; nothing new or different is happening.

Here, we must understand that the alternative and null hypotheses are opposite, so if one hypothesis chooses one of the below operators, the corresponding opposite operator needs to be assessed.

| Operator Selection by Alternative Hypothesis || Null Hypothesis Must Choose the Opponent |
| --- | --- |
| ≤ || \> |
| \= || < and > |
| ≥ || < |
| \> || ≤ |
| < || ≥ |
| < and > || \= |

Table 14.1 The Operators of Hypothesis

### 14.4 For Population Proportions

Same thing as mean. Population proportions will get handled instead of mean. If 40% (0.40) of men are interested in beards, the alternative hypothesis may toss the below statement.

“It is not accurate to 40%. It will be below 40% or above 40%.”

This example comes under the “< and >” category of the operator. Just see below Table 14.2 with the highlighted row. Here Null hypotheses must take “=” as the operator. (To make it opposite for < and >)

H0 = “Exactly 40% men is interest in beard”

| Operator Selection by Alternative Hypothesis || Null Hypothesis Must Choose the Opponent |
| --- | --- |
| ≤ || \> |
| \= || < and > |
| ≥ || < |
| \> || ≤ |
| < || ≥ |
| < and > || \= |

Table 14.2 The Operators of Hypothesis

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If H0 is μ = or p =, then H1 is μ ≠ or p ≠
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If H0 is μ ≤ or p ≤, then H1 is μ > or p >
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If H0 is μ ≥ or p ≥, then H1 is μ < or p <
    

### 14.5 Significance Level and Type I and II Errors

As discussed, we need to match the population with our collected samples. Therefore, we will have lots of deviations. Even if the sample was random, it might need to be more representative.

We may face two types of errors when we select the samples for our hypothesis testing.

Let us take our example with null hypotheses statement, like the one below. Then, the samples will not satisfy the needs when we handle our samples.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H0 = “Exactly 40% men is interest in beard”
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H1 = “It is not accurate to 40%. It will be below 40% or above 40%.”
    

When we collect the samples for the above hypotheses analysis, imagine we found that the null hypotheses are wrong from the samples. So, we rejected the null hypothesis. However, in population, this Null hypothesis is true. We call this mismatch or error a Type 1 Error. The probability of making a Type 1 error is α. It is also getting called a “Level of significance.”

Type I error: Supporting the alternative hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.

Now we can imagine that we found that null hypotheses are suitable from samples. So, we accepted the null hypothesis. However, in population, this Null hypothesis is false. We call this mismatch or error a Type 2 Error. The probability of making a Type 2 error is β.

Type 2 error: Not supporting the alternative hypothesis when the null hypothesis is false.

|  || The Null Hypothesis (H0) is TRUE || The Null Hypothesis (H0) is FALSE |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Reject the Null Hypothesis (H0) || Type 1 Error – Alpha || Correct |
| Accepted the Null Hypothesis (H0) || Correct || Type 2 Error – Beta |

Table 14.3 Type 1 and Type 2 errors of hypothesis

So here, why we call Type 1 for the first case means the severity is very high when we reject the null hypothesis for the valid one.

Thinking about Type I and Type II errors can get people a little twisted around sometimes, so if we find one description that makes more sense to us than the others, we can stick with that one. Because α is the probability of making a Type I error, and β is the probability of making a Type II error, we can say that the alpha level is the probability of making the wrong decision when the null hypothesis is true or the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis when it is true, or the probability of making a Type I error. Here the beta level is the probability of making the wrong decision when the null hypothesis is false, the probability of accepting the null hypothesis when it is false, or the probability of making a Type II error.

Since the probability of committing a Type I error is α, the probability of making a correct decision when H0 is true is 1 − α.

Oppositely, the probability of committing a Type II error is β, and the probability of making a correct decision when H0 is false is 1 – β.

It provides us good power if we perform our work correctly without issues like rejecting the H0 when it is false. It equals 1 – β

Confidence levels and the α value: The α value is also called the significance level. It is the same α value we discussed in the previous sections (during sampling). Also, the confidence levels and confidence intervals, which we have discussed in the sampling section, will fit here. Usually, we pick the confidence values as 90 %, 95 %, or 99 %.

For these confidence values, the α values will be something like this.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)At 90 % confidence, the alpha value is α = 1 − 90 % = 10 %
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)At 95 % confidence, the alpha value is α = 1 − 95 % = 5 %
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)At 99 % confidence, the alpha value is α = 1 − 99 % = 1 %
    

In reality, we need to pick the corresponding alpha values for the corresponding confidence values. When we decide on α, we decide how much we want to risk committing a Type I error. In other words, if we choose α = 0.05, we are saying that 5 % of the time, or 1 out of 20 times, we will reject the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is true.

If we choose the significant level of 1%, we should have more confidence compared to α = 10 %.

α is the probability of making a Type I error, and β is the probability of making a Type II error. So, our target is to reduce both these error values. In addition, we want to reduce the possibilities for errors or probabilities for errors.

Here the contradiction is this. When we decrease α values, we make it difficult to reject null hypotheses. However, on the other hand, when we make it false in null hypothesis rejection, the type 2 error risk will be high (β gets more prominent). It is the contradiction we need to face.

Here the alternative contradiction is this. When we increase α values, we make our null hypothesis rejection easier. We are at risk for Type 1 errors.

So, it is something like this. If we decrease the probability of Type 1 error, we risk Type 2. If we decrease the probability of Type 2 error, we risk Type 1.

### 14.6 Test Statistics for One- and Two-Tailed Tests

Recalling our hypothesis testing steps one more time.

1.  State the null and alternative hypotheses.
2.  Determine the level of significance.
3.  Calculate the test statistic.
4.  Find critical values and determine the regions of acceptance and rejection.
5.  State the conclusion

So far, we have done the first two steps. First, we have tossed the statements and created the Null and alternative hypotheses. Then we have determined the level of significance.

The third step is calculating the test statistics.

Here we need to recall our operator table for reference.

| Operator Selection by Alternative Hypothesis || Null Hypothesis Must Choose the Opponent || Test Statistic Method |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ≤ || \> || One tail test |
| \= || < and > || Two tail tests |
| ≥ || < || One tail test |
| \> || ≤ || One tail test |
| < || ≥ || One tail test |
| < and > || \= || Two tail tests |

Table 14.4 Test statistics for one- and two-tailed tests

If we refer to the above table, it is easy to understand. When we have the “=” operator, the opponent hypothesis must conduct the test on two sides. One for the “<” operator and the other one for the”>” operator. That is why we call these two-tail tests. Otherwise, for all other operators, one tail test is sufficient.

The two-tail region will look like below.

![](Images/Image171569.png)

Figure 14.1 Two Tails – Region of Rejection

We call it a “two-tailed test” because we have two regions of rejection, one in each tail. Since we are dealing with the “=” operator, the nonrejection region for the null hypothesis will go in the middle. (Refer to Figure 14.1). On the other hand, the region of rejection will go in two extreme tails because we reserve this for alternative “< and >” operators.

One tail test will get represented like below. Figure 14.2 depicts the lower tail test, and the upper tail test is specified in Figure 14.3. Here the lower tail test is called the left-tailed test, and we work on this test for the operator “>=” Null hypothesis. The null hypothesis is always a more significant number (Example: more than or equal to 10%). We also call the upper tail test a right-tailed test, and we work on this test for the operator “<=” Null hypothesis. The null hypothesis is always a lower number (For example: less than or equal to 10%).

![](Images/Image171577.png)

Figure 14.2 One Tail \[Left\] – Lower tail – Region of rejection

![](Images/Image171584.png)

Figure 14.3 One Tail \[Right\] – Upper tail – Region of rejection

### 14.7 Choosing a One-Tailed or Two-Tailed Test

One tail or two test tests are getting decided by the operator selection. Here while choosing the operators and statements in hypothesis creation, we need to consider the type of test too. Here, one tail test will have a larger rejection region than two-tail tests for the same data. It is happening because the two tails divide the region of rejection into two ends. Please refer to Figure 14.4. It says that for the same significance level, the region for rejection shirks in two tail tests. It means that a two-tailed test is always more conservative than a one-tailed one. Looking at this Figure 14.4, we could get a whole range of results that fall within the rejection region of the one-tailed test but fail to reach the rejection region of the two-tailed test.

![](Images/Image171592.png)

Figure 14.4 One-tail and two-tailed tests comparison

### 14.8 The Α Value for One- and Two-Tailed Tests

Imagine that we are running two different tests. One is two-tailed, and the other is one-tailed. If we set the significance level at α = 0.05, then in the two-tailed test, we will split that 5 % evenly into two tails, 2.5 % in the lower tail and 2.5 % in the upper tail.

![](Images/Image171601.png)

Figure 14.5 α values for two-tailed tests

Here z-score lookup needs to be done for two extreme ends. The 5% Alpha value will be divided into two points (2.5 %). On the left side, the z-score needs to be looked for 0.025, and the right-side z-score needs to be looked for 0.975. Please refer to Figure 14.6 for this split.

![](Images/Image171612.png)

Figure 14.6 α values and Z-score values for two-tailed tests

If we look into the one-tail test, the z-score value lookup will not get divided into two ranges. Please refer to below Figure 14.7 for these details.

![](Images/Image171621.png)

Figure 14.7 Z-score values for the one-tailed test

### 14.9 Calculating the Test Statistic

The next step is always to calculate the test statistic and then determine whether that value lies inside or outside the region of rejection. The formula will be like the one below.

Test statistic = Observed values – Expected values / Standard deviation

We need to recall our sampling section here. When σ is known, the formula will be

![](Images/Image171629.png)

When σ is unknown, and/or if we are using a small sample n < 30, we will use the sample standard deviation ![](Images/Image171638.png)instead of the population standard deviation σ, but we will use the more conservative t-table to compensate for using ![](Images/Image171638.png).

![](Images/Image171652.png)

These are test statistics for the mean, but the statistic for the proportion will look below. Here ![](Images/Image171660.png)value should be >=5 and ![](Images/Image171668.png)\>= 5

![](Images/Image171675.png)

### 14.10 The p-Value and Rejecting the Null

The P-value in the minor significance level: We can reject the null hypothesis at this level. We assume that we are in a genuine null hypothesis case. The P-value is the total area of the region of rejection. Also, in one tail test, the region of rejection is consolidated into one tail. In the two-tail tests, the region of rejection is consolidated into two tails. Therefore, we divide the region of rejection into two tails. Calculating the p-value as the area of the rejection region will be slightly different depending on whether we are using a one-tailed or two-tailed test and whether the one-tailed test is upper-tailed or lower-tailed.

Calculating the p-value for a one-tailed

For a one-tailed test, we will first calculate the z-test statistic. For a lower-tailed test, z will be negative. For an upper-tailed test, z will be positive. Then the value we find from the negative z-table represents the area under the probability distribution to the left of the negative z-value in the lower-tailed test. The value we find from the positive z-table represents the area under the probability distribution to the right of the positive z-value in the upper-tailed test. For instance, let us assume we calculated a test statistic of z = 1.96. Then, in a z-table, we find like below.

![](Images/Image171683.png)

Figure 14.8 Z-score value table

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)So, 0.9750 is the value for z = 1.96. Therefore, we should calculate the p-value in two ways for the lower-tailed and upper-tailed tests.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)In the lower-tailed test, it is the direct Z-score value will be considered a P value
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)For example, we get 0.0721 for z=-1,46 in the lower tail test. So, the P value will be 0.0721.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)However, for upper tail tests, we need to calculate the P values by separating the value from 1.
    

From the above, the z-score value is 0.9279. Here the P value will be calculated as

1-0.9279 = 0.0721

So, 0.0721 is the area under the curve to the right of z = 1.46; this is the p-value. So, p = 0.0721.

For a two-tailed test:

For a two-tailed test, we will first calculate the z-test statistic. For a two-tailed test, z could be either positive or negative. Then the value we find from the z-table represents the area under the probability distribution to the left of the z-value.

Rejecting the null using the p-value:

P-value will help us to make quick decisions on null hypothesis rejection. Whether or not we should reject the null hypothesis, the relationship between the α level and the p-value can determine H0. If p ≤ α, reject the null hypothesis. If p > α, do not reject the null hypothesis.

In our earlier examples, we found p = 0.0721 for the lower-tailed one-tailed test, p = 0.0721 for the upper-tailed one-tailed test p = 0.2186 for the two-tailed test. With these in mind, let us say, for instance, we set the confidence level of our hypothesis test at 90 %, which is the same as setting the α level at α = 0.10. In that case, p = 0.0721 ≤ α = 0.10 p = 0.2186 > α = 0.10.

Therefore, we would have rejected the null hypothesis for both one-tailed tests but failed to reject the null hypothesis in the two-tailed test. If, however, we had picked a more rigorous α = 0.05 or α = 0.01, we would have failed to reject the null hypothesis every time. So, to summarize the p-value approach when σ is known,

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Lower-tailed test → Reject H0 when p ≤ α
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Upper-tailed test →Reject H0 when p ≤ α
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Two-tailed test → Reject H0 when p ≤ α
    

Lastly, remember that this entire p-value approach applies in precisely the same way, whether we are using a z-test statistic or a t-test statistic. So, when our test statistic is a t-value, we look up the t-value in the t-table. For a lower-tailed test, the value we find becomes the p-value; for an upper-tailed test, we subtract the value from 1 to get the p-value; and for a two-tailed test, we double the value we find to get the p-value. Then we use the same rules as the z-test statistic to compare p to α to determine whether or not to reject the null hypothesis.

Rejecting the null using the critical value:

We can decide the critical values when we decide about the null hypothesis instead of p-values selection (Remember that both are options to us; p-values and critical values). Critical values are like the cuts of values that need in the rejection region. The critical values are purely depending on the test statistic itself. Z or t and the level of significance α.

### 14.11 Significance

The significance of a test is the probability of obtaining the result by chance. The less likely we obtained a result by chance, the more significant our results are. Hopefully, by now, it is not too surprising that all of these are equivalent statements,

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The confidence level is 90 %
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The Type I error rate is 0.10
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The alpha level is 0.10, α = 0.10
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The area of the rejection region is 0.10
    

As like above, we have one more group with p values

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The finding is significant at the 0.10 level
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)The p-value is 0.10, p = 0.10
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)There is a 10 in 100 chance of getting a result as extreme.
    

### 14.12 Hypothesis Testing for the Population Proportion

As like population means, we can work on population proportion hypothesis testing. We want the number of successes and failures to be at least five because that threshold means that the probability distribution will approximate the normal curve. Sample proportion will be specified here as ![](Images/Image171692.png). P value also represents ‘P”.

One-tailed test: Just like for the mean, a one-tailed test for the proportion indicates directionality, so our hypothesis statements will either be

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H0: p ≤ <Constant value >
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H1: p > <Constant value >
    

if we suspect that p > <Constant value >, or

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H0: p ≥ <Constant value >
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)H1: p < <Constant value >
    

Two-tailed test: As with the mean, we will split the data into two tails.

## Machine Learning: Introduction

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 15.1 Machine Learning

We have studied artificial intelligence earlier part of this book. Intelligent agents help human beings by serving intelligence in various cases, like the brain’s intelligence power. Machine Learning is one of the scientific disciplines of Artificial Intelligence. The name itself implies that a machine learns something. That something is data. That is why we have studied statistics.

Machine Learning learns data. What is the purpose of this? What is remarkable in Machine learning? Machine Learning helps us predict the data model based on the learning points. It means we did not program anything explicitly and asked machine learning to follow the program’s rules. We instruct machine learning to learn the pattern of the data and keep the learning pattern with us (In terminally, we call the learning pattern weights). Whenever required, we predict the pattern for the new data with our learned intelligence.

![](Images/Image171703.png)

Figure 15.1 Machine Learning

Is it too complex to understand? Do not worry. We will see a few examples below.

Example 1: Machine Learning learns the past 365 days’ gold price \[Day wise price change\]. Machine Learning can predict Tomorrow’s gold price based on the learning pattern.

This example comes under the forecasting part of machine learning.

Example 2: We know the house rent trend for the last ten years. It keeps increasing, and it is linearly increasing. What will be the new rent for next year? Machine Learning will predict the rent based on the learning pattern.

This example comes under the linear regression algorithm of machine learning.

Example 3: In the agriculture form, the new employee needs clarification about classifying fruits, apples, and pears. Initial days, he was unable to classify the fruits correctly. Slowly he starts leaning by labels. He checks the labelled bags and learns that apples will be bigger than pears. He learned this with the help of many labelled bags. Finally, his accuracy is perfect for classifying the fruits. Similarly, Machine learning also learns the difference between an apple and a pear with the help of labelled data sets. Machine learning gets the accuracy or perfection based on the number of labelled data it has learned.

This example comes under the classification algorithm of machine learning.

Example 4: In the agriculture form, giant rats are destroying plants. Machine Learning based cameras must identify the rats and kill them. However, although the images initially show rats, Machine learning cannot identify whether the rat is in the image because rats appear in different sizes in images. Also, rats have mud on their faces. So, machine learning learns the rat’s appearance with the help of 10,000 images with labels (whether the rat is there or not). Then the accuracy comes appropriately.

This example comes under the computer vision algorithm of machine learning.

Example 5: Indian government asked machine learning to read all the daily newspapers and classify the news whether it is against the government or not. Machine learning was initially unable to identify with the order of English or other Indian language words. However, the accuracy comes appropriately after learning with the help of labelled 10,000 news articles. It knows which English words and the order of English words talk about government.

This example comes under the Natural language Processing algorithm of machine learning.

Likewise, Machine learning is helping a lot in the contemporary world. A few others are specified below.

### 15.2 Types of Machine Learning

We have many machine-learning algorithms, which we will see in the below section.

Supervised Learning: We provided labels to machine learning algorithms and taught machine learning by specifying the desired outcomes. The five examples mentioned above in the previous topics come under supervised learning. It is simple. The engineer provides everything to the machine in the learning stage, like data, labels, how to get it, and how to identify this.

Unsupervised Learning: When the machine learning algorithms try to learn new labels or experiment in a new area, the engineer’s supervision is invalid. Say Machine learning identifies a new planet in the sky and gives the name for the new planet under a new classification. The engineer needs to mention the desired outcomes in these learning algorithms. We are going to discuss these algorithms more in Chapter 24.

Semi-supervised Learning: The engineer provides some desired outcomes. Sometimes Machine learning also identifies a new outcome that does not fit the abovementioned category. For example, we teach about all the fruits through computer vision with the help of proper labels. In the actual workspace, the machine learning identifies the new type of fruit which did not learn so far, and it learns that fruit with a new label. This kind of algorithm comes under semi-supervised learning.

Reinforcement Learning: Most of the time, machine learning is supposed to learn from mistakes or successes. These self-analysis algorithms come under reinforcement learning. The sequence of actions will get performed here.

### 15.4 Training, Validation, and Testing Data Sets

Machine learning engineers usually classify the available data set into three parts in the learning stage. Those are

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Training set
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Validating set
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Testing set
    

For example, when we have 10000 images in the agriculture farm to learn about the rat in the images, The engineer can split them by 60%: 20%: 20% ratio. So that we consider 6000 images as a training set, 2000 images will be considered for validation, and 2000 images for the testing set.

What is the function of these sets?

Training set:

Machine learning initially learns the images one by one from the training set and creates the pattern (Weights) for the learned items. For example, say if the colour is white for the animal, it could be a cat. Training sets a significant purpose is to learn from the given labels.

Validation set:

Most of the time, machine learning needs to change the pattern weights and check whether these new weights correctly classify the data. So, we need one testing activity within the learning stage. These are validation sets. Changing weights and checking the accuracy comes under hyperparameter tuning activity. We will see what these hyperparameters are later part of the book. However, now, remember that many parameters within machine learning decide the learning patterns.

Testing set:

Finally, machine learning needs to test and measure the outcomes after finishing the training and tuning. Here it uses the training set. It is essential because it will give us the analysis of the results, like the accuracy and prediction error. These training sets should differ from the training set to get the accuracy of the test results. For example, we can test one image in which a rat is super tiny (a newborn rat). Imagine that we did not give these kinds of tiny rats to machine learning in the training phase. This test aims to measure the accuracy of the prediction weights against all varieties of data.
