## Machine Learning: Data Workflow and Data Mining

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

In this chapter, we are going to discuss data. However, before we enter into the machine learning algorithms, we need to know the properties of data.

We need to conduct the data pre-processing activities before executing machine learning algorithms. Moreover, we need to cover pre-processing activity in this chapter.

We will also cover the data performance metrics for all the machine learning outcomes, which is essential.

### 16.1 Definition of Data

Data means information with attributes and also the way we arrange.

Attribute means properties of the data.

Example: If the data is one cat, the attributes of the cat will be like the cat’s size, height of the cat, and health condition of the cat.

Sample data: Name: Red cat 1234, Size: 10 KG; Height: 5 Inches; Health Status: Healthy

The object is the collection of many attributes (data sets).

Example: 18 cat records from cat farm generated the cat table

Sample data:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Name: Red cat 1231, Size: 11 KG; Height: 6 Inches; Health Status: Weak
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Name: Red cat 1232, Size: 12 KG; Height: 4 Inches; Health Status: Unhealthy
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Name: Red cat 1233, Size: 13 KG; Height: 3 Inches; Health Status: Bad
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Name: Red cat 1234, Size: 10 KG; Height: 5 Inches; Health Status: Healthy
    

Attribute values are numbers or symbols assigned to an attribute for a particular object.

Example: We measure the weight of the cat in KG’s (Decimal values)

### 16.2 Types of Attributes

There are different types of attributes are there. We can discuss all the attribute types here in this section.

Nominal: If we have individual identification for all the data, these are called nominal. For example, we can name the cats on an agriculture farm something like this: cat12355, cat134567, cat34.

Ordinal: The ordinal attribute value provides sufficient information to order the objects. Such as Rankings, Grades, and Weight categories. For example, we group the cat’s health status as “Healthy,” Bad,” Weak,” and Unhealthy.”

Interval: We measure it on a scale of equal size units; these attributes allow us to compare, such as temperature in C or F, and thus, values of attributes have been ordered. Always earth’s temperature falls in between some limits. However, it never starts from 0 and maintains within some interval.

Ratio: Both differences and scaling are significant for ratio—for example, age, length, and weight. We compare the age between people and animals. For example, one cat12345 is double in age compared to cat13456.

The type of an attribute depends on which of the following properties/operations it possesses:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Distinctness: = ≠
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Order: < >
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Differences: + –
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ratios: \* /
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Nominal attribute: distinctness
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ordinal attribute: distinctness & order
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Interval attribute: distinctness, order & differences
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Ratio attribute: all four properties/operations
    

### 16.3 Discrete and Continuous Attributes

We can revisit the concept we discussed in this book’s discrete random variables section of the statistics chapter.

Discrete attributes can only take on discrete values.

What is discrete?

Any fixed set is discrete. The opposite of discrete is continuous. We cannot say that the finite option is continuous. The accurate height of a human being can be anything like the one below.

“5.1 feet,  
5.111 feet,  
5.11111 feet,  
5.111111 feet,  
5.11111111 feet,  
5.111111111 feet”

Do we see the standard height in the above example? That is not correct. From accuracy, we can go any deep in height. When we say all the class members have a range of heights between 5 and 6.2, this is called a continuous dataset. So, we cannot make finite the options continuously.

Opposite to this, discrete will have an finite set. So, it can have either 1000 data items or 2 trillion data items. However, it is  
finite.

For example, if we flip a coin twice, we can only get heads zero times, one time, or two times. We cannot get heads 1.5 times or 0.31 times. The number of heads we can get takes on a discrete set of values: 0, 1, and 2.

### 16.4 Characteristics of Data

Dimensionality (number of attributes):

In what dimension we need to classify the data is called dimensionality. For example, we can classify the cats in the following ways.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)One dimension – Just the name of the cats.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Two dimensions – A cat name with health status can classify the cats into four categories.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Three dimensions – A cat’s name, health status, and age classify three dimensions. Therefore, it will classify the cats into multiple categories.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Four dimensions – A cat’s name, health status, age, and sex classify four dimensions. Therefore, it has enormous data categories.
    

Sparsity:

Data sparsity is a term used for how much data we have for a particular dimension/entity of the model.

Tables and databases are the totals of their sparse and dense cells.

A common problem in machine learning is sparse data, which alters machine learning algorithms’ performance and ability to calculate accurate predictions. Data is sparse when specific expected values in a dataset are missing, which is a common phenomenon in general large scaled data analysis.

For example, 98% of the male cats are in the cat form. Therefore, the training data may fit the machine-learning weights only for male cats. It will have a problem when it gets the female cat in  
production.

Resolution:

Patterns depend on the scale. For example, the cat’s age is in number, and the cat’s height is in inches. Therefore, we must match the resolution when we analyse the two-dimension data.

Size:

The type of analysis may depend on the size of the data

### 16.5 Outliers

We have already seen outliers in the statistics chapter. Therefore, we can revisit the details one more time. Outliers are data objects with characteristics considerably different from most other data objects in the data set.

Usage of identifying outliers:

Outliers are noise in data analysis. We should ignore it before moving into machine learning algorithms.

Outliers are the goal of our machine learning analysis. The output itself is an outlier.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Example: Credit card fraud and Intrusion detection

### 16.6 Data Quality Problems

Other than outliers, we have many other data quality problems we need to handle.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Wrong data
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Fake data
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Missing values
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Duplicate data
    

### 16.7 Data Pre-Processing

Aggregation – Combining two or more attributes (or objects) into a single attribute (or object). It will reduce the dimensions. Unnecessary data combinations in dimension are not required.

Sampling – Sampling is the primary technique employed for data reduction. It often uses for the preliminary investigation of the data and the final data analysis. We can pick samples of data instead of the entire population. We saw this in deep when we were discussing sampling in statistics.

Discretization and Binarization – Discretization converts a continuous attribute into an ordinal one. It means we can divide the continuous data into multiple categories. Binarization maps a continuous or categorical attribute into one or more binary variables.

Attribute Transformation – An attribute transform is a function that maps the entire set of values of a given attribute to a new set of replacement values such that we identify all the old values with one of the new values. Instead of data X, we can modify the data into a log(X), Square(X).

Dimensionality Reduction – We can directly use the mathematical concept named principal component analysis to reduce dimensionality. For example, it may help to eliminate irrelevant features or reduce noise. One more concept name is singular value decomposition in mathematics which uses this for dimensionality reduction.

Feature subset selection – Here, we remove the irrelevant features.

Feature creation – Create new attributes that can capture the critical information in a data set much more efficiently than the original attributes

### 16.8 Confusion Matrix – Performance Evaluation

A confusion matrix is an N X N matrix used to evaluate the performance of a classification machine learning model, where N is the number of target classes. It compares the actual target values against the ones predicted by the machine learning model. As a result, it provides a holistic view of how a classification model will work and the errors it will face.

Running a classification model produces two outcomes:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Binary 0
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Binary 1
    

where 0 means False and 1 means True.

Based on these two outcomes, we can easily compare the resulting classification outcomes with the actual values of the observation. It helps in judging the performance of a classification model. The matrix used in these outcomes is known as the confusion matrix.

Here is how it works:

![](Images/Image171743.png)

Figure 16.1 Confusion matrix

Let us see the above outcomes in detail.

There are four potential outcomes:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)True positive
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)True negative
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)False positive
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)False negative
    

True positive (TP) is the number of authentic results when the actual observation is positive.

False positive (FP) is the number of incorrect predictions when the actual observation is positive.

True negative (TN) is the number of accurate predictions when the observation is negative.

False negative (FN) is the number of incorrect predictions when the observation is negative.

We already know that we use a confusion matrix to measure the performance indicators for classification models. Let us now understand the four main parameters that play a vital role.

Precision

Precision is the analysis of the true positives over the number of total positives predicted by the machine learning model. We write the formula for precision as TP/(TP+FP). This indicator allows us to calculate the rate at which our positive predictions are optimistic.

Accuracy

Accuracy is the most commonly used parameter to judge the machine learning model. For example, 70% of cases are false, and only 30% are accurate. In that case, there is a high possibility of the ML model having an accuracy score of 70%. Therefore, the formula to calculate accuracy is (TP+TN)/(TP+FP+FN+TN).

Recall

Sensitivity or recall is the measure of the TP over the count of the actual positive outcomes. The formula to calculate Recall is TP/(TP+FN). This parameter assesses how well the ML model can analyse the input and identify the result.

F1 score

The harmonic mean of precision and recall is the F1 score. We use this as an overall indicator that incorporates both precision and recall. These harmonic mean analyses false positives and false negatives and performs well on an imbalanced dataset. The formula to calculate it is 2(p\*r)/(p+r), where r is the recall and p is precision.

### 16.9 Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve

The ROC curve is an evaluation metric for binary classification problems. It is a probability curve that plots the True Positive Rate (TPR) against False Positive Rate (FPR) at various threshold values and essentially separates the ‘signal’ from the ‘noise.’

What is ROC?

As mentioned above, the ROC curve is the plot between TPR and FPR. In other words, it is a graph between sensitivity and (1-Specificity). In the ROC curve, a higher X-axis value indicates a higher number of False positives than True negatives. In contrast, a higher Y-axis value indicates more True positives than False negatives.

### 16.10 Dealing with Imbalanced Classes

Class imbalance is a problem that occurs in machine learning classification problems. It merely tells that the target class frequency is highly imbalanced, i.e., the occurrence of one of the classes is very high compared to the other classes present. In other words, there is a bias or skewness towards the majority class present in the target. For example, suppose we consider a binary classification where the majority target class has 10000 rows, and the minority target class has only 100 rows. In that case, the ratio is 100:1, i.e., for every 100-majority class, there is only one minority class present. This problem is what we refer to as class imbalance. Some general areas where we can find such data are fraud detection, churn prediction, medical diagnosis, and email classification.

### 16.11 Challenges of Machine Learning

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Insufficient training data
    
    ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Sometimes, the given training data needs to be sufficient. For example, more than 10000 images require here to identify all the varieties of rats in agriculture farm.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Model Selection
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Overfitting
    
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)Overfitting leads to high performance in the training set but performs poorly on new data.
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)The reason is simple. We have trained the data too deeply. For example, if one rat has blue eyes, we model the pattern with blue eyes. It is an outlier. The variance of rats is very high. We addressed all categories. However, it is good in training. It will not work in new data. Most of the time, we need to trade off our performance. We would have missed something if we had ignored the blue-eyed rat. However, we will have some clarity in design.
        
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Underfitting
    
    ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)It is a too simple model, and it did not learn any complex parts of the model. For example, say we have provided only a few rat photos to machine learning, and it learned the rat description somehow based on the few images. However, when we provide the different categories of rat images, it will not identify because the learning needs to be improved.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Validation and Testing
    

If we keep the same varieties of test data (Biased data), the assessment will not work as expected.

### 16.12 Bias-Variance Trade-Off

It is the overall concept of the “Bias-Variance Trade-off.” Bias and variance are errors in the machine learning model. As we construct and train our machine learning model, we aim to reduce the errors as much as possible. In an ideal situation, we could reduce both Bias and Variance in a model to zero. However, if Bias were to decrease to zero, then variance would increase, and vice-versa. So, to optimize our model, we must find a balance between the two where we can decrease both Bias and Variance.

Bias is an error in the machine learning model. That error is the difference between the model’s predicted and actual values. In statistics, Bias is the tendency for underestimating or overestimating a parameter. Bias can come in many different forms, such as Sampling Bias or Selection Bias.

Variance is also an error in the machine learning model. The error this time is the model’s sensitivity to the data. If it is too sensitive, the model may see signals and patterns that are not there. Statistically, variance is the distance between each variable in the dataset and all the variables’ average or mean values. It measures the spread of the dataset and how far each variable is from the other.

Between Bias and Variance, which error should we reduce the most? Moreover, what happens if we choose to reduce one error over the other? An error is an error, so overall, having one or the other is not better.

If we were to prioritize the reduction of one error over the other, we would end up with a model that either overfits or underfits the data. As a result, the model will not provide usable results for any new data. In the case of underfitting, the model is unable to fit the training data and, as a result, will be unable to generalize new data. Overfitting will result in a model that models the training data too well, detecting patterns and noise in the data that are not there. It can negatively impact the results from the modelling of any new data.

Because of the effects of prioritizing the reduction of one error over the other, we must reduce both. It will lead us to get the best result. Refer the below Figure 16.2 for more details about the bias-variance trade-off.

![](Images/Image171751.png)

Figure 16.2 Bias-Variance Trade-Off

## Machine Learning: Linear Regression Models

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 17.1 Linear Regression

Any function which gets naturally linear for the given data set is called linear regression. Given the data, it matters a lot. That linearity naturally occurs for any function.

What is function all about?

Consider F(X) as a function deciding the Y value for any X values. We mention this as one-to-one regression.

F (X): X → Y

Where output Y is real.

![](Images/Image171759.png)

Figure 17.1 Linear Regression

Here X values refer to an independent dataset.

Example: If X refers to past years, Y is the average room rent in India. If, naturally, room rent values increased linearly, then we can say that room rent is naturally linear across years.

![](Images/Image171766.png)

Figure 17.2 Example: Linear Regression

What is the significant benefit of linear?

We can easily predict the next set of values based on the linear function in our current example. We can see that every year the room rent is increasing 500 INR (Indian Rupees) extra. So, F(X) = y will be (X – 1) + 500.

If we put the year as a number and start from 1960, We can get the formula below.

Here we can say F(X) = Y, and the Y value will be Y=500(X).

The Y function will decide the Y values for all the X values in linearity.

In Machine Learning, we can train the regression model by previous training sets of data like

{<X1, Y1>, <X2, Y2>, <X3, Y3>………. < Xn, Yn>}.

The Machine Learning model will automatically learn the function when we train the data.

### 17.2 Other Examples of Linear Regression

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Predict weight from gender, height, age
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Stock market’s Linear price movements.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Predict gold’s linear price change for the given demand requirements.
    

Like above, there is a massive set of examples for linear functions and regressions.

Is linear function strict to only one input?

No. In most cases, the input will have more varieties of data like age and gender, both of which decide heights in linear functions. So, we can get the linear regression from multiple data dimensions if that dataset fits the linear function.

| X1 || X2 || Y |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data 1\_1 || Data 2\_1 || Function decides the value |
| . || . || . |
| . || . || . |
| . || . || . |
| . || . || . |
| . || . || . |
| Data\_1\_N || Data\_2\_N || Function decides the value |

Table 17.1 Regression data table format

We can train our machine learning models via multi-data for function values. For example, the function F (X1, X2) learns from multiple data weights in multi-regression.

How does the linear regression model learn Function ‘Y’?

Linear regression can be expressed mathematically like below for the single input model (Single independent variable) and output Y (we can call this variable a dependent variable)

Y = (B0) + (B1) X + e

Here

Y = Dependent variable

X = Independent variable

B0 = Interface of the line

B1 = Linear regression coefficient (Slope of the line)

e = Random error

The last parameter, random error e, is required to get the best-fit line. We will discuss this ‘e’ later when we discuss regularization. Now understand that any linear regression in mathematics needs slope and intercept.

During our school days, we studied that any line will have slope and intercept, which decided the function for line (Y = MX + C). We are using this line concept only for regression.

Whatever we have seen above is the single input one (Single independent variable). We can also work on multi-linear regression using the below formula.

Y = E + (M1) X1 + (M2) X2 + (M3) X3 +………………. (Mn) Xn

Where M1, M2……Mn represents the coefficient values of the independent variables X1, X2, X3….Xn.

The machine learning algorithm aims to get the optimal coefficient values for the given linear data set. (Optimal values for M1, M2, M3…. Mn). In the entire machine learning phase, these values will get tuned for the best fit.

The final optimal model (Final optimal coefficient values) will give the best-fitting line.

### 17.3 How Exactly the Machine Learning Model Learns (Model Engine)

Consider the below example.

We are trying to train the dataset (1,1) (2,4) (3,9) (4,16) one by one to the regression machine learning model.

In the first step, the model learns the first dataset, X=1, and Y=1. So, it will learn the coefficient value M1 as 1.

Then it says that X=1; Y= X; That means F(X) = 1; Coefficient value M1 =1;

If we have only one training data, this is what it learns. Immediately if we test the model after this first data set, it will provide the wrong assumption. For the X value 2, it will say that the Y value is 2.

The model modifies the coefficient when we train the second set of training data (2,4). Now it changes the coefficient value from M to X2. Then it says that X=1; Y =X2; F(X) = X2; m=X Value2;

It is what happens. When we train more data, more change will happen to m values. It is because, in real time, people train the models with more data, like 100,000 data values in the data set.

One more problem comes when we train with massive training data ![emoojii](Images/emoojii.jpg). We have seen this in the previous chapter. An overfitting issue happens when we train tremendous data values.

### 17.4 Cost Function

In machine learning, we try to understand how well the current model is getting learned so far.

Is that model perfect at this moment for the given data set? The model provides answers to this question at one particular point. For example, most predictions were incorrect when we finished our first data set. So, we need to know about it.

As per the previous example (1,1) (2,4) (3,9) (4,16) (5,25), we want to validate the model after completing the training for the above data set. This model verification is getting calculated via the cost function.

Usually, we use validation data for this cost function.

The difference between the actual and predicted values is called the error.

i.e., Y Prediction – Y Actual. It is called loss function.

The average summation of all the loss function values is called the cost function. Usually, the machine learning model tries to reduce the minimal value for the cost function. That is the machine learning’s work in real-time. However, it never goes to zero. Instead, it tries to learn the global minimum.

Another critical point is that we must square the difference values to avoid zero-sum. We need to recall the statistics chapter for the variance calculation. Similarly, we use the squared function for the different values. The difference within multiple data points goes into two extreme values (– and +). We get the positive sum value when we square the difference values. So, the cost function will be like the below.

![](Images/Image171877.png)

The machine learning objective is to reduce this cost function value. So, minimize J. That is the main objective of any machine learning algorithm. It is also named a mean squared error (MSE) from a statistics point of view.

Here predicted value means the actual model value. We can call this

h θ (X(i))

for any single data (i) in the data set. This prediction is happening based on the learning model function.

The Machine Learning model tries to continuously reduce the cost function J(θ) to get the optimal value.

### 17.6 Optimization and Optimization Functions

From mathematics, we use gradient descent/ gradient accent concepts for optimization. However, mathematics also uses the same technique for optimization functions in various disciplines.

When we have a single independent variable in the machine learning model, we get gradient descent like the one below (Figure 17.5)

![](Images/Image171907.png)

Figure 17.5 Linear Optimization

Differential calculus helps us a lot in gradient descent. The convergence will help us to get it to optimal values for “m” coefficient values. Refer to Figure 17.5 for the differential calculus convergence, which finds the optimal m values against to cost value (J value).

We usually check the currently chosen m values performance by verifying the J cost value. We will have the minimized J value as optimal for all the respective m values.

Identifying the best optimal m values for the linear function is very simple. The concept behind this one is differential calculus. Differential calculus helps any function get into the curve’s deep point by taking the slope from differentiation.

In practice, we keep changing the m values slowly to understand the corresponding J value. J value will get reduced when we increase m values. At one particular point, the J value will start increasing. That is the optimal point. Here we may miss the deep dip when we jump with larger intervals. This concept is called the learning rate. We will have significant intervals and big jumps if we keep the high learning rate in gradient descent. It will not find the exact dip because of big jumps.

If we choose the learning rate with minor values, the convergence will take a considerable time. So, we need a trade-off in this. We have good learning rate algorithms which will choose a high learning rate in the initial stage and then reduce the learning rate in the later part of convergence.

This convergence also goes in a zig-zag in real time before reaching the optimal dip. Likewise, we have many concepts in gradient descent optimization functions, which we can refer this from the mathematics section.

### 17.7 Required Mathematics and Statistics for Machine Learning Linear Regression

Continuous optimization from mathematics:

Like the single variable regression concept, the multivariable regression also will use gradient descent. However, here it is in multi-dimensions. Most of the time, it will use convex optimization. Here we use partial differential calculations from mathematics. When we go for the partial differential calculations from multiple independent values, it will look like below.

![](Images/Image171915.png)

Figure 17.6 Linear Regression – Multivariable Optimization

As per Figure 17.6 above, the convergence will happen with the help of partial differential calculus. Partial differential calculus means two-dimensional (Or multi-dimension) differential calculus, keeping both variables below.

![](Images/Image171924.png)

Based on the dimension count, the partial differential calculus will expand it. For example, here in two-dimension, two m values need optimal values.

A few other real-time gradient descent algorithms are getting used

For example, stochastic Gradient Descent is a probabilistic approximation of Gradient Descent. It is an approximation because, at each step, the algorithm calculates the gradient for one data point picked at random instead of calculating the gradient for the entire dataset.

In Batch Gradient Descent, all the training data is taken into consideration to take a single step. We take the average of the training examples’ gradients and then use that mean gradient to update our parameters. So, that is just one step of gradient descent in one epoch. Batch Gradient Descent is great for convex or relatively smooth error manifolds. In this case, we move toward an optimum solution.

### 17.10 L1 and L2 Regularization Methods

L1 and L2 regularization are the best ways to manage to overfit and perform feature selection when we have a large set of features. However, it means that the perfect model will only give accurate results for some varieties of actual data. So, we need to make the model imperfect.

We may create a less complex, more parsimonious model when we have many features in our data set. Two widely used regularization techniques to address overfitting and feature selection are L1 and L2 regularization.

L1 Regularization also called a lasso regression, adds the “absolute value of magnitude” of the coefficient as a penalty term to the loss function.

L2 Regularization, called ridge regression, adds the “squared magnitude” of the coefficient as the penalty term to the loss function.

A regression model that uses the L1 regularization technique is called lasso regression, and a model that uses the L2 is called ridge regression.

Lasso is an acronym for most minor absolute shrinkage and selection operator, and lasso regression adds the “absolute value of magnitude” of the coefficient as a penalty term to the loss function.

![](Images/Image171938.png)

L2 Regularization: Ridge Regression

Ridge regression adds the “squared magnitude” of the coefficient as the penalty term to the loss function.

The critical difference between these techniques is that lasso shrinks the less important feature’s coefficient to zero, thus, removing some features altogether. In other words, L1 regularization works well for feature selection if we have many features.

The MSE (Cost function) will maintain a constant flat value after some Epochs (Data set count). Refer to Figure 17.7. We can stop in the early stage before the cost function gets increased. We can create a specialized algorithm for the early stopping scenarios.

## Machine Learning: Classification (Linear and Logistic classification)

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 18.1 Classification

In decision-making, linear and nonlinear regression help us predict the values accurately. Sometimes, we may need to classify the given data, like identifying the fruit names by seeing the image or reading from given specifications.

Class label is essential in classification. In the machine learning model, we train the dataset by specifying attributes (Features) and class labels; the machine learning model learns well and understands the class label well.

In general, we train a tuple (X, Y) as a training set to a machine learning model, and the model learns both attributes(X) and class labels (Y).

Here X is the independent variable, input

Y is the class label, dependent variable, output

More examples:

|  || X Independent Variable || Y Class Label |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fruit Picking Robot || Fruit attributes: Colour, Size || An apple, An orange, a Banana, strawberry |
| Email Classification || Message Header and Body || Spam or Not Spam |

Table 18.1 Examples of linear classification

As with regression, we can also have the training, validation, and test data sets separately for classifications.

Training set:

Machine learning initially learns the images one by one from the training set and creates the pattern (Weights) for the learned items. For example, say if the colour is white for the animal, it could be a cat. Training set’s significant purpose is to learn from the given labels.

Validation set:

Most of the time, machine learning needs to change the pattern weights and check whether these new weights classify the data correctly. So, we need one testing activity within the learning stage. These are validated using validation sets. Changing weights and checking the accuracy comes under hyperparameter tuning activity. We will see what these hyperparameters are later part of the book. However, now, remember that many parameters within machine learning decide the learning patterns.

Testing set:

Finally, we finish the training and tuning; machine learning needs to test and measure the outcomes. Here it uses the training set. It is essential because it will give us the analysis of the results, like the accuracy and prediction error. Usually, these testing sets should differ from the training set to get the accuracy of the test results. For example, we can test one image in which a rat is super tiny (a newborn rat). Imagine that we need to provide these tiny rats with machine learning in the training phase. This test aims to measure the accuracy of the prediction weights against all varieties of data.

Figure 18.1 Machine learning – General process

The linear classifier also is similar to linear regression. It just uses a linear function and calculates the output value. However, it maintains the decision boundary to classify the input values into specific class labels.

Say, the linear function is like below

Y = MX + c → If the input is single

Y = (W1) X1+(W2) X2+(W3) X3………. (Wn) Xn + W0 → if the inputs have multiple inputs.

However, if the Y value exceeds zero, we can classify Y as one category. Likewise, if the Y value is smaller than zero, we can classify Y as another category.

It all depends on how we fix the decision boundary. As usual, the coefficient values (m values) are fundamental. These final optimal m values fit as decision boundaries in production and testing.

Like linear regression, this classification learning keeps improving the weights when the training set keeps adding the data values.

### 18.3 Logistic Regression

It is a classification model and discriminative function-based model. After getting the Y value in numeric, it just uses the sigmoid activation function to classify the output as 0 or 1. We have discussed many terminologies in the above statement. What are those? We will see these terminologies now.

Here activation function means it takes decisions at multiple stages, like what to do with the previous outcome. Moreover, it converts the numerical data into nonnumerical outcomes.

The sigmoid mentioned above is the mathematical function that converts everything into either 0 or 1 by a sigmoid function. The sigmoid function is like the one below.

![](Images/Image172017.png)

![](Images/Image172028.png)

Figure 18.4 Sigmoid function – Logistic regression

The model will convert any numeric value range into the sigmoid activation like above. So, for example, if we have a range of Y values from 400 to 400,000, we can still divide this range into 0-1. Likewise, if we have the range from –36,500 to 800,000, we can still divide the range to 0-1.

Example:

Linear regression predicts every house rent value in the city for the year (Maybe next year). Logistic regression will predict the same average house rent value in the city as categories like high or low.

Given data x and the associated binary (0/1) classifies label Y. we will get discriminant function P(Y/X) in the below format.

If output Y is 1, P(Y/X) will be 1 otherwise

P (Y/X) will be 0

Here the classifier takes the probability calculation like this. If Y>=0.5, then Y=1, and it is an apple. On the other hand, if Y<0.5, then Y=0, and it is not an apple.

### 18.4 Binary Classification vs. Multi Classification

Binary classification means we classify into any two items. We use logistic regression for that. Either 0 or 1 will get determined. Multi-classification means it will get multi classes (Like an apple, an orange, a Banana, and a Strawberry). We will use the activation function as SoftMax for multi-classifications. We can see more about SoftMax in the next volume of this book series because it is mainly getting used in neural networks. Here in binary classification, we use sigmoid as an activation function.

### 18.5 Cost Function

Our final classification prediction is not linear because we use sigmoid as an activation function. Squares the predictions as MSE (Mean square value) will not work in classification. Say, for example, if we get the predicted values as 0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1 and the actual class labels as 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, then our usual MSE will get calculated like below.

![](Images/3.png)

\= 2/2 = 1 =100% → This cost function is imperfect and will not work for logistic regression.

Also, if our cost function has many local minimums, gradient descent may not find the optimal global minimum. It means we cannot work with gradient descent for cost optimization.

So, we need some other solution. Here we use the cost function as Cross-Entropy (Also called log loss).

### 18.6 Cross Entropy

It measures the performance of a classification model where the output will get calculated from probability values in the 0-1 range.

Cross-entropy loss, or log loss, measures the performance of a classification model whose output is a probability value between 0 and 1.

Cross-entropy loss increases as the predicted probability diverges from the actual label.

So, predicting a probability of .028 when the actual observation label is one would be wrong and result in a high loss value.

A perfect model would have a log loss of 0.

We can divide the cross-entropy loss into two separate cost functions: one for y=1 and one for y=0

As with linear regression, here we use the gradient descent, and the Fθ(X) value (Predicted value function) will get used. This value will converge the cost function value. As per the logic, we will use the below formula.

The perdition function for regression is = mi Xi

## Machine Learning: Decision Tree

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 19.1 Decision Tree

Everywhere, nowadays engineers are using this algorithm. It is getting used in both Regression and Classifications. It is also the supervised learning technique, as in the previous chapters (Linear and Logistic regression classifications). It maintains the tree-structured classification, and the internal nodes represent the dataset’s features. Branches represent the decision nodes, and each leaf node represents the outcome.

The tree has two sets of nodes, the decision node and the leaf node. Decision nodes help to decide the subsequent movements and make decisions on the move based on the data value’s fitment. Leaf nodes at the end give the final predicted outcome.

![](Images/Image172061.png)

Figure 19.1 Decision Tree

If then else, rules will be maintained in decision nodes. The significant advantage of this decision tree algorithm is that it is inexpensive to construct.

When we train the model by training set data for every individual data, the decision tree model will modify the tree accordingly (Learning from training data).

This model’s learning activity will be fast as compared to other models. It is easy to interpret the small-sized trees. It can easily handle any attribute.

There are a few disadvantages. First, this tree model uses enormous space complexity.

### 19.2 Decision Tree – Example

We will see below one sample decision tree, which is a detailed one.

The government was planning for the republic day celebration and decided to conduct one cultural activity. It is the usual activity.

The statistics agency planned to consider some sample people and ask them about their willingness to contribute to the republic day function. In the first batch, they asked ten people in the country’s headquarters, and they got the below details from their survey. They also collected a few other details, like whether they knew how to dance and whether they were movie freaks.

![t\_19\_2](Images/t_19_2.jpg)

Table 19.1 Example data set for decision tree

The machine learning model learns the tree based on the above information Table 19.1. The model learns well and alters the tree based on the dataset. It learns all the ten data one by one. Finally, after feeding all the ten data, we get the decision tree below.

![](Images/Image172109.png)

Figure 19.2 Sample Decision Tree

Here the republic day participation is the final output node. We can also call this dependent variable Y. If we pass any data into this tree, like “their interest in dance,” “their age,” and “their movie interest,” we will predict the dependent variable. That means it is a precise binary classification, and we can easily find whether the person can participate in the republic day function. Finally, we train without a data set. In the above table, we have mentioned ten data pieces used as training data sets.

The tree will slowly get modified when we train the data records individually. After training, we learned to finalize the tree as the optimal machine-learning model. The following section will see how this optimal tree works as a machine-learning model.

Root Node – In the tree mentioned above, the root node is the movie freak node.

Branches – Branches are subtrees. For example, we had two branches in the example mentioned above. One subtree is related to dance. The other branch is inside the dance subtree, and this subtree is related to age.

### 19.3 Pruning

Removing some child branches or nodes based on specific predictions is called pruning. When the subtree is not helpful and does not affect the results and outcomes, it must be identified and destroyed. Simply unwanted leaves or subtrees will get removed. In our example, we can remove the node “age < 46.5”. It will help us to reduce time and space complexity, and this subtree could be more helpful.

### 19.4 How to Create the Decision Tree?

A crucial step in creating a decision tree is splitting the data. First, we need to find the optimal way to split the data sets into two data sets (Data Set 1 and data set 2). We use multiple criteria to find the optimal split and decision against all the available dependent data. One of the methods is “Gini Impurity.” We will discuss this Gini Impurity in the below section.

### 19.5 Gini Impurity

We calculate the Gini Impurity for the data set D with the help of the below formula.

Gini Impurity (Data) = n1 / n \* Gini (Data1) + n2 / n \* Gini (Data2)

Here n = n1 + n2 (The size of the data set)

And

![](Images/Image172117.png)

Here Data 1 and 2 are subsets of D. PJ is the probability of samples belonging to class j at a given node, and C is the number of data elements or classes.

The node’s value (Purity) increases if the Gini Impurity is low. The Gini impurity of the pure node is zero.

Steps to divide the decision tree using Gini Impurity. We need to follow the below steps.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)For every possible division, calculate the Gini Impurity of each child node.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Calculate the Gini Impurity of each split as a weighted average of child nodes.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Select the tree split with the lowest values of Gini Impurity.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Repeat the above steps 1-3 until no further split is possible.
    

Let us see these steps with our example.

First, we must create a subset before creating a decision tree for the entire dataset. In our example. We can consider two features “Love Movie” and “Love Dance.”

First, we need to decide which one is root here. That is important. How do we do this? We need to predict the target with any of the features and then use that feature with the lowest Gini Impurity. This lowest feature will get used as the top node.

Gini Impurity will be

![](Images/Image172125.png)

Figure 19.3 Decision Tree – Gini Impurity

The Gini Impurity for the trees is getting calculated as follows.

Case 1:

TRUE: Gini (Data1): 0.28

FALSE: Gini (Data2): 0

From both these weights, the Gini Impurity of the node is 0.168

Case 2:

TRUE: Gini (Data1): 0.32

FALSE: Gini (Data2): 0.32

From both these weights, the Gini Impurity of the node is 0.32

The lower Gini Impurity will get selected first in the tree. Once we complete this, we will repeat the process for other left-over features at the end; if any of the features remain, that will be the final decision tree node.

![](Images/Image172132.png)

Figure 19.4 Decision Tree – Gini Impurity

The above one refers to all categorical values. We can also add the numerical variable ‘age’ from our example. It is different.

We can sort out the data by the numerical variable (Age)

Find multiple options of the numerical values and calculate Gini Impurity for all the options.

It is getting calculated like below.

![img\_19\_5](Images/img_19_5.png)

Figure 19.5 Decision Tree – Gini Impurity

The lowest Gini index value will get considered for numerical value too. We have taken the age of 46.5 as the decision criterion.

### 19.6 Entropy and Information Gain

Entropy means the degree of randomness in the given training dataset, which will indicate how impure or uncertain the data is in the data set. For example, we can calculate the entropy in the following way.

![](Images/Image172149.png)

Here N: Number of variety of classes in the given data set

Pi: Event Probability

### 19.7 Information Gain (IG)

Information gain will predict the outcome data for all the information on any particular feature or variable in the dataset. We calculate this by subtracting the entropy of a particular attribute inside the data set from the entropy of the whole data set.

![](Images/Image172161.png)

\= H(I) – H (A, DS)

H(I) – Entropy of whole data set (DS)

|DSJ| – Number of instances with the j value of an attribute A

|DS| – Total number of instances in the dataset

M – Set of distinct values of an attribute A

H(DSj) – Entropy of the subset of instances for attribute A

H (A, DS) – Entropy of an attribute A

The above idea is straightforward. We select the entropy of the subset having attribute A from the entropy of the whole set. The difference will provide the amount of information we have gained.

### 19.8 Issues in the Decision Tree Model

A decision tree is prone to overfitting due to the nature of doing a split at each node. It hence needs some form of regularization to reduce the complexity.

Let us take an example: Assume N is the number of data points, k is the number of features, and d is the Depth of the tree.

The time complexity to learn a single node of a decision tree is done by calculating the sorting and updating the impurities as we go.

If we go through and compute the impurities over the entire dataset each time, it is O(Nkd). It means it is somewhere between O(NklogN) and O(N2k).

As we can see in the explanation above, the worst-case performance can be terrible! Hence, we use some pruning techniques to reduce the complexity and thereby reduce the chance of overfitting

Pre-Pruning: When working with tree-based methods, there is a much more direct way to control the complexity: with the number of decision nodes.

A decision tree that subdivides into individual data points is maximally overfitting. It can be controlled directly (say by limiting the number of leaves) or imposing a maximum depth. This idea (stopping early when some criteria are unmet) is called pre-pruning.

Post-Pruning: In this case, we start with a complex tree and trim it backward

The simplest way is reducing error pruning: iterating over leaf nodes and removing those without hurting the predictive accuracy on a withheld validation set. There are many pruning algorithms out there; below are three examples of pruning algorithms.

Pruning by information gain: We can prune our decision tree by using information gain in both post-pruning and pre-pruning. In pre-pruning, we check whether the information gain at a particular node exceeds the minimum gain. In post-pruning, we prune the subtrees with negligible information gain until we reach the desired number of leaves.

Reduced Error Pruning (REP): REP belongs to the post-Pruning category. In REP, the model will prune with the help of a validation set. In REP, the model will evaluate all nodes for pruning in a bottom-up fashion. The node will prune a node if the resulting pruned tree performs no worse than the original tree on the validation set. The model will replace the subtrees with a leaf node assigned the most common class.

## Machine Learning: Instance-based Learning Algorithms

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 20.1 K-nearest Neighbour Classifier

The k-nearest neighbour classifier is the instance-based classifier algorithm. Even though this is a supervised one, it is a quick decision-maker. Learning activities are getting postponed, and the learning will not happen in the training phase. During the actual testing or production phase, the actual data will be analysed from the nearest neighbours, and decisions will be made quickly over there with the help of instance learning.

When we try to predict the outcome classification for any input data, as per the existing data’s weightage, the new data gets accommodated and classified on its own. It means that it is a self-realization. For example, “I am in between three green balls.” and “Nobody else is available near me,” so the K-nearest neighbour model decides the classification as a green ball. We will use this if the data world is complex and has a massive data set.

![](Images/Image172171.png)

Figure 20.1 K-nearest neighbour classifier

Please refer to Figure 20.1. Most of the e-com and recommender system applications in the modern era use this logic. The system provides quick recommendations for all the customers based on their requirement fitment, which is decided quickly in the K-nearest neighbour model.

When we train using a training set, the training data instance first checks the neighbours. The training is required here to feed more data into the model. However, finally, the production or test data will use the same logic. Here there is no relationship between training, testing, and production.

It is getting used in both regression and classification.

Example:

Just imagine 1 million insects related data is available in the biology-based research center. Based on the size of the insect, Insect name and category is getting decided from this database. Suppose we use the KNN (K-nearest neighbors) machine learning model. In that case, it will be easy to predict the output because 1 million data is vast.

Suppose after the KNN model has learned 1 million data; if we place the new insect weight as an input, the analysis will get started. As per the analysis, if the input sits near one particular insect category, we can classify the insect with the identified category without analysing anything.

Refer to Figure 20.2 below for this insect classification.

![](Images/Image172179.png)

Figure 20.2 Example – K-nearest neighbour classifier

### 20.2 Steps involved in KNN Algorithm Models

Select the number ‘K’ as the neighbour count variable.

How to choose the variable ‘K’?

There is no particular way to choose the ‘K’ variable. However, we can generally choose the K as ‘5’.

We have one explicit algorithm named “K-Elbow,” which use for ‘K’ selection. We are going to discuss this in the next section.

Calculate the Euclidian distance for neighbours.

![](Images/Image172188.png)

The above formula helps us get the distance between two ‘n’ distance instances.

We have many norms in mathematics used for calculating distance.

Example norms: L1 norm and L Infinity norm.

However, the KNN algorithm commonly uses Euclidian distance (L2 Norm) as the best one to measure the distance.

The purpose of Euclidian distance is to arrange the neighbours in ascending order based on their distance. This arrangement will help the model to set the boundary as the ‘K’ values. Say, for example, if the ‘K’ value is five and the surrounding neighbour count is 7 for the new data, then Euclidian distance will measure the accurate distance of the neighbours. It will help the model filter five neighbours out of 7.

From all identified ‘K’ neighbours, the model identifies the category.

The category item count will help us decide the maximum count category.

Finally, the model completes the new data’s category allocation.

### 20.3 K-Elbow Method for ‘K’ Value Selection

![](Images/Image172195.png)

Figure 20.3 K-Elbow method for “K” value selection

The model will complete the activity with minimal data set during training. The sum of square error (SSE) or some other error function can be used individually for all the ‘K’ values. For example, from number 1, we can choose all the K integer values and calculate the SSE.

Sample test data can be selected. First, all the SSE error values will be collected; we prepare one graph to compare all the ‘K’ value’s performance; the above Figure 20.3 represents this.

There will be a dip point in the graph, the optimal value. Finally, we can choose this ‘K’ value for the KNN algorithm.

### 20.4 Locally Weighted Regression Model

![](Images/Image172202.png)

Figure 20.4 Locally weighted regression model

Most of the time, the regression linearity will not be like a single line. In practice, there will be many jumps and curves, as mentioned in Figure 20.4. It is not precisely a nonlinear function. There are linear curves in the middle of the data values. The second big issue is the number of data values. If the count is vast in the count, tuning or obtaining coefficient values from all the data is very tough or impossible. So locally weighted regression model is getting used here.

The analysis on cost, cost optimization, and the actual values (If required) will get analysed from these local regression data values.

So finally, for the limited area, we apply the weights. Moreover, all other concepts are the same as linear regression, like cost optimization (Gradient decent convergence).

## Machine Learning: Support Vector Machine

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

Support vector machine model also is getting used in classification and regression. However, mainly, it is getting used for classification algorithm models.

### 21.1 SVM Basics

![](Images/Image172210.png)

Figure 21.1 Example – One-dimensional data set

Imagine our intelligent robotic agent working on an agriculture farm. The agent’s job is to classify the animals as goats or calves. It has to separate these goats and calves, which is part of the robot’s job. Through computer vision, it can just classify the animal. If in case of height is the only classification attribute, the robots easily classify the goats and calves, as mentioned in Figure 21.1 above. Goats are more miniature (Even the old age goats) than calves. Calves are a little taller (Even the youngest calf) than goats. So, it is easy to classify these two animals.

Imagine we have finished the training. Once we complete the training, it classifies the data into the two groups mentioned above with some middle classifiers. This final decision boundary classifies the groups by middle number (particular height based on training data).

Once we finish the training, during the actual production or testing stage, if the new animal falls very far from the middle classification point, it is each to classify the new animal. Again, refer to Figure 21.2 for this reasonable classification effort.

![](Images/Image172218.png)

Figure 21.2 Example – Support vectors in a one-dimensional data set

Similarly, to this, the right side also is straightforward to classify. However, if the new animal is near the middle point, there will be a problem.

![](Images/Image172225.png)

Figure 21.3 Example – Support vectors in a one-dimensional data set

We cannot precisely say that the new animal is either a goat or a calf. Please refer to Figure 21.3. As per the equal split analogy, the model classifies this animal as a calf. However, this animal is a goat. How do we solve this problem?

Initially, we will start discussing the procedure for taking this middle classification point. Usually, on the left, if we see the category elements, there will be one extreme endpoint (Maximum value) for that particular category. Similarly, the second category’s first element on the left will be our target for the second category. We usually take both these points and get the exact middle of these two points. It will help to finalize the middle classification point. It is the point that sits in between two or more groups.

Here the deep endpoints we considered for the middle classification point analysis are support vectors. The supporting points which decide the middle classification divider are called support vectors. The margin is the distance between the support vectors and the middle classification divider. The supporting vector and margin details are available in Figure 21.4.

![](Images/Image172233.png)

Figure 21.4 Example – Support vectors in a one-dimensional data set

The distance is the same in the middle divider point for both category support vectors because we take the exact middle point from these support vectors. However, we are not considering the exact middle point as a classification divider; the margin from one side will be  
small.

### 21.2 Maximum Margin Classifier

![](Images/Image172242.png)

Figure 21.5 Example – Support vectors in a one-dimensional data set

Referring to Figure 21.5 above, the margin between the support vectors is small because the distance is minimal. What is the problem with these small margin means; some production stage data will get a wrong answer. For example, suppose one weak calf looks like a goat. In that case, it will fit precisely in the middle classifier mark (Classifier range). Then lots of wrong predictions will be there with this model.

![](Images/Image172253.png)

Figure 21.6 Example – Support vectors in a one-dimensional data set

To solve the issues mentioned above, we need to trade off something. As with the bias-variance trade-off, we must scarify something to get a good model.

Instead of the small margin, if we put the maximum possible margin from the threshold, we can call this a maximum margin classifier.

What does that mean? Instead of taking a small margin, we should take a significant margin to avoid many mispredictions during the testing or production stage. The only sacrifice is to avoid specific edge data from both groups (Based on the maximum margin decision). We call these points misclassification data points. Refer to Figure 21.6 above for these misclassifications.

The misclassifications are outliers. We can ignore specific extreme data while creating a maximum margin classifier.

We can recall the lesson for bias/variance trade-off from previous chapters. If we do not use misclassifications, the existing model will have a low-bias model. However, we need better performance with high variance in actual production. So, the opposite of this, when we use a misclassification point, we pick the high bias model, which will get low variance in the actual production or testing stage.

We also call these margins soft margins. When we have misclassifications in the margin, we call these soft margins.

How do we decide on the count for the soft margin classifier?

We need to use cross-validation techniques to determine how many misclassifications we need to accept during the soft margin fix. It means it is a trade-off. If we see a high variance in testing, we can reduce the variance by sacrificing the misclassifications slowly. Once we achieve good variance, we can stop the misclassification boundary. We are trading off the bias for variance.

### 21.3 Soft Margin Classifier

We are recalling one more time. Soft margin or support vector classifier means which allow these misclassified data.

### 21.4 Two-Dimensional Data and Support Vector Classifier

Whatever we have seen in the above sections is related to the single-dimension classifier. From now on, we can analyse about two-dimensional support vector classifier.

Getting the optimal line classifier matters a lot.

![](Images/Image172279.png)

Figure 21.9 Example – Support vectors classification in a two-dimensional data set

If we see Figure 21.9 above, we should use multiple possible lines as two-dimensional data classifiers. However, finally, we select the best optimal supporting vector from these lines. That decision is also related to bias, variance trade-offs, and cost optimization techniques.

Here soft margin is calculated from two more additional lines instead of two points in the one-dimensional dataset.

![](Images/Image172286.png)

Figure 21.10 Example – Support vectors classification in a two-dimensional data set

Soft margins as like one dimension data; here, it allows misclassifications in-between lines. As usual, we use cross-validation techniques to decide the range for misclassifications. See Figure 21.10 above for the two-dimension misclassifications and soft margins.

If the data falls under the plan, it is one category, and the upper line data values are in different categories. See Figure 21.11 above for details.

We cannot depict four or more dimensions with a graph.

Support vector classification is always better because it uses misclassifications and bias-variance trade-offs.

### 21.9 Kernal Functions

The kernel Function is a method used to take data as input and transform it into the required form of processing data. “Kernel” is used due to a set of mathematical functions used in Support Vector Machine providing the window to manipulate the data. So, Kernel Function generally transforms the training set of data so that a nonlinear decision surface can transform into a linear equation in a higher number of dimension spaces. It returns the inner product between two points in a standard feature dimension.

Sigmoid Kernel: We have already seen this in our classification section. The Sigmoid mentioned above is the mathematical function that converts everything into either 0 or 1 by a sigmoid function. The sigmoid function is like the one below.

![](Images/Image172324.png)

We convert any numeric value range into the sigmoid activation like above. For example, if we have a range of Y values from 400 to 400,000, we can still divide this range by 0-1. Likewise, if we have the range from –36,500 to 800,000, we can still divide the range to 0-1.

Nowadays, the tanh function also is getting used in neural networks. We will discuss this in the next volume.

![](Images/Image172333.png)

![](Images/Image172345.png)

Figure 21.15 SVM – Sigmoid kernel function

Polynomial Kernel: It represents the similarity of vectors in the training data set in a feature space over polynomials of the original variables used in the kernel.

![](Images/Image172354.png)

Gaussian Kernel: We use this algorithm to perform transformation when there is no prior knowledge about data.

![](Images/Image172362.png)

Gaussian Kernel Radial Basis Function (RBF): Same as above kernel function, adding radial basis method to improve the transformation.
