## Machine Learning: Bayesian Learning

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

### 22.1 Bayesian Machine Learning

Bayesian machine learning is constructing statistical models based on Bayes’ Theorem. Before discussing all the algorithms, we can revisit the Bayes theorem. We will see this one more time because this is very important to start discussing Bayesian Machine Learning.

### 22.2 Bayes’ Theorem (Statistics Point of View)

This theorem tells us the probability of an event, given prior knowledge of related events that occurred earlier. Bayes’ Theorem is

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)

Bayes’ theorem is an excellent tool for identifying conditional probability with prior knowledge and new evidence. We can also use the tree method, which is a simple one. The tree method can be used for minor problems and cannot work with the tree method on big problems. That is why Bayes’ theorem is a very effective one. We will look into one more example. In that example, we will solve the problem with both methods. Both methods will have the same answer.

Example:

Raja is buying one lottery sheet which belongs to one famous lottery system of that city. In that lottery sheet, one crucial secret number is on the backside. It has four flavours (1, 2, 3, 4). There are two lottery shops in the street. One is fair shop. Another shop is unfair one. The fair shop’s lottery sheet will always have an equal probability of getting the four-flavoured secret number. It means we get a lottery sheet with a random number with a probability of (For 1 = ¼; For 2 = ¼; For 3= ¼; For 4=¼).

However, if anyone enters the unfair shop, 50% of the sheets have one as a secret number. So, it is a fake sheet. However, 50% of sheets are valid, and it has an equal probability of getting the other three secret numbers (2, 3, 4).

Raja needs to find out which shop is a fair shop and which one is an unfair shop. He has chosen one shop and bought one lottery sheet already. He got a secret number as 1. What is the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop?

Solution through Bayes’ theorem:

Since we are looking for the probability that the shop is unfair, given that the lottery sheet’s secret number is 1, we are looking for P (unfair shop /1). In this case, P(A)=P (unfair shop) and P(B)=p (1). So, we need to find the following values and put them into the formula

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (unfair shop /1)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (unfair shop)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (1)
    

We know from the problem that P (1/unfair shop) = ½, and since there are two shops in the street and each choice is equally likely P (unfair shop) = ½

The probability of getting one as a secret number is the probability of choosing the unfair shop and getting one as a secret number or the probability of choosing the fair shop and getting one as a secret number.

Let us first find the probability that the secret number is from the unfair shop, and we get one as a secret number.

P (unfair shop) = 1/2, and the probability of a one on the unfair shop is 50 %. So, the probability of unfair shop and 1 is

P (unfair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/2) = ¼

Now let us find the probability that the shop is fair, and we got one as a secret number.

P (Fair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/4) =1/8

Therefore, the probability of getting one as the secret number is

P (1) = 1/4 + 1/8  
P (1) = 2/8 + 1/8  
P (1) =3/8

Putting these values into Bayes’ Theorem, we get

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)  
P(A/B) = ½. ½ / 3/8  
P(A/B) = ¼ / 3/8  
P(A/B) =1/4.8/3  
P(A/B) =8/12 = 2/3

Solution through tree method:

How do we get this? Do we need to reconfirm from the tree view? Let us work on that now; we need to show branches in our tree for every event in our problem. Initially, Raja picked the shop. The shop can be fair or unfair, and we have an equal chance of picking each one.

![](Images/Image172378.png)

Each shop can lead to four possible outcomes, so that we will show these in our tree. However, we also know that the unfair shop will land on one 50 % of the time and that all three other outcomes are equally likely, which means we could think of this as three possible outcomes leading to 1 and one possible outcome leading to each of the other numbers.

![](Images/Image172385.png)

However, we always need to make sure our tree is balanced. We cannot have six branches from the unfair and four from the fair shop side. We have to have an equal number of branches on both sides so that we will scale up both sides to the least common multiple, 24. That means we need to scale up all the unfair shop branches by four and all the fair shop branches by 6.

![](Images/Image172393.png)

The next part of the problem tells us that Raja has chosen one shop and got a 1. So, we will trim all the branches of our tree that lead to results that did not occur.

![](Images/Image172401.png)

Now we want to know the probability that Raja chose \[Given the unfair shop\]. First, remember that each branch on the unfair shop side represents four possibilities, so 12 branches come from the unfair shop side. On the other hand, the branch on the fair shop side represents six possibilities, so six branches are coming from the fair shop side.

Therefore, the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop is all the outcomes coming from the unfair shop side, 12, divided by all of the branches in total, 18: P (unfair shop) = 12/ (12 + 6) = 2/3.

So, we matched Bayes’ theorem and standard tree methods. Both the values are the same. It is a simple example. If we are working on a big problem, imagine how helpful the Bayes’ theorem is. It is very effective in Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning.

### 22.3 Bayes Theorem and Naïve Bayes from Artificial Intelligence’s Point of View

We need one example case to discuss the topics here.

We can recall our exhibition hall example. The intelligent agent is ready to start finding the bomb and terrorists. The goal draft is ready, and the planning is ready. When the agent started moving from the exhibition office, it is seeing one abnormal man standing outside the office. The agent got one doubt about this person, like whether this person was a terrorist or not. The agent must confirm this by analysis.

This man looks strong (Strong body). Also, he looks tense. His age is middle age, and his skin colour is type I, as per the Fitzpatrick scale.

\[Note: Fitzpatrick scale is the measurement tool of human skin colour. Fitzpatrick provided these divisions. As per this scale classification: Scale I is Light and pale white; Scale II is White and fair; Scale III is Medium white to light brown; Scale IV is Olive and moderate brown; Scale V is Brown and dark brown; Scale VI is Very dark brown to black.\]

From the above four appearances, can we confirm whether this person is a terrorist? No. Not certainly. In general,

P (Terrorist = True) = 0.001  
P (Terrorist = False) = 0.999

The prior or unconditional probability

Already we have seen from the statistical section that unconditional probability means the prior probability, which happens before evidence for the event. What does that mean? Before the agent started analysing anything like his appearance, it just confirmed from its prior probability data. For example, “1 out of 1000 people is a terrorist”. It is the prior probability data available in the robot’s memory.

Posterior or conditional probability

It is vital. Now evidence is available with robotic agents. So, it can make decisions based on the evidence or conditions.

P (Terrorist | ¬ Strong Body ∧ Middle Age) = 0.09

See the above probability. The agent analysed the appearance of this man and found that he was a strong man with a muscular body type and looked like he was in middle age. Given these two pieces of evidence, the agent started analysing the probability of whether he was a terrorist.

Probability distribution

We have already seen random variables and random distributions from our statistics section. Usually, in the distribution world, Probability distribution gives values for all possible assignments.

For example, from our exhibition hall case, the skin colour of any man will have the below distributions.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘I’) = 0.16
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘II’) = 0.18
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘III’) = 0.18
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘IV’) = 0.16
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘V’) = 0.16
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Skin Colour = ‘VI’) = 0.16
    

In probability distributions, the sum of all possible probability values will be 1. If the sum is not 1, we can reach one by Normalization.

\[Normalization means converting everything into 100% \[Ex: if the value set is \[0.1, 0.2\], then the normalized value set will be \[0.33, 0.67\]. Here we are making everything to 1\[100%\]\]

We also denote the probability distribution something like this < 0.16, 0.18, 0.18, 0.16, 0.16, 0.16 >

Joint probability distribution

In the previous section, we have seen that we can write the probability distribution of any random variable in the domain of probability possibilities. The set of two or more random variables gives the probability of every atomic event on those variables. These sets of all the probabilities are called joint probability distributions.

In our example, both the skin colour random variable and the terrorist random variable can create the probability set of all atomic events on these two random variables. We can write the product rules for all possible values of body skin colour and terrorism as a single equation.

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Body skin colour, Terrorist) = P (Body skin colour | Terrorist) \* P (Terrorist).

Instead of these, we can write 6 \* 2 = 12 equations like the one below.

\[Note SC = Body Skin Colour and T = Terrorist\]

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale I ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale I | T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale II ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale II | T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale III ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale III | T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale IV ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale IV| T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale V ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale V | T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale VI ∧ T = true) = P (SC = Scale VI | T = true) P (T = true)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale I ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale I | T = false) P (T = false)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale II ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale II | T = false) P (T = false)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale III ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale III | T = false) P (T = false)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale IV ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale IV | T = false) P (T = false)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale V ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale V | T = false) P (T = false)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (SC = Scale VI ∧ T = false) = P (SC = Scale VI | T = false) P (T = false)
    

The conditional probability table for all these options is available below in Table 22.1

![t\_22\_1](Images/t_22_1.jpg)

Table 22.1 Example: Conditional probability table

The primary purpose of joint distribution is to answer any conditional probability queries within the domain. For example, if the agent found the person’s skin colour is “Type III,” from the abovementioned probability distribution table, it can easily find that the probability of the terrorist is 0.169.

Full joint inference and inference

What is the primary purpose of all the above probability analyses? First, our brain or intelligent agent should derive some probabilistic conclusion about the query. Then, as per our example, our intelligent agent must derive the terrorist probability based on the evidence given (Person’s appearance).

We need a full joint distribution for all the evidence we are working on for this inference. Then, we can take two pieces of evidence from our case, “Strong Body “and “Looks tensed,” and conclude all the possibilities and probabilities. Finally, the full joint distribution table will look like the one below (Table 22.2)

![t\_22\_2](Images/t_22_2.jpg)

Table 22.2 Example: Full joint distribution table

Here for any proposition, the sum of atomic events where it is true:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P(Proposition) = Sum of all possibilities. It is called marginal probability.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It means Strong body probability. We can add all four probabilities.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Strong Body) = 0.116 + 0.006 + 0.072 + 0.034 = 1.182 = 11% chances \[The below red colour values in Table 22.3 visualizes this\].
    

![t\_22\_3](Images/t_22_3.jpg)

Table 22.3 Example: Full joint distribution table – Marginal probability

Inference by enumeration

Suppose we need the probability that the person is not a terrorist when the person has a strong body. In that case, we can calculate this conditional probability from our full joint probability distribution table.

i.e., P (¬Terrorist | Strong Body) = P (Terrorist ∧ Strong Body) / P (Strong Body) → As per conditional probability formula.

\= 0.072 + 0.034 / (0.116 + 0.006 + 0.072 + 0.034) = 0.089

Refer to Table 22.4 for details.

![](Images/Image172538.png)

Table 22.4 Example: Full joint distribution table – Conditional probability

We can also make normalized values for all the possible values of the random variables whenever required.

Like above, we can get answers to any query. In reality, the intelligent agent or our brain will have 200 \* 200 possibilities-based join distributions. There will be 200 queries that ask our brain in reality. Our agent also asks 200 queries from joint probability tables.

Independence

We have already seen that if two events are independent (mutually exclusive), the conditional probability will be the independent probability, like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If A and B are independent, if only if
    
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)P (A/B) = P(A)
        
    *   ![box\_bullet](Images/box_bullet.jpg)P (B/A) = P(B)
        
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Here P (A ∧ B) = P(A) \* P(B)
    

Like above, if we refer to our example, Body skin colour is entirely independent of the terrorist decision. Terrorists exercise well, and their body strength differs from the usual public. Terrorist may get tension when he sees the agent. So, body strength and body tension will become evidence for our analysis.

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension, Body Skin Colour) =

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness, Tension, Body Skin Colour) \* P (Body Strongness, Tension, Body Skin Colour).

There are 48 possibilities from all the combinations since body skin colour is independent of everyone else at the table.

It means that

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension, Body Skin Colour) = P (Terrorist, Body Tension, Body Strongness) \* P (Body skin colour)

We can eliminate this from this table. So, the final decomposed joint table will have only 12 possibilities. Therefore, we have saved 36 possibilities in our analysis and analysis time. Please see Figure 22.1 below for this decomposition.

![](Images/Image172546.png)

Figure 22.1 Example – Probability Independence

The power of conditional independence is to reduce storage and analysis complexity. It applies to both our brains and intelligent agent.

Conditional independence

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension) gives 23 – 1 = 7 independent entries.

If the person is a Terrorist, Body Strongness will be independent of Body Tension. Therefore, both of these pieces of evidence do not have any relationship.

In that case, if we want to get the probability for Body Strongness given the Terrorist probability and Body Tension probability, we can ignore the Body Tension probability. It is called conditional independence.

P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = True) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)

The same is applicable for

P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = False) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)

It means that the body tension of the terrorist never has any connection with body strength. Instead, body strongness has a connection with Terrorist probability. So, this conditional probability will help us to reduce two entries from 7.

Here, the probability of terrorists also will get a different perspective.

For example, now that we know Body Strongness and Body Tension are conditionally independent, we will have all the four equations like below.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = True) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist).
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = False) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness = False) = P (Body Tension | Terrorist)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness = False) = P (Body Tension | Terrorist)
    

We can use directly the chain rule instead of full joint distribution \[Recall Conditional Probabilities Multiplication Rule\]. The simple way is this.

P (Body Tension, Terrorist, Body Strongness)

\= P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness) \* P (Terrorist, Body Strongness)

\= P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness) \* P (Terrorist | Body Strongness) \* P (Body Strongness)

\= P (Body Tension | Body Strongness) \* P (Terrorist | Body Strongness) \* P (Body Strongness)

This analysis still reduces our possibilities. All these are possible from conditional independence.

Bayes Theorem and Naïve Bayes

We have already seen this in the previous statistics section. Mr. Bayes innovated this theorem very effectively. We know that

P(A/B) = P(B/A) \* P(A) / P(B)

What does that mean?

Posterior Probability = Likelihood \* Prior probability / Evidence

From our intelligent agent’s point of view, if we need to get the probability of cause given effect, we need to get help from the probability of effort given cause.

P(Cause/Effort) = P(Effort/Cause) \* P(Cause) / P(Effort)

Is it confusing? It is simple. Suppose we need to know the possible output (cause) for the actions we are trying (effort). In that case, we need to know the below likelihood history.

What and all the actions (efforts) have we taken when we got those possible outcomes in the past?

Here likelihood means to get all that old history and get that probability.

Here, the prior probability is causing probability. Without any evidence, what is the possibility of those outcomes in general?

The evidence is, of course, our effort’s probability. We have made that effort. Then, based on the effort probability, calculate the cause probability. So, evidence comes as a conditional one.

We will take one example here. Suppose an intelligent agent wants to know the probability of catching the bus when we use the car. In that case, it just uses the Bayes rule like the one below.

P (Catching Bus /Car) = P (Car/Catching Bus) \* P (Catching Bus) / P (Car)

Here agent already knows to catch the bus. How did the car help in the past? One thousand times, it used a car in the past to catch the bus. Generally, the likelihood of using the car when the bus was the target is 0.50.

The prior probability of catching a bus in general (Either by using a car or any other vehicle) is 0.90. It is just the prior probability without telling what we use to catch the bus. In general, 90% of the time, we catch the bus.

Finally, what is our current evidence? We are using a car, and the probability of using a car is 100%.

So, P (Catching Bus /Car) = 0.90 \* 0.5 /1 = 0.45 = 45%. So, we have only a 45% chance of catching the bus when we use the car.

If we ignore the evidence, the posterior decides from evidence and likelihood.

Posterior probability = α likelihood \* Prior probability

Here α refers to the probability normalization constant (Making the sum to 1).

Now we need to analyse the case for two or more multiple pieces of evidence. If we take our example, getting the terrorist probability from both the pieces of evidence, we combine it like below

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness, Body Tenson) = α (0.02, 0.02) = (0.50, 0.50) → (After normalization)

It is good. However, not good when the evidence count grows. It is impossible to have a vast evidence count.

We need to use Bayes’ rule to reformulate the problem:

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson) = α P (Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson | Terrorist) \* P (Terrorist)

To solve the problem using the Bayes rule, we need to know the conditional probabilities of the connection.

“Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson” for each value of terrorist.

Here we have only two pieces of evidence. So, it is possible to fetch the conditional probabilities. However, as we mentioned in the direct rule, this will become complex when we use many pieces of evidence. So, it is impossible to have colossal evidence.

Here conditional independence will help us.

P (Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson | Terrorist) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) \* P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

We are concluding the above probability because Body Strongness and Body Tenson are independent and disconnected.

If we plug this into our Bayes rule formula, we get

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson) = α P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) \* P (Body Tenson | Terrorist) \* P (Terrorist)

The general definition of conditional independence of two variables, A and B, gives a third variable C, which is

![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)P (A, B | C) = P(A|C) \* P (B |C)

For our example, when we plug this conditional independence

P (Body Strongness, Body Tenson | Terrorist) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) \* P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

This formula is stronger than the previous conditional independence one.

Earlier in the previous section, we have seen that conditional independence provides equal statements like

P (A | B, C) = P (A | C) and P (B | A, C) = P (B | C)

From the previous section, the absolute independence assertions allow the full joint distribution decomposition into much smaller pieces. The same is true for conditional Independence assertions.

In our example, we can decompose the conditions like below.

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tenson)

\= P (Body Strongness, Body Tenson | Terrorist) P (Terrorist) → (As per Product Rule)

\= P (c | Terrorist) \* P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

The above one is excellent; we can use this for the ‘N’ number of independent evidence.

When we come to our general model, this conditional independence helps us to derive like below.

P (Cause, Effect 1, Effect 2……. Effect n) = P(Cause) \* P (Effect 1 | Cause) \* P (Effect 2 | Cause) …………\* (Effect n | Cause)

This is called naïve Bayes model. Also called as Bayesian classifier

![](Images/Image172555.png)

Figure 22.2 Bayesian classifier – Naïve Bayes model

### 22.4 Naïve Bayes Classifier in Machine Learning

X = {X1, X2, ……XN) represents N features.

Y is the label with K possible values (Class). From a probabilistic perspective, X and Y are random variables. The value of Xi is x, and Y is Y.

Then the probability will get calculated as below.

P (Y, X1, X2, ……XN) = P(Y) \* P (X1 | Y) \* P (X2 | Y) …………\* (XN | Y)

Naive Bayes is a classification algorithm for binary (two-class) and multi-class classification problems. The technique is easiest to understand when described using binary or categorical input values.

The representation for naive Bayes is probabilities.

A list of probabilities is stored to file for a learned naive Bayes model. It includes:

Class Probabilities: The probabilities of each class in the training dataset.

Conditional Probabilities: The conditional probabilities of each input value given each class value.

Learn a Naive Bayes Model from data:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Learning a naive Bayes model from our training data is fast.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)We calculate the probability of each class given different input (x) values. No coefficients need to be fitted by optimization procedures.
    

Gaussian Naïve Bayes Classifier

We can use Naive Bayes in real-valued attributes. For example, assume we have Gaussian distribution, which uses Native Bayes.

This extension of naive Bayes is called Gaussian Naive Bayes. Of course, we can use other functions to estimate the data distribution. However, the Gaussian (or Normal distribution) is the easiest to work with because you only need to estimate the mean and the standard deviation from your training data.

Learn a Gaussian Naive Bayes Model From Data: This is as simple as calculating each input variable’s mean and standard deviation values (x) for each class value.

mean(x) = 1/n \* sum(x). Where n is the number of instances, and x is the value for an input variable in our training data.

We can calculate the standard deviation using the following equation: standard deviation(x) = sqrt (1/n \* sum(xi-mean(x)^2)). It is the square root of the average squared difference of each value of x from the mean value of x, where n is the number of instances, sqrt () is the square root function, sum () is the sum function, xi is a specific value of the x variable for the instance ‘i’, and we describe mean(x) from the above statements, and ^2 is square.

### 22.5 Maximum Likelihood Estimation

Find parameters that maximize the probability of seeing the data.

Then get the argmax (the maximum one) → argmax θ P (data | θ)

Easy to compute (Just via counting the experience)

We need to assume a uniform prior probability.

We need to create the distribution of the data.

We can also get an idea about maximum likelihood from a statistics point of view.

Imagine we have collected the statical data for the height of the students in one class. It is mentioned in Figure 22.3.

![](Images/Image172566.png)

Figure 22.3 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

In that class, the height of students varies from low to high, as per the above Figure 22.3.

Any dataset will fit under some probability-based random variable distribution. For example, it is mentioned in Figure 22.4.

![](Images/Image172575.png)

Figure 22.4 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

Figure 22.4 mentions that the entire dataset may fit under the normal distribution. However, sometimes it may fit either in the exponential or Gamma distributions.

Now assume that our data is in a normal distribution.

![](Images/Image172583.png)

Figure 22.5 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

The average height in the class fits precisely in Figure 22.5 above. It is in the centre. Here, the mean will be in the centre of the data. Therefore, normal distribution’s standard deviation 1 has high weightage. Please refer statistical section of our book (Chapters 11-15) for more details.

The measurements should be symmetrical around the mean. Sometimes this symmetrical is not possible with normal distribution. That is ok. It comes under different shapes like skinny, medium, and large bonded.

![](Images/Image172592.png)

Figure 22.6 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

We refer to Figure 22.6 above. As per that, the normal distribution may have any shapes. So, the shapes are not only bell shapes.

![](Images/Image172599.png)

Figure 22.7 Example – Maximum Likelihood procedure

If we generate the normal distribution with the mean in the edge, so much data will reside too far from the mean. As in the above Figure 22.7, we can easily find out that.

![](Images/Image172606.png)

Figure 22.8 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

We may have many options if we consider so many varieties of normal distributions, as in Figure 22.8 above. However, the likelihood of observing all the weights could be higher.

Likelihood of observing data: If we see the below Figure 22.9, we can understand that the all-normal distributions will have different experiences in spreading the data from the mean. We need to get the maximum likelihood from this distribution. If we observe Figure 22.9 below, it clearly shows that one of the “N” normal distribution curves fits under maximum likelihoods. This one, we need to Figure it out.

![](Images/Image172614.png)

Figure 22.9 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

In Bayesian networks, we use maximum likelihood for parameter selection. Therefore, we choose the maximum likelihood parameter.

The value of Theta that maximizes this expression gives the maximum – likelihood hypothesis. The log-likelihood obtains the exact value.

What the maximum likelihood algorithm says means we must give equal priority to all the parameter options like 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.9 before we toss the coin. Give equally likely (Respect to all options) and measure the likelihood. Say we tossed the coin 1000 times. We may fall into any of the below options.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.1 times got head – 10% (100 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.2 times got head – 20% (200 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.55 times got head – 55% (550 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.7 times got head – 70& (700 out of 1000)
    

Irrespective of the parameter’s nature, we choose the best parameter as likelihood. Here 0.7 is the learning probability for heads based on the maximum likelihood calculation.

### 22.6 Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) Learning

Maximum A posteriori (MAP) involves calculating a conditional probability of observing the data given a model weighted by a prior probability or belief about the model.

MAP provides an alternate probability framework to maximum likelihood estimation for machine learning and artificial intelligence Bayesian learning.

Choose the parameter θ that maximizes

argmax θ P (θ | data) = P (data | θ) P(θ)

Find parameters that maximize the probability of seeing the data.

Allows the user to input additional domain knowledge via P(θ) –Prior probability assumption.

For example, before we toss the coin, we know the probability of heads is 0.5. It is prior probability.

What the maximum likelihood algorithm says means we must give equal priority to all the parameter options like 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0 .9 before we toss the coin. Give equally likely (Respect to all options) and measure the likelihood. Say we tossed the coin 1000 times. We may fall into any of the below options.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.1 times got head – 10% (100 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.2 times got head – 20% (200 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.55 times got head – 55% (550 out of 1000)
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)0.7 times got head – 70& (700 out of 1000)
    

Irrespective of the parameter’s nature, we choose the best parameter as likelihood. Here 0.7 is the learning probability for heads based on the maximum likelihood calculation.

However, MAP tells us to add our prior intuition. Do not give equal weightage to all the parameters. Instead, tell the algorithm that we already know the probability of heads is 0.5. Then, we multiply this value by the likelihood of getting an accurate and maximum posterior report.

Say choose P(θ) – 0.5 for heads

Now P (θ | data) = 0.4 \* 0.5 (assume Likelihood is 0.4). for 0.5

From these values, get the maximum value (Argmax)

It will produce better parameters.

When unlimited data is available, MAP is the best choice for learning.

## Machine Learning: Ensemble Learning

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

Ensemble learning generally creates better predictive performance by combining the predictions from multiple machine learning  
models.

So far, whatever we have seen in machine learning is to predict the outcomes based on the learning. Therefore, the performance of all these machine learning models differed, and we cannot guarantee optimal performance from any other machine learning models.

Here Ensemble learning helps everyone to get better predictive performance from the combined effort.

We can classify all the ensemble learning models into three classes. Those are Bagging, stacking, and boosting.

Bagging: Bagging uses the same data set. However, it takes multiple samples from this fixed dataset and creates many decision trees. The primary purpose of this activity is to average the predictions or find the optimal outcome from all these samples.

Example: Random Forest algorithm

Stacking: Stacking involves fitting many different model types on the same data and using another model to learn how to combine the predictions best.

Boosting: It adds the ensemble members sequentially one by one, corrects the predictions made by previous models, and finally outputs a weighted average of the predictions.

Example: Adaboost Algorithm, Gradient Boosting, and XG Boosting

In this chapter, we see details about bagging and boosting. In addition to this, we are going to discuss the Random Forest algorithm (The part of bagging one), AdaBoost algorithm (The part of boosting one), Gradient Boosting algorithm (The part of boosting one), XGBoost algorithms (The part of boosting one).

### 23.1 Bagging and Boosting

Bagging: Bootstrapping the data plus using the aggregate to make the decision is called “Bagging.” We will see this in detail when we discuss the Random Forest algorithm. Refer to below Figure 23.1 for the general bagging principle.

![](Images/Image172622.png)

Figure 23.1 Ensemble Learning – Bagging

Boosting: It adds the ensemble members sequentially one by one, corrects the predictions made by previous models, and finally outputs a weighted average of the predictions. Please refer to Figure 23.2 for more details about boosting.

The algorithm creates a robust classifier from several weak ones. We first build a weak model and then build a second one based on the first model’s errors. We repeat this process until we build a classifier that can make predictions accurately and minimize the error.

![](Images/Image172629.png)

Figure 23.2 Ensemble Learning – Boosting

### 23.2 Random Forest Algorithm

Random forest used decision trees. Usually, decision trees are not suitable when classifying new samples. However, the decision tree will look good when we create it.

Random forest helps to solve this decision tree issue. Random forests use bagging principles. It combines the simplicity of decision trees and provides good performance with the help of the bagging principle.

Steps involved in the Random Forest algorithm:

Step 1: Create a “bootstrapped” dataset.

We create the bootstrapped dataset for the same size as the original. However, we pick the rows at random from the original dataset.

For example, let us take our decision tree chapter’s example table. We will randomly create the bootstrapped table the same size as below (Table 23.1 and 23.2). First, however, we randomly pick up the rows from the original dataset.

Original Dataset:

![t\_23\_1](Images/t_23_1.jpg)

Table 23.1 Ensemble Learning – Example dataset

Bootstrapped Dataset:

![t\_23\_2](Images/t_23_2.jpg)

Table 23.2 Ensemble Learning – Example bootstrapped dataset

While creating the bootstrapped data set, we can repeat the rows. We are just creating random rows from the original dataset. If we see the above Bootstrapped dataset, we can observe that we have used the same row two times (Age 36 data repeats two times).

Step 2: Now, we will create the decision tree from the bootstrapped dataset. While creating the decision tree, a random subset of the variables will use in random order.

It means we can use any random variables for decision tree creation. There are no restrictions for this.

![](Images/Image172722.png)

Figure 23.3 Random Forest algorithm steps

Step 3: Like the above step, repeat the same “N” number of times and get the corresponding decision trees. Everything will have a unique decision analysis. Variables are also random. The dataset is also random. However, we must pick it from the original dataset. That is the only criterion we have.

![Img\_23\_23\_23](Images/Img_23_23_23.png)

Figure 23.4 Random Forest algorithm steps

The above one (Figure 23.4) is called a random forest. It is because we are creating an “N” number of decision trees based on different random data combinations from the same dataset.

Step 4: Voting: We pick one test dataset from the testing set, predict the outcome from the first decision tree, and note down the outcome. For example, in our case, we get a “True” outcome from “Whether the person is willing to participate in the republic day function,” which means we note down the results. Next, the same test data will be tested against all the decision trees and get their respective outcomes. Finally, we get the consolidated outcome table below.

 
| Willing to Contribute to the Republic Day Function |
| --- |
| Yes || No |
| 234 || 134 |

Table 23.3 Ensemble Learning – Example decision

Step 5: We got the answer and outcome from the above votes.

Out of the bagged dataset: We are picking all the rows from the original dataset and creating these bootstrapping datasets. We may miss some datasets in all the tables when we pick these. We call this dataset the “Out of the bag” dataset. We can use these unique datasets in validation or testing datasets. It will create a good performance analysis for our random forests.

Repeated activities in Random Forest:

Random forest activity only ends once we get the super performance. For example, say, a random forest creates 500 cases in a random forest. Once we complete everything, we can evaluate the performance; if we get poor performance with testing data or “Out of bag” data, we can modify the random forests one more time and evaluate the performance. We will repeat this multiple times until we meet the accuracy.

### 23.3 AdaBoost Algorithm

The Adaboost algorithm is part of the boosting algorithm.

Adaboost algorithm creates a forest of trees like a random forest. Here Random Forest does not have any limitation on the depth of the bootstrapped trees. However, AdaBoost has restrictions in creating a random forest with only two leaves and one parent.

![](Images/Image172758.png)

Figure 23.5 Ada boost algorithm steps.

These trees are called stumps. Stumps are decision trees that have only two leaves and one root node.

Stumps are getting created by only one variable. So, from our example, we can create a stump like the one below.

![](Images/Image172767.png)

Figure 23.6 Ada boost algorithm steps.

In the random forest, all the bootstrapped trees had equal votes at the end for the final classifications. Here this is the boosting model. All the stumps have different “Amount to say” weights. It means that some stumps will be helpful for optimal performance, and some mayn’t be useful. See from our decision tables that many columns are not helpful in the decisions, and only some are. The primary purpose of boosting principle is to add feedback from everyone sequentially and get the final optimal output.

Also, in random forests, we get an equal vote from everyone at the end. In Adaboost, it learns from one stump to another stump. It also classifies stump learning as weak learning and strong learning. It has weightage. It learns very little from weak learners and a massive set of information from strong learners.

### 23.4 Steps involved in Ada Boosting

Step 1: We will create the initial weight for all the rows in the table. We can consider our example. From our table, ten records are there. We can initially create the weight as “1/10” for all the data rows.

![t\_23\_4](Images/t_23_4.jpg)

Table 23.4 Ensemble Learning – Ada boost steps

Step 2: Now, we will create our first stump from the original dataset. We use our typical decision tree algorithms (either Gini Index or Information Gain) and take the best optimal variable from the group of variables. Then the first stump will be created like the one below. Also, we need to keep track of the outcome weightage and how this variable is helping in the final decision. See the below Figure 23.7. The “Love Dance” option is helpful in the final decision with an 80% decision factor. Even “Do not love Dance” is helpful, and 80% of the samples are not interested in participating because they do not know how to dance.

![](Images/Image172807.png)

Figure 23.7 Ada boost algorithm steps.

Step 3: We must create the “Total error” for this stump. The total error is the sum of the weights for the incorrectly classified samples. For example, in our stump, the model misclassifies from the “Yes” group, and one misclassifies from the “No” group. So, if we add those weights, we get 1/10 + 1/10 = 2/10 = 1/5. It is the total error. What is the meaning of this? We are trying to understand the degree of usefulness of this variable, “Love Dance”.

Step 4: We need to calculate the “amount to say” of this stump with the help of “Total error.” The formula is this.

Amount to Say = ½ log (1-Total error / Total Error)

In our example case and stump, we get the answer as “0.3010” after calculating from” Total Error.”

“Amount to say” is directly propositional to “Total error.” If “Total errors” has a high weightage, the amount to say will come down.

Step 5: Now it is time to change all our weights with the help of “amount to say.” This new table will repeat the steps. So, this is very important. The formulas are this.

Initially, we will work for misclassified rows. Because for misclassified rows, the new sample weight calculation is different from the corrected classified rows.

For misclassified data:

New Sample weight = sample weight \* e amount to say

For correctly classified data:

New Sample weight = sample weight \* e –amount to say

For example, the misclassified rows are two from our table.

![377](Images/377.jpg)

Here new sample weight will be calculated like the below for misclassified rows.

New Sample weight = 1/10 \* e 0.3010 = 0.135

Here new sample weight will be calculated like the below for correctly classified rows.

New Sample weight = 1/10 \* e –0.3010 = 0.074

So, if we update all the new weights of the table, the new table will look like below.

![t\_23\_5\_1](Images/t_23_5_1.jpg)![t\_23\_5\_2](Images/t_23_5_2.jpg)

Table 23.5 Ensemble Learning – Ada boost steps

The sum of the weights should always get to 1. However, when we add these weights, we get the sum value of 0.862. So, we need to normalize the weights.

![t\_23\_6](Images/t_23_6.jpg)

Table 23.6 Ensemble Learning – Ada boost steps

So, these are the new weights. What does these weights? It tells us that we can reduce a few misclassified data’s power in the table because we misclassify these records in decision-making. We also increased the weights for all the good classified records.

Step 6: Now we got a new table and table weights. We need to repeat the process for another stump. The learning is moving through these weights across the Adaboost algorithm. To select the second stump, we can use the weighted Gini Index to choose it from the new weights. The weighted Gini index would emphasize correctly classifying the sample (The one misclassified by the last stump) since the sample has the most considerable sample weight.

We have one more option. We can create a new collection of samples with more duplicates from heavy-weighted rows. It will help us to move with misclassified rows in further analysis.

Step 7: The stumps and weight modifications will get repeated multiple times. Finally, we will add the “Amount to say” from all the trees to get the maximum classification. It means that in the production or testing stage, the data will perform in all the test trees and get the “Amount to say” count. The highest “Amount to say” count will decide the classification. As per our example, finally, after all the stumps, the “willing to participate” – “Amount of say” and “willing to not participate” – “Amount of say” will decide the classifications. If we insert a record and we get the final sum is “2.5” for the Yes category. “1.2 “for the No category, the algorithm’s decision will go to “Yes.”

So, there is a connection between decision trees and stumps. From one tree and stump, we pass the learning weights to another tree and stump. The repeated weight movements boost the performance.

### 23.5 Gradient Boosting Algorithms

Gradient boosting algorithms is also the boosting algorithm, following the same boosting algorithm principles. However, it is different from AdaBoost. The weights of the training instances are not tweaked. Instead, we train all the predictors using the residual errors of the predecessor as labels. There is a technique called the Gradient Boosted Trees. Usually, gradient-boosting algorithms are getting used in both regression and classification.

The Gradient Boosting Regressor algorithm works in the following way:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 1: The algorithm starts by fitting a simple model (e.g., a decision tree) to the training data.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 2: Then, it calculates the model’s residuals (i.e., the difference between the actual and predicted values).
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 3: We train the new model to predict the residuals of the previous model, and we add this new model to the ensemble.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 4: The algorithm repeats steps 2 and 3 until a specified number of models have been added to the ensemble or until the loss function converges.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Step 5: The final prediction of the Gradient Boosting Regressor is the sum of the predictions of all the models in the ensemble.
    

Instead of the stump, initially, we use a single leaf in Gradient boosting. Later big trees will get used as random forests. Instead of weights, we use residuals (the difference between the actual and predicted values). The name itself shows that it is a residuals-based boosting.

### 23.6 XGBoost Algorithms

XGBoost stands for eXtreme Gradient Boosting. It improves the speed and performance of the gradient-boosting algorithm.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Regularized learning: Regularization helps to smooth the final learned weights to avoid overfitting.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)It is also using unique regression trees with applied regularization.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)We calculate the similarity score instead of residuals.

## Machine Learning: Unsupervised Learning

![ct\_beloww](Images/ct_beloww.jpg)  
  
  
  

As we mentioned in the first chapter of this section, unsupervised learning in many real cases where we cannot teach the labels to the model.

Whatever we have seen so far in the machine learning section is all related to supervised learning. First, we provide the output while training the model. Then, we provide the input and output and ask the model to learn the weights for future prediction.

However, in many cases, even we do not know the output classification, or it is impossible to classify the outputs.

Examples:

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)If we learn new stuff like analysing astronomy.
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)In some cases, the machine learning model needs to identify the classifications from e-com businesses, like classifying the category of buyers.
    

Unsupervised learning has two subcategories.

*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Clustering
    
*   ![bullet\_p](Images/bullet_p.jpg)Association
    

### 24.1 Clustering

Group similar data items and classify them into new categories. The similarities will get identified in-between data items, and classification will happen based on these clusters.

Algorithm: K means clustering model

![](Images/Image172882.png)

Figure 24.1 Unsupervised Clustering

### 24.2 Association

This algorithm is to find the relationships between variables in the extensive database. It determines the set of items that occurs together in the dataset.

Example: In an e-com business, the recommender system will suggest the product to customers recently buying similar products. We recommend frequent combinations of the products from the buyer’s database to the customer who recently bought one from this group of products.

### 24.3 K-Means Clustering

Step 1: Select the number of clusters we want to identify from our data. It is called “K”. Recall our KNN-supervised algorithm. Initially, we need to fix the “K” value because we do not know which “K” value is optimal. We have used the K-Elbow algorithm to determine the optimal “K” values in the KNN-supervised algorithm. Then, similar to this in unsupervised cluster learning methodology, we have to point out the number of possible unknown clusters which may occur in this dataset. That is called “K.”

Initially, by default, we will choose the K value as 3. It means that we want to identify 3 clusters from the given dataset.

For example, we will consider the below unknown data values (Figure 24.2), and we need to create clusters within these unknown data values.

![](Images/Image172889.png)

Figure 24.2 Example – K-means clustering steps

Step 2: We will randomly select three distinct data points. We will consider that these are the initial clusters.

![](Images/Image172896.png)

Figure 24.3 Example – K-means clustering steps

Step 3: We will measure the 3 cluster distances for all other data values in the data set. For example:

![t\_24\_3](Images/t_24_3.jpg)

Table 24.3 Example dataset – K means clustering steps

Step 4: We will find the shortest distance from all these three clusters and assign the respective data to that shortest-distance cluster.

![384\_](Images/384_.jpg)

Table 24.2 Example dataset – K means clustering steps

Finally, we get the classification like this.

![](Images/Image172956.png)

Figure 24.4 Example – K-means clustering steps

Step 5: We need to find the average mean for all the clusters.

![](Images/Image172965.png)

Figure 24.5 Example – K-means clustering steps

Step 6: We must repeat the distance measurement for all the data points against this new mean value.

![384\_2](Images/384_2.jpg)

Table 24.3 Example dataset – K means clustering steps

Step 7: We need to repeat the above actions until we do not see any changes in the new attempt. For example, if we have the similarity below.

Previous set:

![](Images/Image172992.png)

Figure 24.6 Example – K-means clustering steps

Current set:

![](Images/Image173000.png)

Figure 24.7 Example – K-means clustering steps

Step 8: This still needs to be done. If we see unequal variance between the clusters, we may not be happy because we do not see equal variance in-between the clusters. The cluster variance can be any, as mentioned in Figure 24.8 and Figure 24.9 below. This difference occurs because of the difference in the random value selection in Step 1.

![](Images/Image173010.png)

Figure 24.8 Example – K-means clustering steps

![](Images/Image173021.png)

Figure 24.9 Example – K-means clustering steps

See the above two finalized clusters (Figure 24.8 and Figure 24.9). Both are perfect. However, one cluster data value got changed between these two varieties.

Which final cluster is the optimal one? How to select this?

Repeat the K-means clustering algorithm with different starting data points to get many finalized clusters. From them, we can choose the optimally deviated clusters.

![](Images/Image173031.png)

Figure 24.10 Example – K-means clustering steps

We can get an “N” number of variances between the clusters like the above Figure 24.10. Then, finally, we can choose the best variance. As per Figure 24.10 above, the first cluster is the valid one because the distribution of this cluster is equal.

### 24.4 How to Decide the “K” Value

As with the Elbow method in KNN Algorithm, we need to choose all the K values and try clustering the data set.

Then as mentioned in this section, the cluster variance can be measured. The reduction in variance matters a lot. Next, we can plot the graph for this variance reduction. Please refer to the below Figure for details. Then, we can find the optimal point and decide the “K” value for clustering.

What does that mean? The reduction in variance suggests that we cannot further increase the clusters within this range.

This variance may go up after some point in the graph. However, this first reduction in variance is the best optimal point.

![](Images/Image173040.png)

Figure 24.11 Example – K-means clustering – “k” value section

### 24.5 K-Means Clustering for a Two-Dimensional Dataset

![](Images/Image173051.png)

Figure 24.12 Example – K-means clustering for a two-dimensional dataset

A similar algorithm will get followed for the 2-dimensional dataset. The only difference is that we need to use Euclidian distance instead of direct distance when calculating the distance from the data element with the mean point.
